On higher regulators of Picard modular surfaces
Linli Shi

Abstract
. We prove the motivic classes in the motivic cohomology groups of Picard modular surfaces with non-trivial coefficients constructed in a paper of Loeffler–Skinner–Zerbes are in the motivic cohomology groups of the interior motives. Then we establish a relation between the motivic classes and non-critical values of the motivic L L -functions associated to cuspidal automorphic representations of GU ⁡ ( 2 , 1 ) \GU(2,1) , thus deducing non-triviality of the motivic classes and providing evidence for Beilinson’s conjectures.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

The analytic class number formula is a deep relationship between an analytic invariant and an arithmetic invariant of a number field
F
. It relates the leading Taylor coefficient at
0
of the Dedekind zeta-function
\zeta_{F}(s)
of
F
to the Dirichlet regulator

r\colon O_{F}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}+r_{2}},

where
O_{F}^{\times}
is the unit group and
r_{1}
(resp.,
r_{2}
) is the number of real (resp., complex) places of the number field
F
. In the 1980s, Beilinson made deep conjectures about special values of motivic
L
-functions generalizing the classical analytic class formula
Bei84a
. In order to do so, Beilinson replaced the units
O_{K}^{\times}
by the motivic cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{i}_{M}(X,\mathbb{Q}(n))
where
X
is a smooth
\mathbb{Q}
-scheme and replaced the Dirichlet regulator by the higher regulator map

r_{H}\colon\mathrm{H}^{i}_{M}(X,\mathbb{Q}(n))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{H}^{i}(X,\mathbb{R}(n))

from motivic cohomology to absolute Hodge cohomology. For an introduction to Beilinson’s conjectures, the interested reader may consult
Nek 94
.

In this paper, we establish a connection between motivic classes in motivic cohomology with non-trivial coefficients of Picard modular surfaces and the motivic
L
-functions of some cuspidal automorphic representations of the unitary similitude group
\GU(2,1)
, through which we show that the motivic classes are non-trivial. It answers a question raised in
LSZ 22
, in which the authors constructed an Euler system for
\GU(2,1)
from the motivic classes. Along the way, we also prove a vanishing on the boundary result for the motivic classes. We hope this work could shed some light on proving more general relationships between special values of motivic
L
-functions associated to automorphic representations and mixed motives.

1.2.
Statement of the main results

Now we introduce the main results. These can be divided into two parts. In the first part, we construct motivic classes
\mathcal{E}is_{M}(\phi_{f})
in the motivic cohomology with non-trivial coefficients of Picard modular surfaces
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
and then we prove the classes are in a “nice” subspace of the cohomology. Along the way, we also prove the regulators of the classes
\mathcal{E}is_{H}(\phi_{f})
are in some “nice” subspace of the absolute Hodge cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
. In the second part, we prove a relation between the image of the motivic class and a non-critical value of the motivic
L
-function associated to some “nice” irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
. The relation resembles Beilinson’s conjectures on special values of
L
-functions.

Now, we state the results more precisely. Let
G=\GU(2,1)
and let
H
be a subgroup of
G
that can be embedded into
G
and is an extension of the group
\GL_{2}
. You can find the precise definition of
H
in Definition
3.2
. The relation between the groups
\GL_{2}
,
H
and
G
can be summarized in following diagram:

(resp.,
M
) is defined to be the Weil restriction to
\mathbb{Q}
of

(resp.,
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}
) (see Convention
3.25
for more details). But in the introduction, we ignore this technical detail.

V
on
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}
,
V_{2}

\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}

\mathrm{Sh}_{H}
(see Lemma
4.12
and Proposition

). Then we have the corresponding morphisms of motivic cohomologies:

Beilinson constructed the
Eisenstein symbol

(
Bei86a
, §3)
. For each nonnegative integer
n
, the Eisenstein Symbol is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear map

Eis^{n}_{M}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}},\mathrm{Sym}^{n}V_{2}(1)),

where the source
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is the space of locally constant
\mathbb{Q}
-valued functions on
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
satisfying some equivariant properties (see Section
7.6
).

If we compose the Eisenstein symbol with the pullback
p^{*}\colon\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(M,\mathrm{Sym}^{n}V_{2}(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(M,W(1))
and then with the Gysin map
\iota_{*}\colon\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(M,W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
, we get the map
\mathcal{E}is^{n}_{M}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
. Hence, for
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, we have a motivic class
\mathcal{E}is_{M}^{n}(\phi_{f})\in\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
. In other words, we have the following diagram:

1.2.1.
Geometric results

Our first geometric theorem states that the Hodge class
\mathcal{E}is^{n}_{H}(\phi_{f})
is in a “nice” subspace of
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))
, whose proof follows from the method used in
Lem 15
:

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem
5.15
)
.

For
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
as in Definition
3.7
, under certain conditions (see Theorem
5.15
),
the map
\mathcal{E}is_{H}^{n}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n,\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))
factors through the inclusion

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbf{1},\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}},

where
\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is the abelian category of mixed
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge structures,
{\mathbf{1}}
denotes the trivial Hodge structure that is the unit of
\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
and
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))
is the interior Betti cohomology with coefficients in
V
defined by

\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,c}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}).

Remark 1.2
.

The interior Betti cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}
is the cohomology group containing the “cohomological” cuspidal automorphic representations
\pi
of
G(\mathbb{A})
(see Proposition
3.27
and its proof). Hence, Theorem
1.1
tells us that the regulator of the motivic class
\mathcal{E}is^{n}_{H}(\phi_{f})
is in a “nice” subspace that is related to automorphic representations. It gives one hope that the class
\mathcal{E}is^{n}_{H}(\phi_{f})
can be related to the motivic
L
-function of some automorphic representation.

Our second geometric theorem is a “motivic lifting” of the first theorem that says that the motivic class
\mathcal{E}is^{n}_{M}(\phi_{f})
is in a “nice” subspace of
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
:

Theorem 1.3

(Theorem
6.20
)
.

Under the same conditions of the previous theorem,
the map
\mathcal{E}is_{M}^{n}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
factors through the inclusion

\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0))\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2)),

where
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0))
(see Definition
6.27
) is the motivic cohomology of the interior motive
\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(S,V(2))
(see Definition
6.17
).

Remark 1.4
.

•

The interior Betti cohomology is the realization of the interior motive
\mathrm{Gr_{0}M_{gm}}(S,V(2))
through the Betti realization functor.

•

Under the Beilinson regulator
r_{H}
, the image of
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0))
lies in

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbf{1},\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}).

Hence, Theorem
1.3
can be viewed as a “motivic lifting” of Theorem
1.1
.

•

Theorem
1.3
tells us that the motivic class
\mathcal{E}is^{n}_{M}(\phi_{f})
is in a “nice” subspace that is related to the Grothendick motive associated to some automorphic representation
(
Wil 15
, Theorem 5.6)
. It gives one hope to relate the motivic class
\mathcal{E}is^{n}_{M}(\phi_{f})
to the motivic
L
-function of some automorphic representation.

•

In
Kin 98
, the author raised the question of proving the motivic version of vanishing on the boundary for Eisenstein class on Shimura varieties. In the case of Hilbert modular surfaces, a result of motivic vanishing on the boundary has been proved in
(
Wil 12
, Corollary 3.14)
using a different formalism from us.

1.2.2.
Results on special values of
L
-functions

Let
\pi=\pi_{f}\otimes\pi_{\infty}
be some “cohomological” irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
G(\mathbb{A})
, where we denote by
E(\pi_{f})
its rational field (see Section
10
).
Let
M(\pi_{f},V(2))
be the Grothendieck motive associated to
\pi

(
Wil 15
, Theorem 5.6)
after a Tate twist and
M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))
be its Betti realization. Then the absolute Hodge cohomology of
M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))
is a rank-
1

E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
-module. In it, we define two rational structures
\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))
and
\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))
, where the rational structure
\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))
(maybe trivial) is defined using the motivic class
\mathcal{E}is_{M}(\phi_{f})
and
\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))
(non-trivial) is defined using the comparison between the Betti and de Rham realizations (see Section
7.1
). Our theorem says that the difference between the two rational structures can be measured using a non-critical value of the motivic
L
-function
L(M_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\pi_{f},V(2)),s)
(see Definition
10.10
) of
\pi
:

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem
10.16
)
.

Under the conditions in the statement of Theorem
10.16
,
the relation between
\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))
and
\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))
is as follows:

\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))=C\cdot L(M_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\pi_{f},V(2)),0)\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2)),

where
C\in(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})^{\times}
and
C
can be expressed explicitly in terms of a comparison of Whittaker periods (see Defintion
9.9
) and Deligne periods (see Definition
9.2
).

Remark 1.6
.

•

That the motivic
L
-function
L(M(\pi_{f},V(2)),s)
is being evaluated at
s=0
is exactly what Beilinson conjectured.
(
Nek 94
, Conjecture (6.1))

•

The
C
in Theorem
1.5
should be in
(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}})^{\times}
according to Beilinson’s conjectures.

•

Theorem
1.5
is a weight
w\leq-3
counterpart of the main theorem proved in
PS 18
, where the authors constructed a motivic class
\xi
in the motivic cohomology with trivial coefficients
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(S,\mathbb{Q}(2))_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
and proved a relation between
\xi
and a non-critical
L
-value of some cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
2
2

2

There is a gap in the proof of
PS 18
, which seems could be fixed by the tool of tempered currents developed recently in
BGCLRJ 24
.

•

A direct corollary of Theorem
1.5
is that the map
\mathcal{E}is_{M}
is non-trivial (see Corollary
10.18
). This answers a question raised in
LSZ 22
, where the authors needed the non-triviality of the motivic classes
\mathcal{E}is(\phi_{f})
for
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
as an input to construct an Euler system for
\GU(2,1)
.

•

Theorem
1.5
is deduced from an automorphic version of the theorem (see Theorem
10.4
) and a comparison between automorphic
L
-functions and motivic
L
-functions after many normalizations (see Proposition
10.14
).

1.3.
An overview of the proof

1.3.1.
An outline of the proof of the geometric results

We first give an outline of the proof of vanishing on the boundary of absolute Hodge cohomology (Theorem
1.1
, Theorem
5.15
).

For each of the two Shimura varieties
X=\{M,S\}
, we denote by
X^{*}
its Baily–Borel compactification (see Section
5.1
for more details) and let
\partial{X}=X^{*}-X
be the cusps of the compactification. Hence, we have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial{S},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

where
j
and
j^{\prime}
are open immersions,
i
and
i^{\prime}
are closed immersions and
\iota
,
p
and
q
are closed immersions
3
3

3

It holds after a careful choice of level structures.
.

By properties of the derived category of real algebraic mixed Hodge modules (see
(
HW 98
, Definition A.2.4)
), we have the following exact sequence (see Proposition
5.17
):

(1.1)

0\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbf{1},\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).

By the functoriality, we can get the following commutative diagram (see Proposition
5.18
):

(1.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{n,\mathbb{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(M,W(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{H}}

By exact sequence (
1.1
) and commutative diagram (
1.2
), in order to prove that the map
\mathcal{E}is_{H}^{n}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n,\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))
factors through the inclusion

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbf{1},\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2)),

it suffices to prove that the map
\theta^{H}
is zero, which is carried out in Proposition
5.26
. The key input of the proof of that fact is the explicit computation of degeneration of Hodge structures over
M
and
S
(see Lemma
5.7
, Lemma
5.10
), which is based on a theorem of Burgos-Wildeshaus (see Theorem
5.2
) and Kostant’s theorem (see Theorem
5.5
). Finally, we remark that the degeneration of Hodge structures over
S
has been computed in
Anc 17
.

We now outline the proof of vanishing on the boundary of motivic cohomology (Theorem
1.3
, Theorem
6.20
). The proof is parallel to the proof in the absolute Hodge cohomology case. However, we need to translate everything into the language of motives.

First, we have a motivic version of the exact sequence (
1.1
) (see Proposition
6.28
):

(1.3)

0\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).

Second, by functoriality of the triangulated category of mixed motives, we have the motivic version of the commutative diagram (
1.1
) (see Proposition
6.30
):

(1.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(M,W(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(\partial{M},i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{M}}

Finally, similar to the absolute Hodge cohomology case, in order to prove that the map
\mathcal{E}is_{M}^{n}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
factors through the inclusion

\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0))\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2)),

it suffices to prove that
\theta^{M}
is zero, whose proof is based on the fact that
\theta^{H}
is zero and weight conservativity of Hodge realization functor for Voevodsky motives of Abelian type
(
Wil 15
, Theorem 1.12.)
(see Proposition
6.32
).

1.3.2.
An outline of the proof of the
L
-value results

In order to prove the relation

\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))=C^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2)),

in the rank
1

E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
-module
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})
for some
C^{\prime}\in(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R})^{\times}
, it suffices to find an
E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
-linear form
\psi\colon M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1)\rightarrow E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
which is trivial on
F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\psi(\tilde{v}_{K})=C^{\prime}\cdot\psi(\tilde{v}_{D}),

where
v_{K}
(resp.,
v_{D}
) is a
E(\pi_{f})
-generator of
\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))
(resp.,
\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))
) and
\tilde{v}_{K}
(resp.,
\tilde{v}_{D}
) is a lifting of
v_{K}
(resp.,
v_{D}
) through the exact sequence (
7.1
) (see Lemma
7.8
).

By the Poincaré duality pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}\colon\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))\otimes\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,D)\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(0),

where
D
is the contragredient representation of
V
and the construction of a differential form
\Omega\in M(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)_{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
, which is carried out in Section
7.2
, we construct a linear form
\Psi
and we get

\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))=\frac{\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}}{\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{D}\rangle_{B}}\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2)).

Hence, we are left to compute
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}
and
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{D}\rangle_{B}
.

The way to compute the pairing
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}
is as follows. We first map the class
v_{K}
to Deligne–Beilinson cohomology through the natural map
r_{H\rightarrow D}
from absolute Hodge cohomology to Deligne–Beilinson cohomology (see definition of Deligne–Beilinson cohomology in Proposition
7.30
), in which the class will be represented by a pair of tempered currents (see Remark
7.51
)

(\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})},\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})})

and this can be used to compute the pairing
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}
(see Lemma
7.52
for details). Finally, we express the pairing in terms of an integral of differential forms over Shimura varieties
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}
(see Proposition
7.55
), which is expressed explicitly in Propositin
7.59
.

Remark 1.7
.

•

The tool of tempered currents defined in
BGCLRJ 24
is a key ingredient of this step. Hence, we recall the key definitions in Section
7.4
.

•

In order to apply the tool of Deligne–Beilinson cohomology, we need to apply “Liebermann’s trick” (see Definition
7.34
) because we are working with non-trivial coefficients.

By a careful choice of test vectors (see Proposition
8.6
), we can express the above explicit integral as a zeta integral
I(\varphi,\Phi,\nu,s)
(see Definition
8.5
) constructed by Gelbart and Piatetski-Shapiro, which gives an integral repersentation of the twisted standard automorphic
L
-function of the contragredient
\tilde{\pi}
of
\pi
. We then prove the algebraicity of the nonarchimedean local Zeta integral at rational points in Propositon
8.21
and compute the archimedean Zeta integral explicitly in Proposition
8.34
. Combining all of these with an identity (see Lemma
10.3
) between automorphic
L
-functions of an automorphic representation and it congragredient, we can express the pairing
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}
in terms of an automorphic
L
-value of
L(s,\pi,\mathrm{std})
(see Definition
8.13
) at non-critical points and Whittaker periods (see Definition
9.9
).

By the definition of Deligne periods and Deligne
E(\pi_{f})
-structure (see Definition
7.4
), we express the pairing
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{D}\rangle_{B}

in terms of Deligne periods (see Proposition
9.7
).

Combining the above computation of
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}
and
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{D}\rangle_{B}
, we prove a relation

\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))=C^{\prime}\cdot\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2)),

where
C^{\prime}
is expressed in terms of a non-critical value of
L(s,\pi,\mathrm{std})
at
s=0
, Whittaker periods and Deligne periods (see Theorem
10.4
). Finally, we relate the automorphic
L
-function

L(s,\pi,\mathrm{std})

to the motivic
L
-function
L(M_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\pi_{f},V(2)),s)
(see Proposition
10.14
). The fact that
C^{\prime}\in(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})^{\times}
follows from
L(M_{\text{\'{e}t}}(\pi_{f},V(2)),0)\neq 0
(see the proof of Corollary
10.18
).

1.4.
Structure of the paper

In Section
3.1
-Section
3.2
, we define certain algebraic groups, Lie groups and their representations. In Section
3.3
-Section
3.4
, we define the corresponding Shimura varieties and recall the needed properties of cohomology of Picard modular surfaces. In Section
3.5
-Section
3.6
, we compute the
L
-packet of discrete series at the archimedean place and compute the Hodge decomposition of corresponding motives associated to automorphic representations. In Section
4.1
-Section
4.3
, we recall the definition of motivic cohomology, absolute Hodge cohomology and Beilinson regulators. In Section
4.4
, we recall the construction of the motivic classes in
LSZ 22
and their image under the Beilinson regulator, which we call the Hodge classes. In Section
5.1
-Section
5.4
, we prove that the Hodge classes belong to a “nice” subspace of absolute Hodge cohomology. In Section
6.1
-Section
6.4
, we prove that the motivic classes belong to a “nice” subspace of motivic cohomology. In Section
7.1
-Section
7.3
, we explain how to associate a differential form to the contragredient automorphic representation and use it and Poincaré duality to construct a linear form. In Section
7.4
, we recall the definition of Deligne–Beilinson cohomology using tempered currents. In Section
7.5
-Section
7.7
, we give an explicit expression of the classes in Deligne–Beilinson cohomology and an explicit expression of its pairing with the above differential form. In Section
8.1
-Section
8.2
, we express the above integral in terms of a zeta integral. In Section
8.3
-Section
8.5
, we unfold the zeta integral and recall an algebraicity result of nonarchimedean zeta integrals and compute the archimedean zeta integral explicitly. In Section
9.1
, we compute the pairing of the Deligne rational structure with the diffferential form. In Section
9.2
, we recall the definition of the Whittaker period. In Section
10.1
-Section
10.2
, we prove the main results of the paper.

2.
Notations and Conventions

General notations

•

Let
\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}
be the set of rational numbers, real numbers and complex numbers.

•

Let
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}_{+}
be the set of non-zero positive rational numbers.

•

We use
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
to denote the set of algebraic numbers and use
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
to denote the set of non-zero algebraic numbers.

•

=_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}}
: for
A,B\in\mathbb{C}
,
A=_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}}B
means that there exists a
c\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
such that
A=c\cdot B
.

•

For a number field
F
, we use
\mathbb{A}_{F}
to denote the ring of adeles and use
\mathbb{A}_{F,f}
to denote the ring of finite adeles. We use
\mathbb{A}
to denote the ring of adeles of
\mathbb{Q}
and use
\mathbb{A}_{f}
to denote the ring of finite adeles of
\mathbb{Q}
.

•

We use the notation
\hat{\mathbb{Z}}
to denote the ring
\varprojlim\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
and use
\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
to denote the abelian group
\varprojlim(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
, where
N
runs over positive integers.

•

We use
i
to denote the complex number
\sqrt{-1}
.

Algebraic geometry

•

\mathcal{O}_{X}
: When
X
is a scheme (resp., a complex manifold), we denote by
\mathcal{O}_{X}
the structure sheaf (resp., structure sheaf of holomorphic functions) of
X
.

•

Let
\mathbb{S}=\mathrm{Res}_{\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R}}\Gm
be the Deligne torus. Following Deligne and Pink, we use the following convention for the equivalence of categories between algebraic representations of
\mathbb{S}
in finite-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces and (semisimple)
\mathbb{R}
-mixed Hodge structures. Let
(\rho,V)
be such a representation of
\mathbb{S}
. The summand
V^{p,q}\subset V
of Hodge type
(p,q)
is the summand of
V
such that
\rho(z_{1},z_{2})
acts by
z_{1}^{-p}z_{2}^{-q}
for
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{C})
. In particular, any algebraic representation
V
of
\mathbb{S}
with central character
c
corresponds to a pure Hodge structure of weight
-c
.

•

For a
\mathbb{C}
-scheme
X
, we use the notation
X^{an}
to denote the complex analytic variety associated to
X
through GAGA.

•

For a field extension
E
of
F
and a scheme over the field
E
, we use the notation
\mathrm{Res}_{E/F}(X)
to denote the Weil restriction of
X
to
F
. To be more precise, for any
F
-scheme
S
, the
S
-points of the scheme
\mathrm{Res}_{E/F}(X)
are the
S\times_{\Spec(F)}\Spec(E)
-points of
X
.

•

When we say Betti cohomology of an algebraic variety over
\mathbb{Q}
, we always mean Betti cohomology of the
\mathbb{C}
-points of the algebraic variety. In order to simplify notation, we avoid any notation for taking
\mathbb{C}
-points.

Group Theory

•

For a positive integer
n
, we use
S_{n}
to denote the permuation group of
n
elements.

•

For an affine group scheme
G
over a ring
k
, sometimes we write
g\in G
to replace

g\in G(R),

for a
k
-algebra
R
.

•

For a reductive group
G
over
\mathbb{Q}
, we denote by
A_{G}
the connected component of the identity of the real points of the maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-split center of
G
.

•

For a reductive group
G
over
\mathbb{Q}
, let
K
be a maximal compact group of
G(\mathbb{R})
and denote
K_{G}=A_{G}K
.

•

For a Lie group
G
, we denote by
G^{+}
the connected component of the identity in
G
.

Representation theory

•

For a representation
(\pi,V)
of a group
G
, sometimes we use
\pi
to denote the space
V
when there is no confusion.

•

A representation of a
p
-adic group is always a smooth admissible
\mathbb{C}
-representation.

•

For a representation
V
of a group (algebraic group, real Lie group,
p
-adic group or adelic group), we use
V^{\vee}
to denote the contragredient representation of
V
.

•

We use the notation
\Ind
to the denote unnormalized induction functor.

•

For a representation
\pi
of a group
G
over a field
E
, which is an extension of a field
F
, we use the notation
\mathrm{Res}_{E/F}\pi
to denote the restriction of the representation to the field
F
.

Automorphic Forms

•

For a reductive group
G
over
\mathbb{Q}
and a quasi-character
\xi
(not necessarily unitary) of
A_{G}
, let
\mathrm{L}^{2}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
be the space of functions
\phi\colon G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

\phi(zx)=\xi(z)\phi(x),\,\,\,z\in A_{G},x\in G(\mathbb{A}),

and which are square integrable modulo
A_{G}
.

•

Let
\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
, respectively
\mathrm{C}_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
be the space of functions
\phi\colon G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that

–

\phi(zx)=\xi(z)\phi(x),\,\,\,z\in A_{G},x\in G(\mathbb{A})
,

–

the restriction of
\phi
to
G(\mathbb{R})
is smooth, respectively, smooth and compactly supported modulo
A_{G}
,

–

the restriction of
\phi
to
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
is locally constant and compactly supported.

•

Let
\mathrm{C}_{(2)}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
be the space

\mathrm{L}^{2}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)\cap\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi).

We have inclusions of
((\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G})\times G(\mathbb{A}_{f}))
-modules

\mathrm{C}_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)\subset\mathrm{C}_{(2)}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)\subset\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi).

•

Let
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)=\mathrm{L}^{2}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
be the space of cusp forms in

\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi).

Smooth truncation to a large compact set modulo
A_{G}
induces a map of
((\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G})\times G(\mathbb{A}_{f}))
-modules

\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)\rightarrow\mathrm{C}_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi).

•

An irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi=\pi_{f}\otimes\pi_{\infty}
is an irreducible
((\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G})\times G(\mathbb{A}_{f}))
-submodule of
\mathrm{C}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
.

3.
Preliminaries

3.1.
Algebraic groups, algebraic representations and branching laws

In this subsection, we define the reductive group
\GU(2,1)
and a subgroup
H
of
G
, together with their representations.

3.1.1.
Algebraic groups

Notation 3.1
.

Let
E
be an imaginary quadratic field of discriminant
-D
, and let
x\mapsto\bar{x}
be the non-trivial Galois automorphism of
E
over
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\mathcal{O}
be the ring of integers of
E
. We fix an identification of
E\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
with
\mathbb{C}
such that the imaginary part of
\delta=\sqrt{-D}
is positive.

Definition 3.2
.

(1)

Let
J\in\GL_{3}(E)
be the Hermitian matrix

J=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\delta^{-1}\\
0&1&0\\
-\delta^{-1}&0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathrm{where}\ \delta=\sqrt{-D},

and let
G=\GU(J)
be the group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}
such that for all
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras
R
, we have

G(R)=\{(g,\mu)\in\GL_{3}(\mathcal{O}\otimes R)\times R^{\times}|\prescript{t}{}{\bar{g}}Jg=\mu J\}.

This is a unitary similitude group and we denote by
\mu\colon G\rightarrow\Gm
the similitude character. The center
Z_{G}
of the group
G
can be identified with
\Res_{\mathcal{O}/\mathbb{Z}}(\Gm)
via

(3.1)

z\in\Res_{\mathcal{O}/\mathbb{Z}}(\Gm)(R)\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}z&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&z\end{pmatrix}\in Z_{G}(R).

The group
G
is quasi-split over
\mathbb{Q}
and is split when base change from
\mathbb{Q}
to
E
:

G_{E}\cong\GL_{3,E}\times\Gm_{,E}.

We define
G_{0}=\ker(\mu)
as the unitary group. Hence, we have

G(R)=Z_{G}(R)G_{0}(R),

for all
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras
R
.
Note that
G
is reductive over
\mathbb{Z}_{l}
for
l\nmid D
(even for
l=2
).

(2)

Let
H
be the group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}
such that for a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras
R
, we have

H(R)=\{(g,z)\in\GL_{2}(R)\times(\mathcal{O}\otimes R)^{\times}|\det(g)=z\bar{z}\}.

The center
Z_{H}
of the group
H
is

(3.2)

Z_{H}(R)=\{(\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix},z)\in\GL_{2}(R)\times(\mathcal{O}\otimes R)^{\times}|t^{2}=z\bar{z}\}.

The group
H
is quasi-split and is split when base change from
\mathbb{Q}
to
E
:

H_{E}\cong\GL_{2,E}\times\Gm_{,E}.

(3)

We have the following two maps:

\iota\colon H\hookrightarrow G,\quad(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix},z)\mapsto(\begin{pmatrix}a&0&b\\
0&z&0\\
c&0&d\end{pmatrix},z\bar{z})

and

p\colon H\twoheadrightarrow\GL_{2},\quad(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix},z)\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}.

The relation between the groups
\GL_{2}
,
H
and
G
can be summarized in the diagram:

\hbox to87.67pt{\vbox to47.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 43.83397pt\lower-23.90279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(4)

Via the map
\iota
, we define the group scheme
Z
as

(3.3)

Z(R):=Z_{G}(R)\cap H(R)=\{(\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix},t)\in\GL_{2}(R)\times R^{\times}\},

which is the common center of
G
and
H
.

The definition of
H
is natural for the following reasons.

Let
J_{2}\in\GL_{2}(E)
be the Hermitian matrix

J_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\delta^{-1}\\
-\delta^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathrm{where}\ \delta=\sqrt{-D},

and let
\GU(J_{2})
be the group scheme over
\mathbb{Z}
such that for all
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras
R
, we have

G(R)=\{(g,\mu)\in\GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}\otimes R)\times R^{\times}|\prescript{t}{}{\bar{g}}J_{2}g=\mu J_{2}\}.

Let
\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}
be the subgroup scheme of
\GU(J_{2})
defined by

\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}=\{g\in\GU(J_{2})|\det(g)=\mu(g)\}.

Fact 3.3
.

We have

\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}=\GL_{2},

through which the similitude character
\mu
is identified with the determinant character
\det
. Hence, we use the notation
\mu
to denote the determinant character for
H
and
\GL_{2}
.

Proof.

For
g\in\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}
, we have
{}^{t}{\bar{g}}J_{2}g=J_{2}
, and if we left multiply both sides by
\left(\begin{matrix}\delta&0\\
0&\delta\end{matrix}\right)
,
we have

{}^{t}{\bar{g}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}g=\mu\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

Hence, for
g=\left(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{matrix}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-\bar{c}&\bar{a}\\
-\bar{d}&\bar{b}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{a}&\bar{c}\\
\bar{b}&\bar{d}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mu\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}d&-b\\
-c&a\end{pmatrix}\frac{1}{ad-bc}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-c&a\\
-d&b\end{pmatrix}\frac{\mu}{ad-bc}=\begin{pmatrix}-c&a\\
-d&b\end{pmatrix}.

So we have
a=\bar{a},b=\bar{b},c=\bar{c}
and
d=\bar{d}
, which means
g\in\GL_{2}
.

For
g\in\GL_{2}
, the above process can be reversed.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

The natural subgroup
\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}\boxtimes\Res_{\mathcal{O}/\mathbb{Z}}(\Gm)
of
G
, which is defined by

\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}\boxtimes\Res_{\mathcal{O}/\mathbb{Z}}(\Gm)(R)=\{(g,z)\in\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}(R)\times(\mathcal{O}\otimes R)^{\times}|\mu(g)=z\bar{z}\}

for all
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras
R
, can be identified with
H
.

Convention 3.5
.

When we consider
H
as an algebraic group over
\mathbb{Q}
, we are free to use the notation

H=\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}\boxtimes E^{\times}=\GL_{2}\boxtimes E^{\times}.

3.1.2.
Algebraic representations

Convention 3.6
.

In this subsubsection, the algebraic groups
G
,
H
and
\GL_{2}
are all defined over
\mathbb{Q}
.

We first define algebraic representations of
G
.

Definition 3.7
.

(1)

Let
\chi_{i},i=1,\dots,4
, be the four characters of the diagonal torus
T/E
of
G
such that

\displaystyle\chi_{1}\colon\begin{pmatrix}x&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&\frac{z\bar{z}}{\bar{x}}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto x,\ \chi_{2}\colon\begin{pmatrix}x&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&\frac{z\bar{z}}{\bar{x}}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\bar{x},

\displaystyle\chi_{3}\colon\begin{pmatrix}x&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&\frac{z\bar{z}}{\bar{x}}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\frac{\det}{\mu},\ \chi_{4}\colon\begin{pmatrix}x&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&\frac{z\bar{z}}{\bar{x}}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\frac{\overline{\det}}{\mu}.

Here,
x,\bar{x},z,\bar{z}
are
4
independent variables.

(2)

In this paper, we also use the notation
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3};d)
to denote the character of the diagonal torus
T
such that

t=\begin{pmatrix}a&0&0\\
0&b&0\\
0&0&c\end{pmatrix}\mapsto a^{\mu_{1}}b^{\mu_{2}}c^{\mu_{3}}\mu(t)^{d}.

We denote by
\lambda(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3};d)
the irreducible algebraic representation of
G
with highest weight
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3};d)
. When the action of
\mu
is clear, sometimes we write
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3})
for
\lambda(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3};d)
.

(3)

For
a_{1},a_{2}\geq 0
, let
V^{a_{1},a_{2}}
denote the representation of
G
of highest weight
a_{1}\chi_{1}+a_{2}\chi_{2}
.

(4)

For each representation
V
of
G
, we write
V\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
for its twists by
\chi_{3}^{a_{3}}\chi_{4}^{a_{4}}
.

Each irreducible representation of
G
is
V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
for some
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a_{1}\geq 0,a_{2}\geq 0
.

Proposition 3.8
.

(1)

Each irreducible representation of
G
is
V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
for some
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a_{1}\geq 0,a_{2}\geq 0
.

(2)

The highest weight of
V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
is
(a_{1}+a_{3}-a_{4},a_{3}-a_{4},a_{3}-(a_{2}+a_{4});2a_{4}+a_{2}-a_{3})
.

(3)

The contragredient representation
D^{a_{1},a_{2}}
of
V^{a_{1},a_{2}}
is
V^{a_{2},a_{1}}\{-(a_{1}+a_{2}),-(a_{1}+a_{2})\}
.

Proof.

(1)

The special unitary group
SU(J)
is the subgroup of
G
such that
\det=\mu=1
, which is a semisimple algebraic group. It is enough to consider the algebraic representation of
\SU(J)\cong\SL_{3,E}
. For
\SL_{3,E}
, the fundamental weights are
\omega_{1}=(1,0)
and
\omega_{2}=(1,1)
.
For any
t=\begin{pmatrix}x&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{xz}\end{pmatrix}\in T_{E}
, we have
\chi_{1}(t)=x=\omega_{1}(t)
and
\chi_{2}(t)=\bar{x}=xz=\omega_{2}(t)
.
Hence, by highest weight theory, every irreducible algebraic representation of
\SU(J)_{E}
is determined by

a_{1}\omega_{1}+a_{2}\omega_{2}=a_{1}\chi_{1}+a_{2}\chi_{2},

for
a_{1}\geq 0
and
a_{2}\geq 0
. Hence, every irreducible algebraic representation of
G
is of the form
V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
for some
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a_{1}\geq 0,a_{2}\geq 0
.

(2)

The highest weight of
V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
is

\displaystyle t=\left(\begin{pmatrix}x&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&\frac{z\bar{z}}{\bar{x}}\end{pmatrix},z\bar{z}\right)\in T_{E}\mapsto\,

\displaystyle x^{a_{1}}(\bar{x})^{a_{2}}(\frac{xz}{\bar{x}})^{b_{1}}(\frac{\bar{x}\bar{z}}{x})^{b_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{a_{1}+b_{1}-b_{2}}\bar{x}^{a_{2}+b_{2}-b_{1}}z^{b_{1}}\bar{z}^{b_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{a_{1}+b_{1}-b_{2}}z^{b_{1}-b_{2}}(\frac{z\bar{z}}{\bar{x}})^{b_{1}-(a_{2}+b_{2})}(z\bar{z})^{2b_{2}+a_{2}-b_{1}}.

(3)

First, we have

\displaystyle a_{1}\omega_{1}+a_{2}\omega_{2}

\displaystyle=a_{1}(\frac{2}{3},-\frac{1}{3},-\frac{1}{3})+a_{2}(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3},-\frac{2}{3})

\displaystyle=(\frac{2a_{1}+a_{2}}{3},\frac{-a_{1}+a_{2}}{3},\frac{-a_{1}-2a_{2}}{3}).

Hence, when restrict to
\SU(J)
, the highest weight of
D^{a_{1},a_{2}}=(V^{a_{1},a_{2}})^{\vee}
is

\displaystyle(\frac{a_{1}+2a_{2}}{3},\frac{a_{1}-a_{2}}{3},\frac{-2a_{1}-a_{2}}{3})=a_{2}\omega_{1}+a_{1}\omega_{2}.

Second, for the
t
in (2), we have

\displaystyle\chi_{1}(t)=x

\displaystyle=x^{\frac{2}{3}}z^{-\frac{1}{3}}(\frac{z\bar{z}}{\bar{x}})^{-\frac{1}{3}}\cdot(x\cdot z\cdot(\frac{z\bar{z}}{\bar{x}}))^{\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle=\omega_{1}(t)\chi_{3}(t)^{\frac{2}{3}}\chi_{4}(t)^{\frac{1}{3}}.

Similarly, we have

\chi_{2}(t)=\omega_{2}(t)\chi_{3}(t)^{\frac{1}{3}}\chi_{4}(t)^{\frac{2}{3}}.

So the highest weight of
D^{a_{1},a_{2}}
is
\omega_{1}^{a_{2}}\omega_{2}^{a_{1}}\chi_{3}^{\frac{-2a_{1}-a_{2}}{3}}\chi_{4}^{\frac{-a_{1}-2a_{2}}{3}}
which is

\chi_{1}^{a_{2}}\chi_{2}^{a_{1}}\chi_{3}^{-(a_{1}+a_{2})}\chi_{4}^{-(a_{1}+a_{2})}.

∎

Remark 3.9
.

For later use, we record the highest weight and central character in the following table.

(3.4)

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\text{Representation}&\text{Highest weight}&\text{central character}\\
\hline\cr V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}&(a+r-s,r-s,r-s-b;2s-r+b)&a+b+r+s\\
\hline\cr D=V^{\vee}&(b+s-r,s-r,s-r-a;-2s+r-b)&-a-b-r-s\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Now we define irreducible representations of
H
.

Definition 3.10
.

(1)

For each representation
W
of
H
, we write
W\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
for its twists by
\chi_{3}^{a_{3}}\chi_{4}^{a_{4}}
through
\iota\colon H\hookrightarrow G
.

(2)

In this paper, we also use the notation
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};d)
to denote the character of the diagonal torus

t=(\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&b\\
\end{pmatrix};z)\in H\mapsto a^{\mu_{1}}b^{\mu_{2}}z^{d}.

We denote by
\lambda^{\prime}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};d)
the irreducible algebraic representation of
H
with highest weight
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};d)
.

Convention 3.11
.

When the action of
\mathrm{Res}_{E/\mathbb{Q}}\Gm
in
H
is clear, we sometimes write
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
instead of
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};d)
.

Remark 3.12
.

Along the map
\iota
, the restriction of
\chi_{3}
and
\chi_{4}
is

\displaystyle\chi_{3}\colon(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix},z)\in H\mapsto z

\displaystyle\chi_{4}\colon(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix},z)\in H\mapsto\bar{z}.

So the representation
W\{a_{3},a_{4}\}
is the representation
W
with a twist by the character
z\mapsto z^{a_{3}}\bar{z}^{a_{4}}
.

Hence, we can get the following proposition:

Proposition 3.13
.

(1)

For
b_{1}\geq 0
, let
W^{b_{1}}
be the representation
\mathrm{Sym}^{b_{1}}V_{2}
of
H
, where
V_{2}
denotes the pullback of the standard representation of
\GL_{2}
through the map
p\colon:H\twoheadrightarrow\GL_{2}
.

Then every irreducible representation of
H
has the form
W^{b_{1}}\{b_{2},b_{3}\}
for some
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
b_{1}\geq 0
.

(2)

The contragredient of
W^{b_{1}}
is
W^{b_{1}}\{-b_{1},-b_{1}\}
.

3.1.3.
Branching law

The following is a corollary of the classical branching law for
\GL_{2}\subset\GL_{3}

(
GW 09
, Lemma 8.3.1)
.

Proposition 3.14
.

For an irreducible representation
V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
of
G
satisfying:

\displaystyle 0\leq-b_{1}\leq a_{1},

\displaystyle 0\leq-b_{2}\leq a_{2},

we have an embedding in the category of representations of
H
:

W^{n}\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{b_{1},b_{2}\},

where
n=a_{1}+a_{2}+b_{1}+b_{2}
and
\iota^{*}
denotes the restriction of the representation of
G
to
H
through
\iota
.

Proof.

The highest weight of
W^{n}
is
(a_{1}+a_{2}+b_{1}+b_{2},0)
and the highest weight of
V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
is

(a_{1}+b_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-(a_{2}+b_{2});2b_{2}+a_{2}-b_{1})
.

First, the common center of
G
and
H
is isomorphic to
\Gm
via

z\in\Gm\mapsto(\begin{pmatrix}z&0\\
0&z\end{pmatrix},z)\in H.

Its action on
W^{n}
is via

z\mapsto z^{n}

and its action on
\iota^{*}V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
is

z\mapsto z^{a_{1}+a_{2}+b_{1}+b_{2}}.

Hence, their central actions are compatible.

Second, the highest weight of
W^{n}\{-(2b_{2}+a_{2}-b_{1}),-(2b_{2}+a_{2}-b_{1})\}
is
(a_{1}+2b_{1}-b_{2},b_{1}-2b_{2}-a_{2})
. Up to conjugation, by the classical branching
\GL_{2}\subset\GL_{3}

(
GW 09
, Lemma 8.3.1)
, in order to have the branching

W^{n}\{-(2b_{2}+a_{2}-b_{1}),-(2b_{2}+a_{2}-b_{1})\}\hookrightarrow(\iota^{*}V^{a_{1},a_{2}}\{b_{1},b_{2}\})\{-(2b_{2}+a_{2}-b_{1}),-(2b_{2}+a_{2}-b_{1})\}

we must have

a_{1}+b_{1}-b_{2}\geq a_{1}+2b_{1}-b_{2}\geq b_{1}-b_{2}\geq b_{1}-2b_{2}-a_{2}\geq b_{1}-(a_{2}+b_{2}),

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle 0\leq-b_{1}\leq a_{1},

\displaystyle 0\leq-b_{2}\leq a_{2}.

∎

Remark 3.15
.

In this paper, we only consider the case
W^{n}\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
when
n=a+b+r+s
and will write
W
instead of
W^{n}
and
V
instead of
V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
to simplify notations. We will also denote by
D
the contragredient of
V
.

Notation 3.16
.

•

Throughout the paper, we always let
n=a+b+r+s
.

•

Throughout the paper, we always use
V
to denote the algebraic representation
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
of
G
,
D
to denote the contragredient of
V
and
W=W^{n}
to denote the algebraic representation of
H
except in the situation where we will explain the meaning.

•

For a representation
V
of
G
,
H
or
\GL_{2}
and an integer
d
, we use the notation
V(d)
denote the representation
V
with a twist of the character
\mu^{d}
.

3.2.
Lie groups and representations

3.2.1.
Lie groups and Lie algebras

Notation 3.17
.

To describe the maximal compact subgroup of
G(\mathbb{R})
easily, when considering representations of Lie groups, we use the Hermitian form

J^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}

to define the group
G=\GU(J^{\prime})
and use the Hermitian form

J_{2}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}

to define the group
\GU(J_{2}^{\prime})^{\prime}
, and then define the group
H=\GU(J_{2}^{\prime})^{\prime}\boxtimes(\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\Gm))
.
The definition of
G
(resp.,
H
) is the same as in Definition
3.2
except that we use
J^{\prime}
(resp.,
J^{\prime}_{2}
) instead of
J
(resp.,
J_{2}
).

Convention 3.19
.

In this paper, we write
G(\mathbb{R})
(resp.,
H(\mathbb{R})
and
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
) for
\GU(J^{\prime})(\mathbb{R})
(resp.,

(\GU(J_{2}^{\prime})^{\prime}\boxtimes E^{\times})(\mathbb{R})
and
\GU(J_{2}^{\prime})^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
).

Let
K_{G}
be the real Lie group
A_{G}(\U(2)\times\U(1))(\mathbb{R})
,
K_{H}
be the real Lie group
A_{H}(\U(1)\times\U(1))(\mathbb{R})
and
K_{\GL_{2}}
be the real Lie group
A_{\GL_{2}}\U(1)(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\lieg
be the Lie algebra of
G(\mathbb{R})
,
\lieh
be the Lie algebra of
H(\mathbb{R})
and
\liegl_{2}
be the Lie algebra of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Then by the
Iwasawa decomposition, we have the following decomposition of complexified Lie algebra:

\displaystyle\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{G,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep_{\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle\lieh_{\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{H,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle\liegl_{2,\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{H,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}},

where
\liek_{\mathbb{C}}
,
\liek_{H,\mathbb{C}}
and
\liek_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}
are the complexified Lie algebras of
K_{G}
,
K_{H}
and
K_{\GL_{2}}
, which is

\displaystyle\liek_{\mathbb{C}}=\{\begin{pmatrix}*&*&0\\
*&*&0\\
0&0&*\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle\liek_{H,\mathbb{C}}=\{\begin{pmatrix}*&0\\
0&*\end{pmatrix}\}\oplus\liegl_{1,\mathbb{C}}\subset\lieh_{\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle\liek_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}=\{\begin{pmatrix}*&0\\
0&*\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\liegl_{2,\mathbb{C}};

and
\liep_{\mathbb{C}}
,
\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}}
and
\liep_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}
are the sub Lie algebras of
\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}
,
\lieh_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\liegl_{2,\mathbb{C}}
:

\displaystyle\liep_{\mathbb{C}}=\{\begin{pmatrix}0&0&*\\
0&0&*\\
*&*&0\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}}=\{\begin{pmatrix}0&*\\
*&0\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\lieh_{\mathbb{C}},

\displaystyle\liep_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}=\{\begin{pmatrix}0&*\\
*&0\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\liegl_{2,\mathbb{C}}.

We also have a natural embedding
\iota_{*}\colon\lieh_{\mathbb{C}}\hookrightarrow\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}
induced by the map
\iota\colon H\hookrightarrow G
that maps
\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}}
to
\liep_{\mathbb{C}}
. Explicitly, the map is given by

\begin{pmatrix}0&A\\
B&0\end{pmatrix}\in\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}0&0&A\\
0&0&0\\
B&0&0\end{pmatrix}\in\liep_{\mathbb{C}}.

3.2.2.
Representation Theory of compact Lie groups

We recall some basic facts of representation theory of the compact Lie group
(\U(2)\times\U(1))(\mathbb{R})
.

The compact torus
T_{c}
is
(\U(1)\times\U(1)\times\U(1))(\mathbb{R})
. Hence, the dominant weights of
T_{c}
are parametrized by the triples
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
such that
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}
. By highest weight theory of representations of compact Lie groups, for each dominant weight
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
, there is an unique irreducible representation
\tau_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}
of
(\U(2)\times\U(1))(\mathbb{R})
whose highest weight is
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
.

By the Weyl dimension formula, we have

\dim\tau_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+1.

We denote
d=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+1
.
Actually, there exists a basis
(\nu_{s})_{0\leq s\leq d}
of
\tau_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}
such that

(3.5)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)\nu_{s}=(s+\lambda_{2})\nu_{s},

(3.6)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\nu_{s}=(-s+\lambda_{1})\nu_{s},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)\nu_{s}=(s+1)\nu_{s+1},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{matrix}\right)\nu_{s}=(d-s+1)\nu_{s-1}.

Convention 3.20
.

•

We will use
K_{G}
(resp.,
K_{H}
and
K_{\GL_{2}}
) to denote
(\U(2)\times\U(1))(\mathbb{R})
(resp.,
(\U(1)\times\U(1))(\mathbb{R})
and
\U(1)(\mathbb{R})
) when the action
A_{G}
is clear.

•

We will use the coordinate system
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
in subsection
3.5
and
3.6
to denote the Blattner parameter and Harish-Chandra parameter of a discrete series representation of a real reductive Lie group.

3.3.
Shimura varieties

In this subsection, we define the Shimura varieties associated to
G
,
H
and
\GL_{2}
.

Notation 3.21
.

Throughout the paper, we let
\mathbb{S}=\Res_{\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R}}\Gm
be the Deligne torus.

Definition 3.22
.

(1)

Let
X_{G}
be the
G(\mathbb{R})
-conjugacy class of the morphism
h\colon\mathbb{S}\rightarrow G_{\mathbb{R}}
given by

z=x+iy\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}x&0&y\\
0&z&0\\
-y&0&x\end{pmatrix}.

The pair
(G,X_{G})
is a (pure) Shimura datum associated to
G
.

The Hodge cocharacter
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
of
\Gm_{,\mathbb{C}}
associated to
h
is given on
\mathbb{C}
-points by

z\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}z&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Since it is not complex conjugation invariant, we can see that the reflex field of
(G,X_{G})
is
E
with a the fixed complex embedding of
E
into
\mathbb{C}

(
LR 92
, Lemma 4.2., P15)
.
For any neat compact open subgroup
L
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, there is a smooth
quasi-projective

E
-scheme
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)
such that as complex analytic varieties, we have

\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}=G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(X_{G}\times G(\mathbb{A}_{f})/L),

where
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}
is the analytification of the base change of
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)
to
\mathbb{C}
.

(2)

Let
X_{H}
be the
H(\mathbb{R})
-conjugacy class of the morphism
h\colon\mathbb{S}\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{R}}
given by

z=x+iy\mapsto(\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
-y&x\end{pmatrix},z).

The pair
(H,X_{H})
is a (pure) Shimura datum associated to
H
.

The Hodge cocharacter
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
of
\Gm_{,\mathbb{C}}
associated to
h
is given on
\mathbb{C}
-points by

z\mapsto(\begin{pmatrix}z&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},z).

The reflex field of
(H,X_{H})
is
E
with a fixed complex embedding of
E
into
\mathbb{C}
.
For any neat compact open subgroup
K
of
H(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, there is a smooth
quasi-projective

E
-scheme
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)
such that as complex analytic varieties, we have

\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}=H(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(X_{H}\times H(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K),

where
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}
is the analytification of the base change of
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)
to
\mathbb{C}
.

(3)

Let
X_{\GL_{2}}
be the
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
-conjugacy class of the morphism
h\colon\mathbb{S}\rightarrow\GL_{2,\mathbb{R}}
that is given by

z\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
-y&x\end{pmatrix},

The pair
(\GL_{2},X_{\GL_{2}})
is a (pure) Shimura datum associated to
\GL_{2}
.

The Hodge cocharacter
\mu_{\mathbb{C}}
of
\Gm_{,\mathbb{C}}
associated to
h
is given on
\mathbb{C}
-points by

z\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}z&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

The reflex field of
(\GL_{2},X_{\GL_{2}})
is
\mathbb{Q}
.
For any neat compact open subgroup
K^{\prime}
of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
, there is a smooth
quasi-projective

\mathbb{Q}
-scheme
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}(K^{\prime})
such that as complex analytic varieties, we have

\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}(K^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}=\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash(X_{\GL_{2}}\times\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})/K^{\prime}),

where
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}(K^{\prime})_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}
means the analytification of the base change of
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}(K^{\prime})
to
\mathbb{C}
.

Remark 3.23
.

•

Our choice of Shimura datum is standard, bu is a little different from
(
LSZ 22
, 8.1.1)
(see the difference in
(
LSZ 22
, Remark 8.1.1)
).

•

The maps
\iota\colon H\hookrightarrow G
and
p\colon H\twoheadrightarrow\GL_{2}
of algebraic groups induce the following
E
-morphisms of Shimura varieties for suitable neat compact open subgroup
L
,
K
and
K^{\prime}
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
,
H(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
:

(3.9)

\hbox to146.84pt{\vbox to52.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 73.41791pt\lower-26.32838pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-73.41791pt}{-20.16866pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

to
E
.

Fact 3.24
.

•

The decomposition

\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{G,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}

can be further decomposed as

\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{G,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{+}_{G,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{-}_{G,\mathbb{C}},

where
\liep^{+}_{G,\mathbb{C}}
is

\{\begin{pmatrix}0&0&*\\
0&0&*\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},

on which
(z,\bar{z})\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{C})
acts by
\frac{z}{\bar{z}}
and

\liep^{-}_{\mathbb{C}}
is

\{\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
*&*&0\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},

on which
(z,\bar{z})\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{C})
acts by
\frac{\bar{z}}{z}
.

•

Similarly, we have the decomposition for
H
:

\lieh_{\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{H,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{+}_{H,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{-}_{H,\mathbb{C}},

where
\liep^{+}_{H,\mathbb{C}}
is

\{\begin{pmatrix}0&*\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\lieh_{\mathbb{C}}

and
\liep^{-}_{H,\mathbb{C}}
is

\{\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
*&0\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\lieh_{\mathbb{C}}.

•

We have a decomposition for
\GL_{2}
:

\liegl_{2,\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{+}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{-}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}},

where
\liep^{+}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}
is

\{\begin{pmatrix}0&*\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\liegl_{2,\mathbb{C}}

and
\liep^{-}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}
is

\{\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
*&0\end{pmatrix}\}\subset\liegl_{2,\mathbb{C}}.

Convention 3.25
.

(1)

We use
M(K)
(resp.,
S(L)
) to denote
\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K))
(resp.,
\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L))
), where
\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}
is the Weil restriction. Hence, we have

\displaystyle S(L)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}=\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}\bigsqcup\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}},

\displaystyle M(K)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}=\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}\bigsqcup\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}},

where
\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}}
(resp.,
\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}}
) is the complex conjugate of
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}
(resp.,
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}
).

(2)

Since we compute complex regulators in this paper, an explicit choice of a level structure of a Shimura variety is not necessary. Hence, we will sometimes ignore the level structure in the notation related to Shimura varieties and write
S
,
M
and
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}
instead of
S(L)
,
M(K)
and
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}(K^{\prime})
when there is no confusion. If reader prefers, this can be explained as a Shimura variety with suitable neat level structure.

(3)

Applying the Weil restriction functor
\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}
to the diagram (
3.9
), we get the following
\mathbb{Q}
-morphisms of Shimura varieties:

3.4.
Cohomology of Picard modular surfaces

Convention 3.26
.

(1)

For any
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(G)
(resp.,
W\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(H)
), we denote by
\overline{V}
(resp.,
\overline{W}
) the twist of
V
(resp.,
W
) by the non-trivial element of
\Gal(E/\mathbb{Q})
.

(2)

For any morphism
(f\colon V\rightarrow V^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(G)
(resp.,
(g\colon W\rightarrow W^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(H)
), we denote by
\overline{f}\colon\overline{V}\rightarrow\overline{V^{\prime}}
(resp.,
\overline{g}\colon\overline{W}\rightarrow\overline{W^{\prime}}
) the twist of
f
(resp.,
g
) by the non-trivial element of
\Gal(E/\mathbb{Q})
.

(3)

We denote by
\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
(resp.,
\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H)
) the cateogry whose objects are
(V,\overline{V})
(resp.,
(W,\overline{W})
), where
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(G)
(resp.,
W\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(H)
); and whose morphisms are
(f,\overline{f})
(resp.,
(g,\overline{g})
), where
(f:V\rightarrow V^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(G)
(resp.,
(g:W\rightarrow W^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(H)
).

(4)

We denote by
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
(resp.,
W\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H)
) the pair
(V,\overline{V})
(resp.,
(W,\overline{W})
). When talking about weights of
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
(resp.,
W\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H)
), we offen mean the weights of
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(G)
(resp.,
W\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(H)
).

(5)

For any
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
(resp.,
W\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H)
), there is a canonical local system over
S(L)
(resp.,
M(K)
), which is also denoted by
V
(resp.,
W
).

(6)

We denote by
\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{C}}(G)
(resp.,
W\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{C}}(H)
the category whose objects are
V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
(resp.,
W\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
) such that
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
(resp.,
W\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H)
).

For any
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{C}}(G)
, let
\xi
be the restriction of the inverse of the central character of
V
to
A_{G}
and let
L
be an compact open subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Let
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{c}(S(L),V(2))
be the de Rham complex of
C^{\infty}
-differential forms with compact support on
S(L)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}
with values in the local system
V(2)
. Similarly, let
\mathcal{A}^{*}(S(L),V(2))
be the usual de Rham complex and let
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{(2)}(S(L),V(2))
be the complex of square integrable differential forms. If
\circ
is the symbol
c
,
(2)
or the empty symbol, we define

\mathcal{A}^{*}_{\circ}(S,V(2))=\varinjlim_{L}\mathcal{A}^{*}_{\circ}(S(L),V(2)).

Let
\lieg
be the Lie algebra of
G(\mathbb{R})
and
K_{G}=A_{G}(\U(2)\times\U(1))(\mathbb{R})
be the subgroup of
G(\mathbb{R})
which is maximal modulo the center. For any
(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G})
-module
M
, let
\mathcal{C}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};M)
be the
(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G})
-complex of
M
. Then we have
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-equivariant isomorphisms of complexes:
(
BW 00
, VII §2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{*}_{c}(S_{\mathbb{Q}},V(2))\cong\mathcal{C}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}C_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{*}(S_{\mathbb{Q}},V(2))\cong\mathcal{C}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)),

that are compatible with the inclusions
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{c}(S_{\mathbb{Q}},V(2))\subset\mathcal{A}^{*}(S_{\mathbb{Q}},V(2))
and

\mathcal{C}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}C_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi))\subset\mathcal{C}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)).

If we take cohomology on both sides, we obtain
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-equivariant isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR,c}(S,V(2))\cong\mathrm{H}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}C_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)),

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR}(S,V(2))\cong\mathrm{H}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)),

which are compatible with the maps from cohomology with compact support to cohomology without support.

Let
\mathrm{H}^{*}_{(2)}(S,V(2))
be the cohomology of the complex
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{(2)}(S,V(2))
which
is the
L^{2}
cohomology of
S
with coefficients in
V(2)
. According to
Bor 83
, we have a
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-equivariant isomorphism

\mathrm{H}^{*}_{(2)}(S,V(2))\cong\mathrm{H}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}C_{(2)}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)),

where
C_{(2)}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
denotes the subspace of functions in
C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
which are square integrable modulo the center of
G
.
We also use the notation
\mathrm{H}^{*}_{\text{cusp}}(S,V(2))
to denote

\mathrm{H}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}C_{\text{cusp}}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)),

where
C_{cusp}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
denotes the subspace of functions in
C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
which are cuspidal.

By definition, we have the following maps:

C_{\text{cusp}}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)\rightarrow C_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)\rightarrow C_{(2)}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)\rightarrow C^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi),

where the first map is defined by smooth truncation to a large set which is compact modulo the center, and the second and the third arrows are the natural inclusions.
These maps induce maps of the corresponding cohomology groups:

\mathrm{H}^{*}_{\text{cusp}}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR,c}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}_{(2)}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR}(S,V(2)).

Let

\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR,!}(S,V(2))=\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR,c}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR}(S,V(2)).

be the interior de Rham cohomology.
In general, by
(
Bor 74
, Theorem 7.5)
, the map

\mathrm{H}^{*}_{\text{cusp}}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR,!}(S,V(2))

is injective.

Proposition 3.27
.

If the algebraic representation
V
is regular, we have

\mathrm{H}^{*}_{\text{cusp}}(S,V(2))=\mathrm{H}^{*}_{(2)}(S,V(2))=\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR,!}(S,V(2))=\mathrm{H}^{2}_{dR,!}(S,V(2)).

Proof.

For the equality besides the last one, by the fact that the map

\mathrm{H}^{*}_{\text{cusp}}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR,!}(S,V(2))

is injective, it suffices to prove

\mathrm{H}^{*}_{\text{cusp}}(S,V(2))=\mathrm{H}^{*}_{(2)}(S,V(2)).

By
(
Bor 80
, Theorem 4)
(which applies for
\rank G=\rank K_{G}
), we have

\mathrm{H}^{*}_{dR,!}(S,V(2))=\bigoplus_{\pi=\pi_{\infty}\otimes\pi_{f}}m(\pi)\mathrm{H}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{\infty})\otimes\pi_{f},

where
\pi
runs over the discrete spectrum of
L^{2}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}),\xi)
, and
\xi
is the restriction of the central character of
V^{\vee}
to
A_{G}
.
Let
\pi=\pi_{f}\otimes\pi_{\infty}
be such an automorphic representation. By
(
VZ 84
, Theorem 5.6)
4
4

4

Although
\pi_{\infty}
is not unitary as a representation of
G(\mathbb{R})^{+}
, if
we restrict to the semisimple part we could make it unitary. Hence, we can use the theorem in
VZ 84
, because the authors assume the real Lie group is semisimple.
,
the assumption
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{\infty})\neq 0
implies
\pi_{\infty}=A_{\lieq}(\lambda)
, which is a cohomological induction from a
\theta
-stable parabolic subalgebra
\lieq
. Since the highest weight of
V
is regular, the only
\lieq
that satisfies the condition in
(
VZ 84
, Theorem 5.6)
is the Borel. In our case, the torus of the Borel is compact, so
A_{q}(\lambda)
must be a discrete series. Discrete series are tempered, so by
(
BW 00
, III, Corollary 5.2)
,
\pi_{\infty}
only contributes to middle degree, that is

\mathrm{H}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{\infty})=\mathrm{H}^{2}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{\infty}).

Finally, for
\pi
appearing in the discrete spectrum, by Wallach’s theorem
(
Wal 84
, Theorem 4.3)

\pi
must be cuspidal.
∎

Remark 3.28
.

The condition that
V
is regular is very important for Proposition
3.27
to hold.

3.5.

Convention 3.29

According to

We have the decomposition:

\wedge^{2}\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}/\liek_{G,\mathbb{C}}=\wedge^{2}\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{+}\oplus\wedge^{2}\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{-}\oplus\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{+}\otimes\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{-}.

And we have the following identities as representations of

:

\displaystyle\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{+}\otimes\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{-}=\tau_{(1,-1,0)}\oplus\tau_{(0,0,0)}.

We also have the following proposition:

.

The dimension of the
\mathbb{C}

.

Finally, we can compute explicitly the elements of

.

Proposition 3.34
.

If the highest weight of
V(2)
is
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d)
such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}
,
we have

P(V(2))=\{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2},\bar{\pi}_{3}\},

where for
i=1,2,3
,
\bar{\pi}_{i}
is the complex conjugate of
\pi_{i}
and the Harish-Chandra parameters and Blattner parameters
7
7

7

We ignore the action of similitude factor in the notation of Harish-chandra and Blattner parameters because it is determind by the fixed central character.
of
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{3}
are listed in the following table.

(3.12)

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&\text{Harish-Chandra parameters}&\text{Blattner parameters}\\
\hline\cr\pi_{1}&(1-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2},-1-\lambda_{1})&(1-\lambda_{3},1-\lambda_{2},-2-\lambda_{1})\\
\hline\cr\pi_{2}&(1-\lambda_{3},-1-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2})&(1-\lambda_{3},-1-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2})\\
\hline\cr\pi_{3}&(-\lambda_{2},-1-\lambda_{1},1-\lambda_{3})&(-1-\lambda_{2},-1-\lambda_{1},2-\lambda_{3})\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Proof.

For a dominant root system with positive roots

\Delta^{+}=\{(1,-1,0),(0,1,-1),(1,0,-1)\},

we have
\rho=(1,0,-1)
. Hence, the infinitesimal character of
V(2)
is
(\lambda_{1}+1,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}-1)
.
By
(
BW 00
, II, Proposition 3.1)
, the infinitesimal character of the representation
\pi_{\infty}
of

with

\mathrm{H}^{*}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{\infty})\neq 0

must be the negative of the infinitesimal character of
V(2)
. Hence, the representations in
P(V(2))
must have infinitesimal character
(1-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{2},-1-\lambda_{1})
. Then by
(
Kna 86
, Theorem 9.20)
, we can write down all the discrete series with fixed infinitesimal character.
∎

3.6.
Hodge decomposition

In this subsection, we compute the Hodge decomposition of the Betti realization of the pure motive associated to an automorphic representation
\pi
.

Convention 3.36
.

In this subsection,
V
is any object in
\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
. We use
V_{\mathbb{C}}
to denote
V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
.

Recall that we have the following theorem.

Theorem 3.37

(
BHR 94
,Theorem 3.2.2 and (2.3.3))
.

For any cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi=\pi_{f}\otimes\pi_{\infty}
such that
\pi_{\infty}\in P(V_{\mathbb{C}}(2))
,
\pi_{f}
is defined over a number field
E(\pi_{f})
.

Convention 3.38
.

In this subsection, we use
\pi_{f}
to denote its
E(\pi_{f})
-model.

Definition 3.39
.

We define

M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2)):=\Hom_{\mathbb{Q}[G(\mathbb{A}_{f})]}(\Res_{E(\pi_{f})/\mathbb{Q}}\pi_{f},\mathrm{H}_{dR,!}^{2}(S,V(2)))

and similarly

M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2)):=\Hom_{\mathbb{Q}[G(\mathbb{A}_{f})]}(\Res_{E(\pi_{f})/\mathbb{Q}}\pi_{f},\mathrm{H}_{B,!}^{2}(S,V(2))),

where
\mathrm{H}_{dR,!}^{2}(S,V(2))
and
\mathrm{H}_{B,!}^{2}(S,V(2))
are the interior de Rham and Betti cohomology with coefficients in
V
.
These are
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces with a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear action of
E(\pi_{f})
.

Remark 3.40
.

Since
M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space, define its base change to
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{C}
:

\displaystyle M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}:=M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}:=M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}.

After base change to
\mathbb{C}
, we have the comparison isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\infty}\colon M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

We also have

\displaystyle\rank_{E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}}M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}=\rank_{E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}=6.

Finally, we state a proposition about the Hodge decomposition of
M_{B}(\pi_{f},V)_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proposition 3.41
.

If the highest weight of
V(2)
is
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d)
such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}
,
we have

\displaystyle M_{B}({\pi}_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle M_{B}^{2-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-d,-\lambda_{1}-d}\oplus M_{B}^{1-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}-d,1-\lambda_{2}-d}\oplus M_{B}^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-d,2-\lambda_{3}-d}

\displaystyle\oplus

\displaystyle M_{B}^{-\lambda_{1}-d,2-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-d}\oplus M_{B}^{1-\lambda_{2}-d,1-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}-d}\oplus M_{B}^{2-\lambda_{3}-d,-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-d},

where
M_{B}^{s,t}
is a
1
-dimensional
\mathbb{C}
-subspace with Hodge type
(s,t)
.
So
M_{B}({\pi}_{f},V(2))
is a pure
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structure with weight
2-(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+2d)
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle M_{B}^{2-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-d,-\lambda_{1}-d}=\bigoplus_{\sigma:E(\pi_{F})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{1}),

\displaystyle M_{B}^{1-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}-d,1-\lambda_{2}-d}=\bigoplus_{\sigma:E(\pi_{F})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{2}),

\displaystyle M_{B}^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-d,2-\lambda_{3}-d}=\bigoplus_{\sigma:E(\pi_{F})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{H}^{2}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{3}),

where

P(V_{\mathbb{C}}(2))=\{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3},\bar{\pi}_{1},\bar{\pi}_{2},\bar{\pi}_{3}\}

is defined in Proposition
3.34
.

Proof.

For
i=1,2,3
, if the Hodge type of
\pi_{i}
is
(s,t)
, then the Hodge type of
\bar{\pi}_{i}
is
(t,s)
. So we only need to compute the Hodge type of
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
.
Since complexification of
h
in the definition of the Shimura datum of
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)
is

h_{\mathbb{C}}\colon(z,\bar{z})\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{C})\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}z&0&0\\
0&z&0\\
0&0&\bar{z}\end{pmatrix},

the contribution from the local system
V
only depends on
K_{G}
-types. Then we compute the Hodge type of
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
case by case.

(1)

For
\pi_{1}
: The positive roots in
(
Kna 86
, Theorem 9.20)
for
\pi_{1}
are

\Delta^{+}=\{(1,-1,0),(0,1,-1),(1,0,-1)\}.

Hence the lowest weight of

is
(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1})
, the minimal
K_{G}
-type of

is
\tau_{(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1})}
.
Since the minimal
K_{G}
-type of
\pi_{1}
is
\tau_{(1-\lambda_{3},1-\lambda_{2},-2-\lambda_{1})}
, and the minimal

is
\tau_{(1,1,-2)}
. Hence, only the
K_{G}
-type

contributes to

\mathrm{H}^{2}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{1})=\Hom_{K_{G}}(\wedge^{2}\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}/\liek_{G,\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{1}).

Since the contributed
K_{G}
-type of
V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)
is
\tau_{(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1})}
and

\tau_{(1,1,-2)}\subset\wedge^{2}\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{+}\subset\wedge^{2}\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}/\liek_{G,\mathbb{C}},

the contributed Hodge type of
\pi_{1}
is

(2-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-d,-\lambda_{1}-d).

(2)

For
\pi_{2}
: The positive roots in
(
Kna 86
, Theorem 9.20)
for
\pi_{2}
are

\Delta^{+}=\{(1,-1,0),(0,-1,1),(1,0,-1)\}.

Hence the lowest weight of

is
(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, so the minimal
K_{G}
-type of
V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)
is
\tau_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2})}
.
Since the minimal
K_{G}
-type of
\pi_{2}
is
\tau_{(1-\lambda_{3},-1-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2})}
, the minimal
K_{G}
-type of

is
\tau_{(1,-1,0)}
. Hence, only the
K_{G}
-type
\tau_{(1,-1,0)}
contributes to

\mathrm{H}^{2}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{2})=\Hom_{K_{G}}(\wedge^{2}\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}/\liek_{G,\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{2}).

Since the contributed
K_{G}
-type of
V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)
is
\tau_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2})}
and

\tau_{(1,-1,0)}\subset\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{+}\otimes\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{-}\subset\wedge^{2}\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}/\liek_{G,\mathbb{C}},

the contributed Hodge type of
\pi_{1}
is

(1-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3}-d,1-\lambda_{2}-d).

(3)

For
\pi_{3}
: The positive roots in
(
Kna 86
, Theorem 9.20)
for

are

\Delta^{+}=\{(-1,0,1),(0,-1,1),(1,-1,0)\}.

is

, so the minimal

-type of

is
\tau_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}

Since the minimal

-type of

-type of
V_{\mathbb{C}}(2)
is

and

is

Corollary 3.42
.

If
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
, we have

\displaystyle M_{B}({\pi}_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle M_{B}^{-r,-2-a-b-s}\oplus M_{B}^{-1-a-r,-1-b-s}\oplus M_{B}^{-2-a-b-r,-s}

\displaystyle\oplus

\displaystyle M_{B}^{-2-a-b-s,-r}\oplus M_{B}^{-1-b-s,-1-a-r}\oplus M_{B}^{-s,-2-a-b-r}.

Part I
The vanishing on the boundary

4.
Construction of the motivic classes

4.1.
Relative motives and motivic cohomology

In this subsection, we first recall some basic facts about motivic categories. We then use motivic categories to give the definition of motivic cohomology and collect the needed facts about it. Finally, we give the Gysin map of motivic cohomology corresponding to the map of the Shimura varieties that we are interested in.

4.1.1.
Motivic categories

Let
\Lambda
be a commutative ring with a unit and let
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S,\Lambda)
be the
triangulated category of constructible Beilinson motives over a base excellent scheme
S
with
\Lambda
-coefficients
defined in
(
CD 19
, Def.15.1.1)
, which is a symmetric monoidal category and we denote by
1_{S}
the unit of the category, which is the Tate motive
(
CD 19
, Introduction B, page xxi)
. We write
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
for
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
.

Fact 4.1
.

We have the following properties:

•

(
CD 19
, Theorem 15.2.4)
The triangulated category
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
has the formalism of Grothendieck’s six-functors

(f^{*},f_{*},f_{!},f^{!},\underline{\Hom},\otimes)

and dualizing functor
\mathbb{D}
.

•

(
CD 19
, Remark 11.1.14)
Over a field
F
, we have the following equivalence of triangulated categories:

(4.1)

\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(F))\cong\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(F),

where the right hand side is the triangulated category of geometric motives
(
Voe 00
, Definition 2.1.1, Page 5)
. We use the following isomorphism: for any smooth
f\colon X\rightarrow\Spec(F)
, the motive
f_{*}1_{X}\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(F))
is mapped to the dual of the motive
M_{gm}(X)\in\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(F)
defined in
(
Voe 00
, Definition 2.1.1)
.

Proposition 4.2

(
(
PL 19
, Proposition 1.7)
)
.

For a regular immersion
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
with
\dim X-\dim Y=d
and any
M\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
, we have the following absolute purity isomorphism:

f^{*}(M)\cong f^{!}M(d)[2d].

Remark 4.3
.

It follows from
(
Sta 25
,
Tag 0E9J
)
that if
Y
and
X
are regular schemes, then
f
is a regular immersion.

Let
\Lambda
be a field. Corti and Hanamura
(
CH 00
, Definition 2.9)
defined the category of relative Chow motives
\mathrm{CHM}(S,\Lambda)
with
\Lambda
-coefficients over a base scheme
S
generalizing the category of Chow motives over a field. We write
\mathrm{CHM}(S)
for
\mathrm{CHM}(S,\mathbb{Q})
. The relationship between
\mathrm{CHM}(S)
and
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
proved by Jin is as follows:

Proposition 4.4

(
(
Fan 16
, Theorem 3.17)
)
.

Let
\mathrm{Chow}(S)
be the heart of the Chow weight structure on
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
constructed in
(
H1́ 1
, Théorème 3.3.)
. Then there is a natural functor

F\colon\mathrm{CHM}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Chow}(S),

which is an equivalence of the two categories
\mathrm{CHM}(S)
and
\mathrm{Chow}(S)
.

Convention 4.5
.

This proposition tells us that for any object
V\in\mathrm{CHM}(S)
,
F(V)
is an object of the category
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
. In order to simplify notation, we write
V
for
F(V)
.

Finally, we recall the definition of motives of Abelian type which will be used in the proof of vanishing on the boundary for motivic cohomology.

Definition 4.6

(
(
Wil 15
, Definition 1.1)
)
.

Let
F
be a perfect field and
\bar{F}
be its algebraic closure.

(1)

The category of
Chow motives of Abelian type
over
\bar{F}
is defined as a strict full additive tensor sub-category
\mathrm{CHM}^{Ab}(\bar{F})
of
\mathrm{CHM}(\bar{F})
generated by the following:

(a)

Shifts of Tate motives
\mathbb{Z}(m)[2m]
, for
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(b)

Chow motives of Abelian varieties over
\bar{F}
.

(2)

Define the category of
Chow motives of Abelian type over
\bar{F}
as the (strict) full (additive tensor) sub-category
\mathrm{CHM}^{Ab}(F)
of
\mathrm{CHM}(F)
whose base change to
\bar{F}
lies in
\mathrm{CHM}^{Ab}(\bar{F})
.

(3)

The category
\mathrm{DM}_{gm}^{Ab}(F)
is defined as the (strict) full triangulated subcategory of
\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(F)
generated by
\mathrm{CHM}^{Ab}(F)
.

Notation 4.7
.

We denote by
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}^{Ab}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))
the sub-category of
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))
that is isomorphic to
\mathrm{DM}_{gm}^{Ab}(F)
via the isomorphim in equation (
4.1
).

4.1.2.
The localization exact triangle

We recall the localization exact triangle of Beilinson motives that will be used in the proof of vanishing on the boundary for motivic cohomology.

Let

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{i}

be a diagram in
\mathrm{Sch}(\mathbb{Q})
where
j
is an open immersion and
i
is the complementary reduced closed immersion. We assume that
U
has the same dimension as
X
and that
Y
has codimension
c
in
X
.

Proposition 4.8

(
(
CD 19
, Proposition 2.3.3)
)
.

Let
N
be an object of
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
. We have the following exact triangle

\textstyle{j_{!}j^{*}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}i^{*}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{!}j^{*}N[1]}

in
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
.

4.1.3.
Motivic cohomology

Definition 4.9

(Motivic Cohomology)
.

If
W
is an object of
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
and
{1}_{S}
is the unit object of
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
, then the
motivic cohomology
is defined as the
\Lambda
-module

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{*}(S,W)=\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)}(1_{S},W[*]).

Remark 4.10
.

•

It follows from
(
CD 19
, Corrollary 14.2.14)
that this definition is compatible with the K-theoretical one:
for any regular scheme
X
, we have a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{q}(X,\mathbb{Q}(p))\cong\mathrm{K}_{2p-q}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}^{(p)},

where the right-hand side is the
p
-th graded piece of the
\gamma
-filtration of an algebraic
\mathrm{K}
-group.

•

Recall that in
(
Wil 09
, Page 21)
, for any
M\in\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q})
, the motivic cohomology
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{i}(M,\mathbb{Q}(j))
is defined as

(4.2)

\mathrm{H}_{M}^{i}(M,\mathbb{Q}(j)):=\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q})}(M,\mathbb{Q}(i)[j]).

We can see that this definition is compatible with our definition through the isomorphism in equation (
4.1
).

Notation 4.11
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
temporarily denote any of the three groups
\{\GL_{2},H,G\}
, so we have the associated Shimura variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Lemma 4.12

(
(
Anc 15
, Theorem 8.6)
)
.

Let
F
be a number field. There is an additive functor

\mu_{M}\colon\mathrm{Rep}_{F}(\mathcal{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{CHM}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}},F)

(1)

(2)

For the similitude character

\mathbb{Q}(1)

8

Remark 4.13
.

We give four remarks about Lemma
4.12
.

(1)

The construction by Ancona in Lemma
4.12
works much more generally for arbitrary PEL Shimura varieties, but in this paper, we shall only need it for the above three groups.

(2)

Our normalization is compatible with Ancona’s original normalization, but dual to the normalization of
(

, Theorem 8.3.1)
where they send the multiplier representation to
\mathbb{Q}(-1)
and the defining representation to
\mathrm{h}^{1}(A)
.

(3)

The Ancona functor
\mu_{M}
is faithful.
(
Tor 20
, REMARK 5.10)
.

(4)

For any

and any integer
