, we have
\mu_{M}(V(d))=\mu_{M}(V)(d)
. Hence, we can use the twist
(d)
before or after applying ancona functor.

By applying Torzewski’s general theorem
(
Tor 20
, Theorem 9.7)
in the setting of
G
and
H
, we get the following result:

Proposition 4.14

(“Branching” for motivic sheaves)
.

For the map of Shimura varieties
\iota:M\rightarrow S
, we have the following commutative diagram of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Rep}_{F}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CHM}(S,F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Rep}_{F}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CHM}(M,F),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{M}}

where the left-hand
\iota^{*}
denotes the restriction of representations, and the right-hand
\iota^{*}
denotes the pullback of relative Chow motives.

Convention 4.15
.

•

For any
\mathcal{G}
, we write
\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
for
\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{G})
. For any
V\in\mathrm{Rep}(\mathcal{G})
, we also write
V
for
\mu_{M}(V)
.

•

Recall that we define the elements of
\mathrm{Rep}(G)
and
\mathrm{Rep}(H)
as the Weil restriction of elements of
\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(G)
and
\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(H)
.

•

Recall that we always let
M=\mathrm{Sh}_{H}
and
S=\mathrm{Sh}_{G}
.

From the above discussion, for a representation of
\mathcal{G}
denoted as
V
, we get a relative Chow motive
V\in\mathrm{CHM}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}})
over Shimura variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}}
, which can also be viewed as an object of
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}})
. So for
V
, we can define the motivic cohomology
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{*}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}},V(*))

9
9

9

In this paper, in order to apply Beilinson regulator, we define all motivic cohomology groups to be
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces, which is the same as Weil restriction to
\mathbb{Q}
of the motivic cohomology groups defined in
LSZ 22
.
.

Proposition 4.16

(Gysin morphism for motivic cohomology)
.

For the map of Shimura varieties
\iota\colon M\rightarrow S
defined earlier and for the representations
W\in\mathrm{Rep}(H)
and
V\in\mathrm{Rep}(G)
satisfying
W\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}V
in
\mathrm{Rep}(H)
, we have the Gysin morphism

\iota_{*}\colon\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(M,W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{M}}(S,V(2)),

which is induced by

(4.3)

(\iota_{*}W(1)[1]\rightarrow V(2)[3])\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S).

Proof.

From the above discussions, we have a map
W\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}V
in
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(M)
, where
\iota^{*}
is the pullback functor on the category of Beilinson motives. By the absolute purity isomorphism
(
PL 19
, Proposition 1.7)
, the right hand side is
\iota^{*}V\cong\iota^{!}V(1)[2]
. By adjunction, we get the morphism
\iota_{!}W\rightarrow V(1)[2]
in
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
. Since
\iota
is proper, we have
\iota_{*}=\iota_{!}
. So we get the morphism

\iota_{*}W(1)[1]\rightarrow V(2)[3]\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S).

Then by applying the functor
N\longmapsto\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)}(1_{S},N)
to the morphism, we get

\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)}(1_{S},\iota_{*}W(1)[1])\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)}(1_{S},V(2)[3]).

The right-hand side is
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{M}}(S,V(2))
, and by adjunction and
\iota^{*}1_{S}=1_{M}
, the left-hand side is

\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(M)}(1_{M},W(1)[1]),

which is
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(M,W(1))
.
∎

4.2.
Mixed Hodge modules and absolute Hodge cohomology

In this subsection, we first recall some basic facts about mixed Hodge modules, which can be viewed as a relative version of mixed Hodge structures, “archimedean” analogues of mixed
l{\textendash}\mathrm{adic}
perverse sheaves, or the Hodge realization of the mixed motivic sheaves which have not been defined. We then use mixed Hodge modules to define absolute Hodge cohomology, which can be seen as the Hodge realization of the motivic cohomology defined in Definition
4.9
, and collect the needed facts about it. Finally, we give the Gysin map of absolute Hodge cohomology corresponding to the map of the Shimura varieties that we are interested in. For this part, we mainly follow
(
Lem 15
, §2.2)
.

4.2.1.
Mixed Hodge modules

Let
A\subset\mathbb{R}
be a subfield and
\mathrm{Sch}(\mathbb{Q})
be the category of quasi-projective
\mathbb{Q}
-schemes. For
X\in\mathrm{Sch}(\mathbb{Q})
, we have the abelian category
\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})
of real algebraic mixed
A
-Hodge modules
(
HW 98
, Definition A.2.4)
. Let
\mathrm{D}_{c}^{b}(X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}},A)
be the bounded derived category of sheaves for the analytic topology of
A
-vector spaces with constructible cohomology objects and let
\mathrm{Perv}_{A}(X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}})\subset\mathrm{D}_{c}^{b}(X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}},A)
be the abelian category of perverse sheaves on
X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Fact 4.17
.

We have the following properties:

(1)

Bei 87
The natural functor
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{Perv}_{A}(X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}}))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{c}^{b}(X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}},A)
is an equivalence of categories.

(2)

(
Sai90b
, Theorem 0.1)
There is a faithful and exact functor

rat\colon\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{Perv}_{A}(X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}}).

We also use the same notation to denote its derived functor

rat\colon\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R}))\rightarrow\mathrm{D}_{c}^{b}(X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}},A).

Also, for
M\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R}))
, we have
rat(\mathcal{H}^{i}M)=\prescript{p}{}{\mathcal{H}^{i}}rat(M)
, where
\mathcal{H}^{i}M
is the standard cohomology functor and
\prescript{p}{}{\mathcal{H}^{i}}
is the perverse cohomology functor.

(3)

Sai90a
,
Sai90b
,
(
HW 98
, Theorem A.2.5)
We have six-functors formalism with

(f^{*},f_{*},f_{!},f^{!},\underline{\Hom},\otimes)

and the dualizing functor
\mathbb{D}
on the derived category
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R}))
, which is a symmetric monoidal category. Furthermore, these functors commute with the functor
rat
.

We now collect some facts about the relationship between variations of Hodge structures and mixed Hodge modules.

Assume that
X
is smooth and pure of dimension
d
. Then for any local system
V
of
A
-vector spaces on
X_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{an}}
, by the definition of perverse sheaves the complex
V[d]
concentrated in degree
-d
is an object of
\mathrm{Perv}_{A}(X^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathbb{C}})
.

Let
\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})^{s}
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})
whose objects are those
M\in\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})
such that
rat(M)=V[d]
for some local system
V
. Denote by
\mathrm{Var}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})
the category of real admissible polarizable variations of mixed
A
-Hodge structures over
X
(
HW 98
, Definition A.2.4 b)
.

Fact 4.18

(
(
HW 98
, Definition A.2.4.b)
)
.

There is an equivalence of categories

\mathrm{Var}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})\cong\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})^{s}.

From this, we have the following equivalence of abelian categories:

\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q})/\mathbb{R})\cong\mathrm{MHS}_{A}^{+},

where the right hand side denotes the abelian category of mixed polarizable
A
-Hodge structures
(
HW 98
, Lemma A.2.2)
.

Convention 4.19
.

(1)

We use the convention that a variation of mixed Hodge structure is viewed as a mixed Hodge module but not a shift of mixed Hodge modules, which is the same as the one used in
BW04a
. When
X
is smooth and pure of dimension
d
, the embedding

\iota\colon\mathrm{Var}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R}))

is characterized by the fact: for any object
V\in\mathrm{Var}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})
, the complex
rat(\iota(V))[-d]
is a single non-trivial constituent in degree zero, which means that it is a local system. When the context is clear, we always write
V
for
\iota(V)
.

(2)

In order to simplify terminology, when
A=\mathbb{R}
we will write “variation of Hodge structure” instead of “real admissible polarizable variation of mixed
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge structure” and write “mixed Hodge modules” for “real algebraic mixed
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge modules”.

Proposition 4.20

(
(
Blo 06
, Proposition 2.3.12, Proposition 2.3.14, Lemme 2.3.7)
)
.

Under the embedding

\iota\colon\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)/\mathbb{R}),

we have the following three consequences.

(1)

For any
V,W\in\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R})
, we have

\iota(V\otimes W)[d]=\iota(V)\otimes\iota(W),

where the left tensor product is the tensor product in
\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R})
and the right tensor product is the tensor product in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)/\mathbb{R})
.

(2)

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism between smooth algebraic varieties with
\dim X-\dim Y=d
. For any
V\in\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R})
, we have the identity:

\iota((f^{s})^{*}V)=f^{*}\iota(V)[d],

where
(f^{s})^{*}
is pullback in
\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R})
and
f^{*}
is the pullback in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)/\mathbb{R})
.
In particular, if
s\colon X\rightarrow\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
is a smooth scheme which is pure of dimension
d
,
A_{X}(n)
(resp.,
A(n)
) is the Tate variation on
X
(resp.,
\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
) viewed as an object of
\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})
(resp.,
\mathrm{MHS}_{A}^{+}
), we have the equality
A_{X}(n)=s^{*}A(n)[d]
in
\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})
.

(3)

For any
V\in\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R})
, the following identity holds:

\mathbb{D}(\iota(V))=\iota(V^{\vee})(d),

where
(\cdot)^{\vee}
denotes the dualizing functor in
\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R})
and
\mathbb{D}
stands for the dualizing functor in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)/\mathbb{R})
.

Remark 4.21
.

From the proposition, we can see that the unit
1_{X}
in the symmetric monoidal category
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(X)/\mathbb{R})
is
{A(n)_{X}}[-d]
.

Convention 4.22
.

When the scheme
X
is clear from the context, we write
A(n)
for
A(n)_{X}
.

Theorem 4.23

(
(
Sai 88
, Theorem 2)
)
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Sch}(\mathbb{Q})
be smooth and pure of dimension
d
. A variation of Hodge structure of weight
w
is a mixed Hodge module of weight
w+d
via the above identification.

Finally, we state the absolute purity theorem for mixed Hodge modules, which will be used in the construction of the Gysin map for absolute Hodge cohomology.

Proposition 4.24

(
(
Blo 06
, Proposition 2.3.16)
)
.

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of pure relative dimension
d
between smooth algebraic varieties. Then for any
V\in\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(Y/\mathbb{R})
, we have the identity:

f^{!}V=f^{*}V(d)[2d].

4.2.2.
The localization exact triangle

We recall the localization exact triangle of mixed Hodge modules that will be used in the proof of vanishing on the boundary for absolute Hodge cohomology.

Let

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{i}

be a diagram in
\mathrm{Sch}(\mathbb{Q})
where
j
is an open immersion and
i
is the complementary reduced closed immersion. We assume that
U
has the same dimension as
X
and that
Y
has codimension
c
in
X
.

Proposition 4.25

(
(
Sai90b
, (4.4.1))
)
.

Let
N
be an object of
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R}))
, we have the following exact triangle

\textstyle{j_{!}j^{*}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}i^{*}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{!}j^{*}N[1]}

in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R}))
.

Definition 4.26

(Singular (Betti) cohomology)
.

(1)

For
X\in\mathrm{Sch}(\mathbb{Q})
with the structure map
s\colon X\rightarrow\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
, by
(
HW 98
, Corollary A.1.7.c)
,
\mathcal{H}^{i}s_{*}s^{*}A(0)
is the
i
-th singular cohomology of the underlying topological space of
X_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{an}}
with coefficients in
A
. It also has the mixed Hodge structure constructed by Deligne with the involution induced by the complex conjugation on
X(\mathbb{C})
. From this, we get that if
X
is smooth of pure dimension
d
, and if
A(0)
denotes the trivial Tate variation of Hodge structures on
X
, then the
i
-th singular cohomology
is
\mathcal{H}^{i-d}s_{*}A(0)
.

(2)

For any
X
and any
M\in\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R})
, we define the
i
-th singular cohomology
of
X
with
M
as coefficients,
\mathrm{H}^{i}(X,M)
, as the group
\mathcal{H}^{i-d}s_{*}M
; similarly, we define the compactly supported cohomology
\mathrm{H}_{c}^{i}(X,M)
as
\mathcal{H}^{i-d}s_{!}M
.

Corollary 4.27
.

Let
M
be an object of
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(U/\mathbb{R}))
and assume X is proper. We have the following long exact sequence of mixed Hodge structures

(4.4)

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{c}^{i}(U,M)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i}(U,M)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i-c}(Y,i^{*}j_{*}M)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{c}^{i+1}(U,M)\rightarrow\cdots.

Proof.

Let
N=j_{*}M
. Then apply the above triangle to get the exact triangle:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.12302pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.12302pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{j_{!}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.12302pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 36.12302pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{j_{*}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.2024pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 86.2024pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 152.36649pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 152.36649pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{j_{!}M[1]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Let
s\colon X\rightarrow\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
be the structure morphism of
X
and apply the functor
s_{*}
to the exact triangle and then take cohomology
\mathcal{H}^{i-d}
, where
d=\dim X
.
∎

4.2.3.
Absolute Hodge cohomology

We now give the definition of absolute Hodge cohomology and construct its Gysin morphism in the case that we are interested in.

Definition 4.28

(Absolute Hodge cohomology)
.

Let
X
be the scheme as above. The
i
-th real absolute Hodge cohomology

\mathrm{H}^{i}_{H}(X,M)
10
10

10

The standard notation for real absolute Hodge cohomology is
\mathrm{H}^{i}_{H}(X/\mathbb{R},M)
, which is good to distinguish with complex absolute Hodge cohomology. However, in our paper, we only work with real absolute Hodge cohomology, so we use
\mathrm{H}^{i}_{H}(X,M)
to denote real absolute Hodge cohomology.
of
X
with coefficients in
M
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{i}_{H}(X,M):=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R}))}(A(0)_{X},M[i])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{A}(X/\mathbb{R}))}(s^{*}A(0)[d],M[i])

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHS}^{+}_{A})}(A(0),s_{*}M[i-d]),

where the second isomorphism comes from adjunction.

Remark 4.29
.

(1)

This definition is the “archimedean” analogue of the definition of motivic cohomology given in Definition
4.9
.

(2)

If the abelian category
\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
has cohomological dimension
1

(
Bei86b
, Corollary 1.10)
, then by the Leray spectral sequence, we have the short exact sequence

(4.5)

0\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{i-1}(X,M))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{H}^{i}(X,M)\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{i}(X,M))\rightarrow 0

for all
i
and all
M\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(X/\mathbb{R}))
.

Now we come to the setting of Shimura varieties.

Notation 4.30
.

•

Recall that there is a
\mathbb{R}
-linear tensor functor associating an algebraic representation of the group underlying a given Shimura variety to the corresponding variation of Hodge structure on the Shimura variety, and we denote the functor as
\mu_{H}
(see
(
BW04a
, 2)
).

•

Similar to the last section, we let
\mathcal{G}
temporarily denote any of the three groups
\{\GL_{2},H,G\}
, and we have the associated Shimura variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}}
. So if
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{G})
11
11

11

For
H
and
G
, the meaning of
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
(reps.
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H)
) is that there exists some
V^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(G)
(resp.,
V^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{E}(H)
) such that
V=\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}V^{\prime}
.
, we have the variation of Hodge stucture
\mu_{H}(V)\in\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}})
.

•

Compose with the embedding
\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}}/\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}})/\mathbb{R})
, we can view
\mu_{H}(V)
as an object of
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}}/\mathbb{R}))
which has a single non-trivial constituent in degree
0
. We will write
V
instead of
\mu_{H}(V)
when there is no confusion. In other words, we can view
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{G})
as an object of
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}}/\mathbb{R}))
, and we can then define the absolute Hodge cohomology
\mathrm{H}_{H}^{i}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}},V)
.

•

Recall that we always let
M=\mathrm{Sh}_{H}
and
S=\mathrm{Sh}_{G}
.

Remark 4.31
.

Unlike the motivic setting, the “branching” for variation of Hodge structrues is direct from the definition:
for the map of Shimura varieties
\iota\colon M\rightarrow S
, we have the following commutative diagram of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Rep}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Rep}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(M),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{H}}

where the left-hand
\iota^{*}
denotes the restriction of representations, and the right-hand
\iota^{*}
denotes the pullback of variation of Hodge structures.

Just as with motivic cohomolgy, we have the following Gysin morphism.

Proposition 4.32

(Gysin morphism for absolute Hodge cohomology)
.

For the map of Shimura varieties
\iota\colon M\rightarrow S
defined as before and for the representations
W\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H)
and
V\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
satisfying
W\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}V
in
\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H)
, we have the Gysin morphism

\iota_{*}\colon\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(M,W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2)),

which is induced by

(4.6)

(\iota_{*}W(1)\rightarrow V(2)[1])\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R})).

Proof.

From the above discussions, we have a map
W\hookrightarrow(\iota^{s})^{*}V
in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(M)/\mathbb{R})
, where
\iota^{*}
is the pullback functor for
\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathcal{G}})
. It follows from Proposition
4.20
that
(\iota^{s})^{*}V=\iota^{*}V[-1]
, where
\iota^{*}
is the pullback functor for
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R}))
. By the absolute purity isomorphism (see Proposition
4.24
), the right-hand side is
\iota^{*}V\cong\iota^{!}V(1)[2]
. By adjunction, we get the morphism
\iota_{!}W\rightarrow V(1)[1]
in
\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R}))
. Since
\iota
is proper, we have
\iota_{*}=\iota_{!}
, and we get the morphism

(\iota_{*}W(1)\rightarrow V(2)[1])\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R})).

Finally, applying the functor
N\mapsto\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}(S/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathbb{R}(0)_{S},N[2])
to the map, we get the Gysin map
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(M,W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))
.
∎

4.3.
Beilinson regulator and functoriality

In this subsection, we give the definition of Beilinson regulator using the Hodge realization functor for smooth quasi-projective varieties
12
12

12

This level of generality is enough for our applications.
over
\mathbb{Q}
and state its functorial properties.

Definition 4.33

(Hodge realization)
.

For a smooth quasi-projective variety
S
over
\mathbb{Q}
, let
S_{\mathbb{C}}
and
S_{\mathbb{R}}
be the base change of
S
to
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{R}
. The
Hodge realization functor
is defined as the composition of the following functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{H}\colon\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S_{\mathbb{R}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S_{\mathbb{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{MHM}(S_{\mathbb{C}})),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{H}^{Bou}}

where
f_{1}\colon S_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow S
and
f_{2}\colon S_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow S_{\mathbb{R}}
are the canonical map and the functor
R_{H}^{Bou}
is the Hodge realization functor from the triangulated category of constructible Beilinson motives
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S_{\mathbb{C}})
over
S_{\mathbb{C}}
to the (unbounded) derived category
\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{MHM}(S_{\mathbb{C}}))
of algebraic mixed Hodge modules over
S_{\mathbb{C}}
defined in
(
Bou 22
, Definition 169, Theorem 43)
.

It follows from the étale descent property of constructible Beilinson motives (see
(
CD 19
, Theorem 14.3.4)
), the definition of the cateogory
\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R})
(see
(
HW 98
, Definition A.2.4)
), and the functoriality of
R_{H}
(see
(
Bou 22
, Theorem 43 (ii0))
) that the image of
R_{H}
lies in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R}))
. Hence, we get the covariant Hodge realization functor

R_{H}\colon\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R})).

Remark 4.34
.

•

As the functor
R_{H}^{Bou}
(see
(
Bou 22
, Page 1)
) is an extension of the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Variety}/S_{\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{MHM}(S_{\mathbb{C}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\colon X\mapsto S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}\mathbb{R}_{X},}

where
\mathrm{Variety}/S_{\mathbb{C}}
is the category of
\mathbb{C}
-varieties over
S_{\mathbb{C}}
, we see that when we restrict
R_{H}^{Bou}
to the sub-category
\mathrm{CHM}(S_{\mathbb{C}})
, the image lies in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}(S_{\mathbb{C}}))
.

•

We normalize the realization functor such that for any
V
in the category of relative Chow motives
\mathrm{CHM}(S)
over
S
with
\dim S=d
, we have

R_{H}(V)=V[-d]\in\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R})).

Definition 4.35

(Beilinson regulator)
.

For a smooth quasi-projective variety
S
over
\mathbb{Q}
,
\{i,j\}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
W\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
, the
Beilinson regulator

r_{H}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle r_{H}\colon\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{i}(S,W(j))=\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)}(1_{S},W(j)[i])

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i}_{H}(S,W(j))=\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R}))}(R_{H}(1_{S}),R_{H}(W)(j)[i]),

where
R_{H}
is the Hodge realization functor defined above.

Remark 4.36
.

•

When
\dim S=d
, we have

R_{H}(1_{S})=\mathbb{R}(0)_{S}[-d],

which is the unit of
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R}))
.

The following proposition tells us that our definition is compatible with Beilinson’s original definition (see
(
Bei84b
, §2)
).

Proposition 4.37
.

When
W=\mathbb{Q}
, if we choose a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{i}(S,\mathbb{Q}(j))\cong\mathrm{K}_{2j-i}(S)_{\mathbb{Q}}^{(j)},

then the regulator map

r_{H}\colon\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{i}(S,\mathbb{Q}(j))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i}_{H}(S,\mathbb{R}(j))

is the same as the original definition of Beilinson in
(
Bei84b
, §2)
.

Proof.

It follows from the functoriality of
R_{H}
that the Beilinson regulator can be defined as

\displaystyle r_{H}\colon\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{i}(S,\mathbb{Q}(j))=\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})},a_{*}1_{S}(j)[i])

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i}_{H}(S,\mathbb{R}(j))=\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHS}^{+}_{\mathbb{R}})}(R_{H}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})}),R_{H}(a_{*}1_{S})(j)[i]),

where
a\colon S\rightarrow\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
is the structure morphism of
S
. It can be seen from the definition of Huber’s contravariant Hodge realization functor
R_{H}^{Hu}\colon\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHS}^{+}_{\mathbb{R}})
(see
(
Hub 00
, Theorem 2.3.3)
and
(
Hub 04
, Theorem B.2.2)
) that in this case
r_{H}
is the same as

\displaystyle r_{H}^{Hu}\colon\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{i}(S,\mathbb{Q}(j))\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q})}((a_{*}1_{S})^{*},1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})}(j)[i])

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{i}_{H}(S,\mathbb{R}(j))=\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHS}^{+}_{\mathbb{R}})}(R_{H}^{Hu}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})}),R_{H}^{Hu}(a_{*}1_{S})(j)[i]),

where the notation
(\cdot)^{*}
means taking dual.
Finally, we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}_{2j-i}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{i}(S,\mathbb{Q}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{i}(S,\mathbb{Q}(j))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{i}_{H}(S,\mathbb{R}(j)),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{H}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{H}^{Hu}}

where
r_{H}^{B}
is Beilinson’s original definition of his regulator in
(
Bei84b
, §2)
, and the map
p
is defined in
(
Hub 00
, Corollary 4.2.2)
. The commutativity of the right triangle follows from
(
Hub 95
, Definition 18.2.6, Proposition 18.2.8)
and
(
Hub 04
, Corollary 4.2.3)
and the commutativity of the left triangle comes from the canonical choice of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{M}}^{i}(S,\mathbb{Q}(j))\cong\mathrm{K}_{2j-i}(S)_{\mathbb{Q}}^{(j)}
. Thus, the proposition is proved.
∎

By the definition of the Beilinson regulator and functoriality of the Hodge realization functor, we have the following functorial properties of the Beilinson regulator.

Proposition 4.38
.

(1)

For the morphism of Shimura varieties
p\colon M\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}
induced by the projection
H\rightarrow\GL_{2}
, we have the commutative diagram.

4.4.
Motivic classes and their Hodge realizations

In this subsection, we give the constructions of the motivic classes that we are interested in and compute their Hodge realizations.

4.4.1.
Eisenstein symbols

We first recall the construction of Eisenstein symbols.

Recall that the
Eisenstein symbol

(
Bei86a
, §3)
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear map

Eis^{n}_{M}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}_{M}^{n+1}(E^{n},\mathbb{Q}(n+1))

where
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{n+1}(E^{n},\mathbb{Q}(n+1))
is the direct limit over compact open subgroups
K\subset\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
of the motivic cohomology
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{n+1}(E^{n}_{K},\mathbb{Q}(n+1))
of the
n
-th fiber product of the universal elliptic curve
E_{K}/M_{K}
over the modular curve of level
K
.

Definition 4.39
.

The notation
\mathcal{B}_{n}
stands for the space of locally constant
\mathbb{Q}
-valued functions
\phi_{f}
on
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
statisfying the following conditions:

(1)

for all
a,d\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
ad>0
and for all
b\in\mathbb{A}_{f}
, we have

(4.7)

\phi_{f}(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\\
\end{pmatrix}g)=a^{-1}d^{n+1}\phi_{f}(g),

(2)

\phi_{f}(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{pmatrix}g)=\phi_{f}(g),

(3)

for all
k\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
, we have

\phi_{f}(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&k\\
\end{pmatrix}g)=\phi_{f}(g).

Remark 4.40
.

•

The source of the Eisenstein symbol can identified with a space of
\mathbb{Q}
-valued functions
\mathcal{F}^{n}
on
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
in
(
Bei86a
, 2.1.2, P7)
by the following map

\Psi_{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{n}\mapsto(\phi_{f}\colon g\mapsto\phi_{f}(g)=\Psi_{f}(^{t}g))\in\mathcal{B}^{n}.

•

At present, many of the results relating special value of
L
-functions to regulators rely on Eisenstein symbols (
Bei86a
,
Den 89
,
Den 90
,
Kin 98
,
Lem 15
,
Lem 17
,
Kat 04
, …).

Notation 4.41
.

By the definition of the motivic sheaf
\Sym V_{2}(1)
, the motivic cohomology
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{1}(M,\Sym^{n}V_{2}(1))
is a direct summand of
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{n+1}(E^{n},\mathbb{Q}(n+1))
. Moreover, the Eisenstein symbol map factors through the inclusion
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{1}(M,\Sym^{n}V_{2}(1))\subset\mathrm{H}_{M}^{n+1}(E^{n},\mathbb{Q}(n+1))
and we will also use the notation
Eis_{M}^{n}
to denote the map

Eis_{M}^{n}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}_{M}^{1}(M,\Sym^{n}V_{2}(1)).

Let
\nu
be a finite-order Hecke character, so its archimedean component
\nu_{\infty}
is a unitary character.

Definition 4.42
.

Let
I_{n}(\nu)
be the space of locally constant
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued functions
f
such that for all
a,d\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{\times}
,
\alpha,\delta\in\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
,
b\in\mathbb{A}_{f}
and
g\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
,
the function
f
satisfies that

(4.8)

f(\begin{pmatrix}a\alpha&b\\
&d\delta\\
\end{pmatrix}g)=a^{-1}d^{n+1}\nu(\alpha)f(g),

Thus, the space
I_{n}(\nu)
is endowed with the action of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
by right translation.

Lemma 4.43
.

There is a
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-equivariant decomposition

\mathcal{B}_{n,\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}=\bigoplus_{\nu,\mathrm{sgn}(\nu)=(-1)^{n}}I_{n}(\nu),

where the direct sum is indexed by all finite-order Hecke characters of sign
(-1)^{n}
.

Proof.

The proof follows from the proof of
(
Lem 17
, Lemma 4.3)
. Let
T_{2}
be the diagonal maximal torus of
\GL_{2}
. We are interested in the action of
T_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{f})
on
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
by left translation. It follows from the decomposition
\mathbb{A}_{f}^{\times}=\mathbb{Q}^{\times}_{+}\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
and equation (
4.7
) that we only need to consider the action of
T_{2}(\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times})
. Since each function in
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
is locally constant, it is fixed by an compact open subgroup of
\GL_{2}(\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times})
. Hence,
\mathcal{B}_{n,\bar{\mathbb{Q}}}
can be decomposed into a direct sum of finite-dimensional
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector spaces that are stable under the action of
T_{2}(\hat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times})
. For each of the finite-dimensional vector spaces
V
,
V
is a direct sum of finite order Hecke characters

\chi\colon\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\delta\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\nu(\alpha)\chi(\delta)

because the action is continuous.
By equation (
4.8
), we can see that
\chi
is trivial. Finally, if we plug
\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}
into equation (
4.7
), then we get that
\nu
has to have sign
(-1)^{n}
.
∎

4.4.2.
Motivic classes and their image under the Beilinson regulator

Using the Eisenstein symbol, we will give the construction of motivic classes, which has been done in
(
LSZ 22
, Definition 9.2.3)
and compute
their Hodge realizations.

Construction 4.44
.

We compose the Eisenstein symbol with the pullback

p^{*}\colon\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}},\mathrm{Sym}^{n}V_{2}(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(M,W(1))

and the Gysin map

\iota_{*}\colon\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(M,W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))

to get the map

\mathcal{E}is^{n}_{M}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2)).

Hence, for any
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, we a have motivic class
\mathcal{E}is_{M}^{n}(\phi_{f})\in\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
. In summary, we have the following diagram
13
13

13

Our construction is the Weil restriction to
\mathbb{Q}
of the construction over
E
in
(
LSZ 22
, Definition 9.2.3.)
and it is more convenient for Beilinson’s conjectures.

where we use the subscript
M
and
H
to distinguish maps of motivic cohomology and absolute Hodge cohomology.
Hence, the image of the motivic classes
\mathcal{E}is_{M}^{n}(\phi_{f})
under the Beilinson regulator
r_{H}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}is_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})

\displaystyle=r_{H}(\iota_{M,*}\circ p_{M}^{*}\circ Eis_{M}^{n}(\phi_{f}))

\displaystyle=\iota_{H,*}\circ p_{H}^{*}\circ Eis_{H}^{n}(r_{H}(\phi_{f})).

Convention 4.45
.

In the rest of the paper, when the context is clear, we will ignore the subscript
M
and
H
, and only write
\iota_{*}
,
p^{*}
and
Eis^{n}
.

5.
The vanishing on the boundary of absolute Hodge cohomolgy

5.1.
The Baily–Borel compactification of Picard modular surfaces

In this subsection, we recall the basics of the The Baily–Borel compactification of Picard modular surfaces.

The boundary of the Baily-Borel compactification of a Shimura variety is stratified by Shimura varieties associated to standard (containing the Borel) admissible parabolic subgroups
(
Pin 90
, 4.5 Definition)
of the underlying reductive group
G
.

Let us recall the structure of standard admissible parabolic subgroups in the cases that we are interested in.

•

The reductive group is
H
(Definition
3.2
): Recall that
H
is
\GL_{2}\boxtimes E^{\times}
14
14

14

In the following sections, we use
E^{\times}
to denote
\mathrm{Res}_{E/\mathbb{Q}}\Gm
.
, where the notation
\boxtimes
denotes a pair with the same norm. If we denote by
B_{2}=T_{2}N^{{}^{\prime}}
the standard Borel of
\GL_{2}
together with its Levi decomposition, then the standard admissible parabolic subgroups
Q^{\prime}
of
H
are isomorphic to
B_{2}\boxtimes E^{\times}
, whose Levi decomposition is denoted as
Q^{\prime}=M^{\prime}N^{\prime}
. The canonical normal subgroup
P_{1}^{\prime}
of
Q^{\prime}
defined in
(
Pin 90
, 4.7)
is

P_{1}^{\prime}=\{\begin{pmatrix}*&*\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\}\boxtimes E^{\times}\subseteq H,

whose Levi is

M_{1}^{\prime}=\{\begin{pmatrix}*&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\}\boxtimes E^{\times}\subseteq H.

•

The reductive group is
G
(Definition
3.2
): The standard admissible parabolic subgroups
Q
of
G
is isomorphic to the standard Borel
B=\begin{pmatrix}*&*&*\\
0&*&*\\
0&0&*\end{pmatrix}
of
G
, whose Levi decomposition is denoted by
Q=MN
(see
(
Anc 17
, Proposition 3.3)
). The canonical normal subgroup
P_{1}
of
Q
defined in
(
Pin 90
, 4.7)
is

P_{1}=\{\begin{pmatrix}*&*&*\\
0&*&*\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\}\subseteq G,

whose Levi is

M_{1}=\{\begin{pmatrix}*&0&0\\
0&*&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\}\subseteq G

(see
(
Anc 17
, Lemma 3.8)
).

For each of the two Shimura varieties
X=\{M,S\}
, we denote by
X^{*}
its Baily–Borel compactification and let
\partial{X}=X^{*}-X
be the cusps of the compactification. Then we have the commutative diagram.

and

and

and

\iota

15

For this to hold, we need to choose level structures carefully.
. We also have

\dim\partial S=0,\,\dim\partial M=0.

5.2.
Degeneration of Hodge structures

In this subsection, we compute the degeneration of Hodge structures over Shimura varieties
M
and
S
, which will be used in the proof of vanishing on the boundary.

Notation 5.1
.

In the remaining part of this section, we use
\mu
(instead of
\mu_{H}
) to denote the tensor functor that associates an algebraic representation of a reductive group to the corresponding variation of Hodge structure on the corresponding Shimura variety.

5.2.1.
The theorem of Burgos-Wildehaus and Kostant

We first recall the theorem of Burgos-Wildehaus in the setting of
M
and
S
.

Theorem 5.2

(
BW04b
, Theorem 2.6, 2.9)
.

Let
E
(resp.,
E^{\prime}
) be an algebraic representation of
G
(resp.,
H
). In the derived category
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S))
(resp.,
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(M))
), we have

\displaystyle i^{*}j_{*}\mu(E)\cong\bigoplus_{n}\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(E)[-n]

\displaystyle(\text{resp.,}\,

\displaystyle i^{\prime,*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(E^{\prime})\cong\bigoplus_{n}\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{\prime,*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(E^{\prime})[-n]).

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(E)\cong\mu(\mathrm{H}^{n+2}(N,E))

\displaystyle(\text{resp.,}\,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{\prime,*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(E^{\prime})\cong\mu(\mathrm{H}^{n+1}(N^{\prime},E^{\prime})).

Remark 5.3
.

(1)

The group
G_{1}
(resp.,
G_{1}^{\prime}
) acts on the group cohomology of the unipotent group
\mathrm{H}^{q}(N,E)
(resp.,
\mathrm{H}^{q}(N^{\prime},E^{\prime})
) via its action both on
N
(resp.,
N^{\prime}
) and
E
(resp.,
E^{\prime}
), so the second isomorphism can be viewed an isomorphism of representations of
G_{1}
(resp.,
G_{1}^{\prime}
).

(2)

The theorem of Burgos-Wildehaus and Kostant works for general Shimura varieties.

Now, let us introduce the necessary notations for Kostant’s theorem.

Notation 5.4
.

(1)

Recall that for any unipotent group
N
and any representation
E
of
N
, we have
\mathrm{H}^{q}(N,E)=\mathrm{H}^{q}(\lien,E)
where
\lien
is the Lie algebra of
N
and the right hand side is Lie algebra cohomology.

(2)

Let
Q
be a parabolic subgroup of a reductive group
G
with Levi decomposition
Q=MN
, and
\lieq=\lien\oplus\liem
where
\lieq=\mathrm{Lie}(Q)
,
\lien=\mathrm{Lie}(N)
and
\liem=\mathrm{Lie}(M)
. Let
\lieh
be the Cartan subalgebra of
\lieg=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
corresponding to a fixed Borel and
\mathrm{R}^{+}(\lieg,\lieh)
be the set of positive roots. We also write
\mathrm{R}(\lien,\lieh)
as the subset of
\mathrm{R}^{+}(\lieg,\lieh)
appearing in
\lien
. Denote by
\rho
the half-sum of positive roots, and by
\mathrm{W}(\lieg,\lieh)
the Weyl group. For any
w\in W(\lieg,\lieh)
, we write

•

\mathrm{R}^{+}(w)=\{\alpha\in\mathrm{R}^{+}(\lieg,\lieh)|w^{-1}\alpha\notin\mathrm{R}^{+}(\lieg,\lieh)\}
,

•

\mathrm{l}(w)=|\mathrm{R}^{+}(w)|
,

•

\mathrm{W}^{\prime}=\{w\in\mathrm{W}(\lieg,\lieh)|\mathrm{R}^{+}(w)\subset\mathrm{R}(\lien,\lieh)\}
.

Theorem 5.5

(
Vog 81
, Theorem 3.2.3)
.

Let
E_{\lambda}
be an irreducible representation of
\lieg
of highest weight
\lambda
. Then

\mathrm{H}^{q}(\lien,E_{\lambda})=\bigoplus_{\{w\in\mathrm{W}^{{}^{\prime}}|\mathrm{l}(w)=q\}}F_{w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho}

as representations of
G_{1}
, where
F_{\mu}
is an irreducible representation of
\liem
of highest weight
\mu
.

5.2.2.
Degeneration of Hodge structures on
M
and
S

Now we give the explicit description of degeneration of Hodge structures on the Shimura varieties
M
and
S
.

We first consider the case of
M
.

Notation 5.6
.

Let us denote by
\lambda^{\prime}(d_{1},d_{2})
the algebraic representation of

G_{1}^{\prime}=\{(x;z)\in\Gm\boxtimes E^{\times}|x=z\bar{z}\}

with (highest) weight
(x;z)\mapsto z^{d_{1}}\bar{z}^{d_{2}}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Consider the diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 8.39583pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.39583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.18661pt\raise 6.57835pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.21725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.39583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.39583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M^{*}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 77.78471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\partial{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.01764pt\raise 5.8978pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.8978pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.78471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

As variations of mixed Hodge structures on
\partial M
, we have

(5.2)

i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(W)\cong\bigoplus_{n=-1}^{0}\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(W)[-n],

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{-1}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(W)=\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(a+b+r+s,a+b+r+s)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(W)=\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(-1,-1)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(W)=0,\ \text{for}\ n<-1\ \text{or}\ n>0.

The Hodge type of the corresponding variation of mixed Hodge structures are listed below:

(5.3)

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|}\hline\cr\text{VMHS}&\text{ Hodge type}\\
\hline\cr\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(a+b+r+s,a+b+r+s))&(-(a+b+r+s),-(a+b+r+s)))\\
\hline\cr\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(-1,-1))&(1,1),\\
\hline\cr\end{array},

where “VMHS” stands for variations of mixed Hodge structures.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
5.2
and Theorem
5.5
that

\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(W)=\begin{cases}\mu(\mathrm{H}^{n+1}(N^{\prime},W))&\text{when}\,n\geq-1,\\
0&\text{when}\,n<-1.\end{cases}

Moreover, in our case when
\mathrm{W}^{\prime}=\mathrm{W}=S_{2}
and
\rho=(\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2};0)
, we have the following table of elements
w\in\mathrm{W}^{\prime}
:

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|}\hline\cr w&\mathrm{l}(w)&w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho\\
\hline\cr 1&0&(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2};d)\mapsto(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2};d)\\
\hline\cr(12)&1&(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2};d)\mapsto(\lambda_{2}-1,\lambda_{1}+1;d)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}.

From this, we can see that

\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}\mu(W)=0,\,\,\text{for}\,\,n>0.

For
W=\Sym^{n}V_{2}
where
n=a+b+r+s
, we get as algebraic representations of
G_{1}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{0}(N^{\prime},W)=\lambda^{\prime}(a+b+r+s,a+b+r+s),

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}(N^{\prime},W)=\lambda^{\prime}(-1,-1).

∎

Remark 5.8
.

This computation is essentially the same as
(
Lem 15
, Lemma 4.4)
.

Now, let us consider the case of
S
.

Notation 5.9
.

Let us denote by
\lambda(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};d)
the algebraic representation of

G_{1}=\{\begin{pmatrix}x&&\\
&z&\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}\}\subseteq G

with (highest) weight
x^{\mu_{1}}z^{\mu_{2}}(z\bar{z})^{d}
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{S^{*}}
\textstyle{\partial{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{i}

As variations of mixed Hodge structures on
\partial S
, we have

(5.4)

i^{*}j_{*}\mu(V)\cong\bigoplus_{n=-2}^{1}\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(V)[-n],

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{-2}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(V)

\displaystyle=\mu(\lambda(a+r-s,r-s;2s-r+b)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{-1}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(V)

\displaystyle=\mu(\lambda(r-s-1,a+r-s;2s-r+b))

\displaystyle\oplus\mu(\lambda(a+r-s,r-s-b-1;2s-r+b)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(V)

\displaystyle=\mu(\lambda(r-s-b-2,a+r-s+1;2s-r+b))

\displaystyle\oplus\mu(\lambda(r-s-1,r-s-b-1;2s-r+b)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(V)

\displaystyle=\mu(\lambda(r-s-b-2,r-s;2s-r+b),

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(V)

\displaystyle=0,\ \text{for}\ n<-2\ \text{or}\ n>1.

The Hodge type of the corresponding variation of mixed Hodge structures are listed as follows, where “VMHS” stands for variations of mixed Hodge structures.

(5.5)

VMHS

Hodge type

\mu(\lambda(a+r-s,r-s;2s-r+b))

(-(a+b+r),-(a+b+s))

(-(a+b+s),-(a+b+r))

\mu(\lambda(r-s-1,a+r-s;2s-r+b))

(-(a+b+r),-(b+s-1))

(-(b+s-1),-(a+b+r))

\mu(\lambda(a+r-s,r-s-b-1;2s-r+b))

(-(a+r-1),-(a+b+s))

(-(a+b+s),-(a+r-1))

\mu(\lambda(r-s-b-2,a+r-s+1;2s-r+b))

(-(a+r-1),-(s-2))

(-(s-2),-(a+r-1))

\mu(\lambda(r-s-1,r-s-b-1;2s-r+b))

(-(r-2),-(s+b-1))

(-(s+b-1),-(r-2))

\mu(\lambda(r-s-b-2,r-s;2s-r+b)

(-(r-2),-(s-2))

(-(s-2),-(r-2))

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
5.2
that

\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(V)=\mu(\mathrm{H}^{n+2}(N,V)).

It follows from the fact
\mathrm{R}(\lien,\lieh)=\mathrm{R}^{+}(\lieg,\lieh)
that
\mathrm{W}^{{}^{\prime}}=\mathrm{W}
. By
\rho=(1,0,-1;0)
, we have the following table about elements
w\in\mathrm{W}^{{}^{\prime}}
:

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|}\hline\cr w&\mathrm{l}(w)&w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho\\
\hline\cr 1&0&(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d)\mapsto(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d)\\
\hline\cr(12)&1&(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d)\mapsto(\lambda_{2}-1,\lambda_{1}+1,\lambda_{3};d)\\
\hline\cr(13)&3&(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d)\mapsto(\lambda_{3}-2,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}+2;d)\\
\hline\cr(23)&1&(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d)\mapsto(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}-1,\lambda_{2}+1;d)\\
\hline\cr(132)&2&(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d)\mapsto(\lambda_{3}-2,\lambda_{1}+1,\lambda_{2}+1;d)\\
\hline\cr(123)&2&(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3};d)\mapsto(\lambda_{2}-1,\lambda_{3}-1,\lambda_{1}+2;d)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}.

So if
n<-2
or
n>1
, we have

\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(V)=0.

Therefore, for
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}=V(a+r-s,r-s,r-s-b;2s-r+b)
and
-2\leq n\leq 1
, we get the results listed in the proposition.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

The computation is carried out in
(
Anc 17
, §4)
. Our parametrization is different from
Anc 17
, so we reproduce it here.

5.3.
A vanshing theorem for Betti cohomology

We recall the following vanishing theorem.

Theorem 5.12

(
Sap05b
, Theorem 5)
.

Let
G
be a reductive algebraic group defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and let
D
be the associated symmetric space, which can be written as
D=G(\mathbb{R})/K_{\infty}A_{G}
, where
K_{\infty}
is a maximal compact of
G(\mathbb{R})
and
A_{G}
is the identity component of
\mathbb{R}
-points of a maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-split torus in the center of
G
. Let
\Gamma\subset G(\mathbb{Q})
be a neat arithmetic subgroup,
X=\Gamma\backslash D
be the associated locally symmetric space and
E
be the local system on
X
associated to an irreducible algebraic representation of
G
.

If
D
is a Hermitian or equal-rank symmetric space and the highest weight of
E
is regular, then
\mathrm{H}^{i}(X,E)=0
for all
i<\frac{1}{2}\dim X
, where
\dim X
is the real dimension of the locally symmetric space
X
.

Remark 5.13
.

This result is announced without a detailed proof in
Sap05b
and proved in the preprint
Sap05a
.

5.4.
Vanishing on the boundary for absolute Hodge cohomology

In this subsection, we state and prove the theorem of vanishing on the boundary for absolute Hodge cohomology.

Convention 5.14
.

•

In this subsection, Betti cohomology, compactly supported cohomology and interior cohomology all use
\mathbb{R}
-coefficients. We will not stress this in the notation used for cohomology.

•

For Betti and compactly supported cohomology, we do not write the subscript
B
but for interior cohomology, we write the subscript
B
.

•

In this subsection, we ignore the notation
\mu
and write
V
(resp.,
W
) instead of
\mu(V)
(resp.
\mu(W)
).

5.4.1.
The theorem

We first give the statement of the theorem.

Theorem 5.15
.

Under the conditions

(1)

0\leq-r\leq a
and
0\leq-s\leq b
,

(2)

a>0
and
b>0
,

(3)

r\neq 0
or
s\neq 0
,

the map
\mathcal{E}is_{H}^{n}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n,\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))
factors through the inclusion

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbf{1},\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2)))\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2)),

where
\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is the abelian category of mixed
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge structures and
{\mathbf{1}}
denotes the trivial Hodge structure that is the unit of
\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
.

5.4.2.
The proof the theorem

We give a proof of Theorem
5.15
here and the proof is divided into three steps.

Step I.
In the first step, the goal is to prove the exactness of the sequence in Proposition
5.17
.

Lemma 5.16
.

We have

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)))=0,

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{1}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)))=0,

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{3}(S,V(2)))=0.

Proof.

First, by equation (
5.4
) and
\dim\partial S=0
, we have

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)))=\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)),

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{1}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)))=\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathcal{H}^{1}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).

By Table (
5.5
) in Lemma
5.10
, we can see that there is no part with Hodge type
(0,0)
contained in
\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)
and
\mathcal{H}^{1}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)))=0,

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{1}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)))=0.

Second, by the exact sequence in equation (
4.4
) and Theorem
5.12
, we have

\mathrm{H}^{3}(S,V(2))\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2))).

Hence, we conclude that

\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{3}(S,V(2)))=0.

∎

Proposition 5.17
.

We have the following exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbf{1},\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2)))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2)),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{S}^{H}}

where
i^{*}
and
j_{*}
denote the pullback and pushforward of the mixed Hodge module.

Proof.

Apply the choices
U=S
,
c=2
,
Y=\partial S
and
M=V(2)
in the exact sequence in equation (
4.4
), to get the exact sequence

(5.6)

0\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{c}^{2}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{2}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{c}^{3}(S,V(2)).

By Poincaré duality, we have

\mathrm{H}_{c}^{3}(S,V(2))\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}(S,V^{\vee}(-2))=0,

where the last equality holds since
V^{\vee}
is regular. So we have the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{B,!}^{2}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{2}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2))\rightarrow 0.

Apply the functor
\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),-)
to this short exact sequence to get the exact sequence

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}_{B,!}^{2}(S,V(2)))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{2}(S,V(2)))\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).

Since
\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)))=0
by Lemma
5.16
, we have the following exact sequence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}_{B,!}^{2}(S,V(2)))

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{2}(S,V(2)))\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).

Also by Lemma
5.16
and the exact sequence (
4.5
), we get

(5.7)

\mathrm{H}_{H}^{3}(S,V(2))\cong\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{2}(S,V(2)))

and

(5.8)

\mathrm{H}_{H}^{1}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2))\cong\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)),

so we get the desired exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}_{B,!}^{2}(S,V(2)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}_{H}^{3}(S,V(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}_{H}^{1}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).}

∎

Step II.
In the second step, the goal is to prove the diagram in Proposition
5.18
commutes.

where
\iota_{*}^{H}
is the Gysin morphism defined in Proposition
4.32
,
\partial_{M}^{H}
and
\partial_{S}^{H}
are the corresponding boundary maps and the map
\theta^{H}
is induced from the map

(5.9)

(\partial\iota_{*}^{H}\colon q_{*}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)\rightarrow i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1])\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial S/\mathbb{R})),

which is degenerated from
\iota^{H}_{*}
.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
4.32
that the Gysin morphism
\iota_{*}\colon\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(M,W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))
is induced by

(\iota_{*}W(1)\rightarrow V(2)[1])\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R})).

Apply the functor
j_{*}
to the morphism, and by the fact that
j_{*}\iota_{*}W(1)=p_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)
, we get the map

(p_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)\rightarrow j_{*}V(2)[1])\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{*}/\mathbb{R})).

Then we apply the functor
i_{*}i^{*}
to the morphism and have a morphism

(i_{*}i^{*}p_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1])\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{*}/\mathbb{R})).

From the equation
i^{*}p_{*}=q_{*}i^{\prime*}
which follows from the proper base change theorem of mixed Hodge modules for the proper map
p
, we get the following map

(i_{*}q_{*}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1])\in\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{*}/\mathbb{R})).

By functoriality of the natural transformation
\mathrm{id}\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}
from adjunction, we can see the following commutative diagram in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{*}/\mathbb{R}))
:

Remark 5.19
.

From the construction, the boundary map
\theta_{S}^{H}
in Proposition
5.18
is the same as the
\theta_{S}^{H}
in Proposition
5.17
. Hence, we use the same notation to for them.

Step III.
In the third step, the goal is to prove the map
\theta^{H}_{S}
in Proposition
5.18
is zero, which is stated in Proposition
5.26
.

We first recall some notation.

Recall that the group homomorphism underlying the morphism between the boundary components
q\colon\partial M\rightarrow\partial S
is the map

(5.10)

\hbox to142.65pt{\vbox to52.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 71.32526pt\lower-26.20335pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

•

We denote by
\lambda(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};d)
the algebraic representation of
M_{1}
with weight

(5.11)

\begin{pmatrix}x&&\\
&z&\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}\mapsto x^{\mu_{1}}z^{\mu_{2}}(z\bar{z})^{d},

and we use
\lambda^{\prime}(d_{1},d_{2})
to represent the algebraic representation of
M_{1}^{\prime}
with weight
(x;z)\mapsto z^{d_{1}}\bar{z}^{d_{2}}
.

•

We let
n=a+b+r+s
.

Lemma 5.21
.

The source
\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))
of the map
\theta^{H}_{S}
is isomorphic to

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)).

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
5.7
that

i^{*}j_{*}\mu(W)\cong\bigoplus_{n=-1}^{0}\mathcal{H}^{n}i^{*}j_{*}\mu(W)[-n].

Hence, we have

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))\cong\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))\oplus\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{M},\mathcal{H}^{-1}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)).

We can see

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{M},\mathcal{H}^{-1}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))=0

from the fact that
\mathcal{H}^{-1}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)
is regular and
\dim\partial M=0
.
∎

Remark 5.22
.

From Lemma , we can see that it suffices to prove the map

\theta_{H}\colon\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2))

is zero.

Lemma 5.23
.

We have the following pullback formulas in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))
:

\displaystyle q^{*}\mathcal{H}^{-2}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)=\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(a+b+r+2,a+b+s+2))[1],

\displaystyle q^{*}\mathcal{H}^{-1}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)=\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(a+b+r+2,b+s+1))[1]\oplus\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(a+r+1,a+b+s+2))[1],

\displaystyle q^{*}\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)=\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(a+r+1,s))[1]\oplus\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(r,b+s+1))[1],

\displaystyle q^{*}\mathcal{H}^{1}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)=\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(r,s))[1].

Proof.

First, it follows from
(
Blo 06
, Proposition 2.3.12)
that for
-2\leq l\leq 1
, we have

q^{*}\mathcal{H}^{l}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)=(q^{s})^{*}\mathcal{H}^{l}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1],

where
(q^{s})^{*}
is the pullback functor in the category of variation of Hodge structures. The desired formulas are now
direct consequences of Lemma
5.10
and equation (
5.10
).
∎

Lemma 5.24
.

For
-2\leq l\leq 0
, we have the following vanishing:

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}W(1),q^{*}\mathcal{H}^{l}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1-l])=0.

Proof.

From Lemma
5.7
and Lemma
5.23
, there exist
X,Y\in\mathrm{Var}_{\mathbb{R}}(M)
such that

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}W(1),q^{*}\mathcal{H}^{l}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1-l])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))}(X,Y[2-l]).

From the geometry of
\partial M
and the definition of the derived category,

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))}(X,Y[2-l])\cong\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+})}(X,Y[2-l])\cong\Ext^{2-l}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(X,Y).

Then the lemma follows from the fact that the cohomological dimension of the category
\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}
is
1
.
∎

Lemma 5.25
.

We have the following vanishing result:

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}W(1),q^{*}\mathcal{H}^{-1}i^{*}j_{*}V(2))=0.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
5.7
and Lemma
5.23
that

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}W(1),q^{*}\mathcal{H}^{1}i^{*}j_{*}V(2))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))}(\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(0,0)),\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(r,s))[1])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+})}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(r,s))[1])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(r,s))).

The weight of
\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(r,s))
is greater than zero, so by
(
HW 98
, Theorem A.2.10)
,

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mu(\lambda^{\prime}(r,s)))=0.

∎

Proposition 5.26
.

The map
\theta^{H}
in Proposition
5.18
is zero.

Proof.

From Proposition
5.18
and Lemma
5.22
, in order to prove
\theta^{H}_{S}=0
it suffices to prove

\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial S/\mathbb{R}))}(q_{*}\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1),i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1])=0.

Since
q\colon\partial M\rightarrow\partial S
is a morphism from a
0
-dimensional scheme to a
0
-dimensional scheme, it is both an open immersion and a closed immersion. Therefore, we have

q_{!}=q_{*},q^{!}=q^{*},

from which we get the following isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial S/\mathbb{R}))}(q_{*}\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}W(1),i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1])

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial S/\mathbb{R}))}(q_{!}\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}W(1),i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1])

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}W(1),q^{!}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1])

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\partial M/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j_{*}^{\prime}W(1),q^{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[1]),

where the second isomorphism comes from the adjunction between
q_{!}
and
q^{!}
.
It follows from Lemma
5.10
that

i^{*}j_{*}V(2)=\bigoplus_{l=-2}^{1}\mathcal{H}^{l}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[-l].

Finally, by Lemma
5.24
and Lemma
5.25
, we complete the proof of the proposition.
∎

The following corollary follows directly from Proposition
5.26
and Lemma
5.22
.

Corollary 5.27
.

For any

f\in\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial M,i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))=\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{*}/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathbb{R}(0)_{S^{*}},i_{*}q_{*}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)[2]),

the element

i_{*}(\partial\iota_{*}^{H})\circ f\in\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{*}/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathbb{R}(0)_{S^{*}},i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[3])=\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2))

is
0
.

Remark 5.28
.

Although we have proved that the map

\theta^{H}_{S}\colon\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2))

is zero, the source and the target may not be zero for the following reasons.

•

It can be seen that

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))=\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial M,\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))).

Then by Lemma
5.7
, we get

\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))=\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathbb{R}(0))=\mathbb{R}.

Hence, we have shown that
\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial{M},i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))\neq 0
.

•

It follows from Lemma
5.10
that

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2))=\bigoplus_{l=-2}^{1}\mathrm{H}^{-l+1}_{H}(\partial{S},\mathcal{H}^{i}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).

We can see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{S},\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}V(2))=

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+})}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}V(2))[1])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{0}(\partial S,\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),H_{1})\oplus\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),H_{2}),

where
H_{1}\in\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
has Hodge type
(-(a+r+1),-s)
and
(-s,-(a+r+1))
, and
H_{2}\in\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
has Hodge type
(-r,-(b+s+1))
and
(-(b+s+1),-r)
.
It follows from
(
HW 98
, Theorem A.2.10.)
that if
a+r+s>0
, then
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),H_{1})\neq 0
,
and if
b+r+s>0
, then
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),H_{2})\neq 0
.
Hence, we conclude that when
V
is sufficiently regular,
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial{S},\mathcal{H}^{0}i^{*}j_{*}V(2))\neq 0
.

Finally, by combining Proposition
5.17
, Proposition
5.18
and Proposition
5.26
, we complete the proof of Theorem
5.15
.

6.
The vanishing on the boundary of motivic cohomology

6.1.
Weight structures

6.1.1.
Abstract weight structure

First we collect some definitions and facts about weight structrue on an abstract triangulated category. We mainly follow
(
Wil 09
, §1)
.

Definition 6.1

(Weight structure)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a triangulated category. A
weight structure
on
\mathcal{C}
is a pair
w=(\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0},\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0})
of full-subcategories of
\mathcal{C}
such that if we put

\mathcal{C}_{w\leq n}:=\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}[n],\,\,\,\mathcal{C}_{w\geq n}:=\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}[n]

for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following conditions are satisfied.

(1)

The categories
\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}
are Karoubi-closed: for any object
M
of
\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}
), any direct sum of
M
formed in
\mathcal{C}
is an object of
\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}
).

(2)

(Semi-invariance with respect to shifts) We have the following inclusions:

\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}\subset\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 1},\,\,\,\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}\supset\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 1}

of full sub-categories of
\mathcal{C}
.

(3)

(Orthogonality) For any pair of objects
M\in\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}
and
N\in\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 1}
, we have

\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)=0.

(4)

(Weight filtration) For any object
M\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists an exact triangle

A\rightarrow M\rightarrow B\rightarrow A[1]

in
\mathcal{C}
such that
A\in\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}
and
B\in\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 1}
. We shall refer to any such exact triangle as a weight filtration of
M
.

Remark 6.2
.

•

By (2), we have

\mathcal{C}_{w\leq n}\subset\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}

for any negative integer
n
, and

\mathcal{C}_{w\geq n}\subset\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}

for any postive integer
n
. There are analogues of the other conditins for all the categories
\mathcal{C}_{w\leq n}
and
\mathcal{C}_{w\geq n}
. In particular, they are all Karoubi-closed, and for any object
M\in\mathcal{C}
, there is an exact triangle

A\rightarrow M\rightarrow B\rightarrow A[1]

in
\mathcal{C}
such that
A\in\mathcal{C}_{w\leq n}
and
B\in\mathcal{C}_{w\geq n+1}
.

•

In (4), “the” weight filtration is not assumed to be unique.

•

Recall that on a triangulated category
\mathcal{C}
, there is a notion of
t-structure

(
BBD 82
, Déf. 1.3.1)
. It consists of a pair
t=(\mathcal{C}^{t\leq 0},\mathcal{C}^{t\geq 0})
of full sub-categories satisfying formal analogues of conditions (2)-(4), but putting

\mathcal{C}^{t\leq n}:=\mathcal{C}^{t\leq 0}[-n],\,\,\,\mathcal{C}^{t\geq n}:=\mathcal{C}^{t\geq 0}[-n],

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that in the context of
t
-structures, the analogues of (4) is unique up to isomorphism, and the analogue of (1) is implied by the other conditions.

Definition 6.3

(Heart)
.

(
Bon 10
, Definition 1.2.1)

Let
w=(\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0},\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0})
be a weight structure on
\mathcal{C}
. The
heart
of
w
is the full additive subcategory
\mathcal{C}_{w=0}
of
\mathcal{C}
whose objects lie both in
\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}
and in
\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}
.

Definition 6.4

(Weight exact)
.

(
Bon 10
, Definition 4.4.4)

Let
F\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be a functor between two triangulated categories with weight structures
(\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0},\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0})
and
(\mathcal{D}_{w\leq 0},\mathcal{D}_{w\geq 0})
. The functor
F
is called weight exact if
F
maps
\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}

to
\mathcal{D}_{w\leq 0}
and maps
\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}
to
\mathcal{D}_{w\geq 0}
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
w=(\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0},\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0})
be a weight structure on
\mathcal{C}
. There is an exact triangle

L\rightarrow M\rightarrow N\rightarrow L[1]

in
\mathcal{C}
.

(1)

If both
L
and
N
belong to
\mathcal{C}_{w\leq 0}
, then so does
M
.

(2)

If both
L
and
N
belong to
\mathcal{C}_{w\geq 0}
, then so does
M
.

Proof.

See
(
Bon 10
, Proposition 1.3.3.3)
.
∎

From now on, we consider a fixed weight structure
w
on a triangulated category
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
M\in\mathcal{C}
and
m\leq n
be two integers. A
weight filtration of
M
avoiding weights
m,m+1,\ldots,n-1,n
is an
exact triangle

M_{w\leq m-1}\rightarrow M\rightarrow M_{\geq n+1}\rightarrow M_{w\leq m-1}[1]

in
\mathcal{C}
, with
M_{w\leq m-1}\in\mathcal{C}_{w\leq m-1}
and
M_{\geq n+1}\in\mathcal{C}_{\geq n+1}
.

Proposition 6.7
.

Assume that
m\leq n
, and that
M,N\in\mathcal{C}
admit weight filtrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{w\leq m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\geq n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M_{w\leq m-1}[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{+}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{w\leq m-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{\geq n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{N_{w\leq m-1}[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{+}}

avoiding weights
m,\ldots,n
. Then any morphism
M\rightarrow N
in
\mathcal{C}
extends uniquely to a morphism of exact triangles:

\hbox to278.54pt{\vbox to54.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 139.26988pt\lower-26.8177pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-139.26988pt}{-20.3597pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

Corollary 6.8
.

Assume that
m\leq n
. If
M\in\mathcal{C}
admits a weight filtration avoiding weights
m,\ldots,n
, then it is unique up to a unique isomorphism.

Definition 6.9
.

Assume that
m\leq n
. We say that
M\in\mathcal{C}
does not have weights
m,\cdots n
or
M
is without weights
m\ldots n
, if it admits a weight filtration avoiding
m,\ldots,n
.

6.1.2.
Motivic weight structure

We recall Hébert’s construction of motivic weight structure by applying the abstract machine to the triangulated category of constructible Beilinson motives.

Theorem 6.10

(
(
H1́ 1
, Théorème 3.3)
)
.

Let
S\in\mathrm{Sch}(\mathbb{Q})
be an excellent scheme.
Set

\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)\supset\mathcal{H}_{S}:=\{f_{*}1_{X}(n)[2n]|n\in\mathbb{Z},f\colon X\rightarrow S\,\text{is proper with regular source $X$}\}.

There exists a unique weight structure
w=(\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)_{w\leq 0},\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)_{w\geq 0})
on
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)
such that

\mathcal{H}_{S}\subset\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)_{w=0}.

Remark 6.11
.

•

If
S=\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
, the weight structure

w=(\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q})_{w\leq 0},\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q})_{w\geq 0})

is constructed by Bondarko in
(
Bon 10
, Proposition 6.5.3)
.

•

The existence of this weight structure was also proved independently by Bondarko in
Bon 14
.

•

It can be seen from the definition that
\mathrm{CHM}(S)\subset\mathcal{H}_{S}
.

6.2.
Intersection motives, interior motives and boundary motives

In this subsection, we recall the definition of intersection motives, interior motives and boundary motives of Picard modular surfaces and some of their properties.

Notation 6.12
.

We use the same notation as in section
5
:
for each of the two Shimura varieties
X=\{M,S\}
, we denote by
X^{*}
its Baily–Borel compactification and let
\partial{X}=X^{*}-X
be the cusps of the compactification. Then we have the following commutative diagram:

where
j
and
j^{\prime}
are open immersions,
i
and
i^{\prime}
are closed immersions and
p
and
q
are closed immersions induced by the closed immersion
\iota
. We also have

\dim\partial S=0,\,\dim\partial M=0.

6.2.1.
Boundary motives

Let
g\colon\partial S\rightarrow\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
be the structure morphism of
\partial S
.

Definition 6.13

(Boundary motives)
.

For
V(2)\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
, the boundary motive
\partial M_{gm}(V(2))
is defined as

\partial M_{gm}(V(2)):=(g_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2))^{*}\in\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q}),

where the notation
(\cdot)^{*}
denotes taking dual.

Remark 6.14
.

It can be seen from the proof of
(
Wil 20
, Corollary 3.10)
that the definition of the boundary motive is the same as the one given in
(
Wil 06
, Definition 2.1)
.

Notation 6.15
.

Denote by
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)_{w=0,\partial w\neq 0,1}
the full sub-category of
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)_{w=0}
of objects
N
such that
i^{*}j_{*}N
is without weights
0
and
1
.

Proposition 6.16
.

For
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
and
a>0
and
b>0
, we have

\displaystyle V(2)\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)_{w=0,\partial w\neq 0,1}.

Proof.

First, the
k=\min(k_{1}-k_{2},k_{2})
in
(
Wil 09
, Page 21)
is
\min(a,b)
in our convention, and the condition
k_{1}>k_{2}>0
in
Wil 09
is equivalent to our condition
a>0
and
b>0
. Second, it follows from
(
Wil 15
, Theorem 3.8)
that the boundary motive
\partial M_{gm}(V(2))
is without weights
-1
and
0
. Finally, it can be seen from the fact that
g_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)=g_{!}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)=\partial M_{gm}(V(2))^{*}
and the fact that
g_{!}
is weight exact
(
H1́ 1
, Théorème 3.3(i))
that
i^{*}j_{*}V(2)
is without weights
0
and
1
.
∎

6.2.2.
Intersection motives and interior motives

Let
a\colon S^{*}\rightarrow\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
be the structure morphism of
S^{*}
.

Recall that in
(
Wil 20
, Definition 2.4)
, the author defines the motivic intermediate extension functor

j_{!*}\colon\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S)_{w=0,\partial w\neq 0,1}\rightarrow\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S^{*})_{w=0}.

Definition 6.17
.

(1)

(Intersection motives) For
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
and
a>0
and
b>0
, the
intersection motive
of
S
relative to
S^{*}
is defined as

a_{*}j_{!*}V(2)\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))_{w=0}.

(2)

(Interior motives) For
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
and
a>0
and
b>0
, the
interior motive

\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2))
of
S
is defined as

\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)):=(a_{*}j_{!*}V(2))^{*}\in\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q})_{w=0},

where the notation
(\cdot)^{*}
denotes taking dual.

Remark 6.18
.

•

It follows from
(
Wil 20
, Corollary 3.10(b))
that the definition of interior motives is the same as the one defined in
(
Wil 15
, Corollary 3.9)

•

By
(
Wil 20
, Remark 3.13(a))
, the interior cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))
is the Hodge realization of the interior motive
\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2))
.

Now we recall a few properties of intersection motives.

Proposition 6.19
.

Let
k=\mathrm{min}(a,b)
, which is greater than
0
.

(1)

There are canonical exact triangles in
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))
