\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
m
is the map induced by the first arrow in the exact triangle of Proposition
4.8
, and
\iota_{1}
and
\iota_{2}
are the corresponding maps in (1).

Proof.

The two exact triangles follow from
(
Wil 15
, Corollary 3.9)
and Definition
6.17
, and the commutative diagram of (2) follows from
(
Wil 09
, Theorem 2.4(c))
and Definition
6.17
.
∎

6.3.
Vanishing on the boundary for motivic cohomology

In this subsection, we state the theorem of vanishing on the boundary for motivic cohomology.

Theorem 6.20
.

Under the conditions

(1)

0\leq-r\leq a
and
0\leq-s\leq b
,

(2)

a>0
and
b>0
,

(3)

r\neq 0
or
s\neq 0
,

the map
\mathcal{E}is_{M}^{n}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
factors through the inclusion

\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0))\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2)).

Remark 6.21
.

Using the Beilinson regulator
r_{H}
,
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0))
has image in

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbf{1},\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))).

Hence, Theorem
6.20
can be viewed as a “motivic lifting” of Theorem
5.15
.

6.4.
The proof of motivic vanishing

In this subsection, we prove the theorem of vanishing on the boundary for motivic cohomology.

6.4.1.
Conversativity and weight convervativity

Before going into the steps of the proof, we first recall the conservativity and weight conservativity theorem of the Hodge realization functor over motives of Abelian type.

Theorem 6.22

(Conservativity,
(
Wil 15
, Theorem 1.12.)
)
.

For
M\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}^{Ab}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))
, if

H^{n}R_{H}(M)=0

for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
M=0
.

A direct corollary of the conservativity theorem is the following.

Corollary 6.23
.

For all
M,N\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}^{Ab}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))
, if

\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+})}(R_{H}(M),R_{H}(N))=0,

then

\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))}(M,N)=0.

Theorem 6.24

(Weight conservativity,
(
Wil 15
, Theorem 1.13)
)
.

Let
M\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}^{Ab}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))
and
\alpha\leq\beta
be two integers. We have the following statements:

(1)

M
lies in
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}^{Ab}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))_{w=0}
if and only if
H^{n}R_{H}(M)
is pure of weight
n
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(2)

M
lies in
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}^{Ab}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))_{w\leq\alpha}
if and only if
H^{n}R_{H}(M)
is of weights
\leq n+\alpha
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(3)

M
lies in
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}^{Ab}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))_{w\geq\beta}
if and only if
H^{n}R_{H}(M)
is of weights
\geq n+\beta
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(4)

M
is without weights
\alpha,\alpha+1,\ldots,\beta
if and only if
H^{n}R_{H}(M)
is without weights
n+\alpha,n+(\alpha+1),\ldots,n+\beta
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

6.4.2.
The proof

The proof is parallel to the Hodge case, so it is also divided into three steps.
Recall that we let
a\colon S^{*}\rightarrow\Spec(\mathbb{Q})
be the structure morphism of
S^{*}
.

Step I.
In the first step, the goal is to prove the exactness of the sequence in Proposition
6.28
.

Lemma 6.25
.

We have the following vanishing of motivic cohomology:

\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})},C_{\geq(k+1)}[2])=0,

where
C_{\geq(k+1)}\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))_{w\geq k+1}
is the one in the exact triangle (
6.2
).

Proof.

We first apply the Hodge realization functor
R_{H}
to the above
\Hom
space and get

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{D^{b}}(\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+})}(\mathbb{R}(0),N[2]),

where
N=R_{H}(C_{\geq(k+1)})
.
By the fact that
\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
has cohomological dimension
1
, we have the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathcal{H}^{1}(N))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathrm{D^{b}}(\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+})}(\mathbb{R}(0),N[2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathcal{H}^{2}(N))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

It follows from Theorem
6.24
that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(N)
is of weights
\geq k+2
and
\mathcal{H}^{2}(N)
is of weights
\geq k+3
. Hence, we have
\Hom_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathcal{H}^{2}(N))=0
for weight reasons and
\Ext_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathcal{H}^{1}(N))=0
by
(
HW 98
, Theorem A.2.10)
. Therefore, the following identity holds:

\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+})}(\mathbb{R}(0),N[2])=0.

Finally, the vanishing

\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})},C_{\geq(k+1)}[2])=0

follows from Corollary
6.23
.
∎

Proposition 6.26
.

We have the following exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(S^{*},j_{!*}V(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{S}^{M}}

Proof.

First, if we let
N=j_{*}V(2)
in the exact triangle of Proposition
4.8
and apply the functor
a_{*}
to it, then we get the following exact triangle in
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))
:

a_{*}j_{!}V(2)\rightarrow a_{*}j_{*}V(2)\rightarrow a_{*}i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)\rightarrow a_{*}j_{!}V(2)[1].

We then apply the functor
\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})},-[3])
to it and get the long exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S^{*},j_{!}V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2))\rightarrow\cdots.

Second, if we apply the functor
\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})},-[3])
to the commutative diagram (
6.4
), then we have the following commutative diagram:

where the map

Finally, after applying the functor

\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})},-[3])

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})},C_{\geq(k+1)}[2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(S^{*},j_{!*}V(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{1,*}}

It follows from Lemma
6.25
that
\iota_{1,*}
is injective. Therefore, we get the following exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(S^{*},j_{!*}V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).

∎

Definition 6.27
.

For the interior motive
\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2))\in\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q})
, we define its motivic cohomology
\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0))
as

\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0)):=\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{gm}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0)),

which is isomorphic to

\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(S^{*},j_{!*}V(2))=\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\Spec(\mathbb{Q}))}(1_{\Spec(\mathbb{Q})},a_{*}j_{!*}V(2)[3]).

Hence, we get the following proposition.

Proposition 6.28
.

We have the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}_{M}^{3}(\mathrm{Gr_{0}}M_{gm}(V(2)),\mathbb{Q}(0))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(\partial{S},i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{S}^{M}}

Remark 6.29
.

The exact sequence in Proposition
6.28
is the “motivic lifting” of the exact sequence in Proposition
5.17
.

Step II.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-19.57983pt}{-32.16774pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
\iota_{*}^{M}
is the Gysin morphism defined in Proposition
4.16
,
\partial_{M}^{M}
and
\partial_{S}^{M}
are the corresponding boundary maps and the map
\theta^{M}
is induced from the map,

(6.6)

(\partial\iota_{*}^{M}\colon q_{*}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)[1]\rightarrow i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[3])\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(\partial S),

which is degenerated from
\iota^{M}_{*}
.

Proof.

The proof is parallel to the proof of Proposition
5.18
. First, recall that we have the following diagram:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.95952pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.39583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 13.93661pt\raise 8.68945pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-4.32835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j^{{}^{\prime}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.39583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.95952pt\raise-15.72221pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.11111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.39583pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.96793pt\raise-14.77928pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.09027pt\raise-24.05856pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 77.78471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\partial{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.76764pt\raise 8.00891pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-5.00891pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i^{{}^{\prime}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.78471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.91872pt\raise-14.74998pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 88.83505pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.35416pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.53905pt\raise-25.4479pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63542pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.4375pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.4375pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S^{*}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 79.82639pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\partial{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.37007pt\raise-26.12846pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.31596pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.74304pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

It follows from Proposition
4.16
that the Gysin morphism
\iota_{*}:\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(M,W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
is induced by

(\iota_{*}W(1)[1]\rightarrow V(2)[3])\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S).

Second, after applying the functor
j_{*}
to the morphism and by the fact that
j_{*}\iota_{*}=p_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}
,
we have the morphism

(6.7)

(p_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)[1]\rightarrow j_{*}V(2)[3])\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S^{*}).

The morphism

(i_{*}i^{*}p_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)[1]\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[3])\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S^{*})

follows by applying the functor
i_{*}i^{*}
to the morphism in (
6.7
). It follows from the proper base change theorem for Beilinson motives
(
CD 19
, Theorem 2.2.14.(4))
and the fact that
p
is proper that
i^{*}p_{*}=q_{*}i^{\prime*}
. From it, we get

(i_{*}q_{*}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)[1]\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[3])\in\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S^{*}).

The natural transformation
\mathrm{id}\rightarrow i_{*}i^{*}
from adjunction gives us the following commutative diagram in
\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S^{*})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}q_{*}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}V(2)[3]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[3].}

Finally, we apply the functor
\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S^{*})}(1_{S^{*}},-)
to the above commutative diagram and get the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(M,W(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(\partial M,i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2)).}

∎

Remark 6.31
.

•

From the construction, we can see the boundary map
\partial_{S}^{M}
in Proposition
6.30
is the same as the
\partial_{S}^{H}
in Proposition
6.28
. Hence, we use the same notation for them.

•

The commutative diagram in Proposition
6.30
is the “motivic lifting” of the commutative diagram in Proposition
5.18
.

Step III.
In the third step, the goal is to prove the map
\theta^{M}
in Proposition
6.30
is zero, which is stated in Proposition
6.32
.

Proposition 6.32
.

The map
\theta^{M}
in Proposition
6.30
is zero.

Proof.

First, it follows from Proposition
6.30
that in order to prove
\theta^{M}=0
, it suffices to prove the following statement:
for any

f\in\mathrm{H}^{1}_{M}(\partial M,i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))=\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S^{*})}(1_{S^{*}},i_{*}q_{*}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)[1]),

the element

i_{*}(\partial\iota_{*}^{M})\circ f\in\Hom_{\mathrm{DM}_{B,c}(S^{*})}(1_{S^{*}},i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[3])=\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2))

is
0
.

Second, it follows from the identity
R_{H}(i_{*}(\partial\iota_{*}^{M})\circ f)=i_{*}(\partial\iota_{*}^{H})\circ R_{H}(f)
in

\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{*}/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathbb{R}(0)_{S^{*}},i_{*}i^{*}j_{*}V(2)[3])=\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(\partial S,i^{*}j_{*}V(2))

where

R_{H}(f)\in\mathrm{H}^{0}_{H}(\partial M,i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1))=\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S^{*}/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathbb{R}(0)_{S^{*}},i_{*}q_{*}i^{\prime*}j^{\prime}_{*}W(1)[2])

and Corollary
5.27
that
R_{H}(i_{*}(\partial\iota_{*}^{M})\circ f)=0
.

Finally, by Theorem
6.22
, we have
i_{*}(\partial\iota_{*}^{M})\circ f=0
.
∎

Finally, by combining Proposition
6.28
, Proposition
6.30
and Proposition
6.32
, we finish the proof of Theorem
6.20
.

Part II
Connection to L-values

7.
Constructing the differential forms and pairing with the motivic classes

Convention 7.1
.

In this section, we let
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}\in\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(V(2))
and
n=a+b+r+s
, and Betti cohomology, compactly supported cohomology and interior cohomology all have
\mathbb{Q}
-coefficients, which is different from subsection
6.3
.

7.1.
The use of Poincaré duality

In this subsection, we will explain how the Poincaré duality pairing can be used to compute the Beilinson regulator.
The idea is due to Beilinson
Bei86a
(see also
Kin 98
).

Let us first recall a general result.

Lemma 7.2
.

(
Lem 17
, Lemma 4.11)

Let
E
be a number field and
M
be an object of
\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R},E}^{+}
with pure weight
w<0
. Let
M_{dR}
be the
E\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbb{R}}
-submodule of
M_{\mathbb{C}}
where the de Rham involution acts trivially and let
M^{-}
be the submodule of
M
where the infinitesimal Frobenius
F_{\infty}
acts by multiplication by
-1
. We write
M^{-}(-1)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}M^{-}
. Then there is an short exact sequence of
E\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbb{R}}
-modules

0\rightarrow F^{0}M_{dR}\rightarrow M^{-}(-1)\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M)\rightarrow 0,

where the first map is the composite of the natural inclusions

F^{0}M_{dR}\rightarrow M_{dR}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{C}}

and of the projections

M_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow M(-1)\rightarrow M^{-}(-1)

defined by
v\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(v-\bar{v})
and
\frac{1}{2}(v-F_{\infty}(v))
, respectively.

We apply Lemma
7.2
to the situation that we are interested in to get the following lemma.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\pi_{f}
be the non-archimedean part of an irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
G
(see Definition
3.2
) whose archimedean component
\pi_{\infty}
belongs to the discrete series
L
-packet
P(V_{\mathbb{C}}(2))
and denote by
\pi_{f}
its rational model over
E(\pi_{f})
(see Theorem
3.37
). Then there is a short exact sequence:

(7.1)

0\rightarrow F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1)\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow 0,

where the second map is defined in Lemma
7.2
.

Let
F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{*}
be the dual of
F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))
. It follows from Lemma
7.3
that the one dimensional
E(\pi_{f})
-vector space

\mathcal{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))=\mathrm{det}_{E(\pi_{f})}F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{*}\otimes_{E(\pi_{f})}\mathrm{det}_{E(\pi_{f})}M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}(-1)

is an
E(\pi_{f})
-structure of the
E(\pi_{f})\otimes\mathbb{R}
-module

\mathrm{det}_{E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}}\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}).

Here
\mathcal{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))
is the Beilinson
E(\pi_{f})
-structure defined in
(
Nek 94
, §6.1)
. We now define the Deligne
E(\pi_{f})
-structure using the Beilinson
E(\pi_{f})
-structure.

Definition 7.4

(Deligne
E(\pi_{f})
-structure)
.

Let
\delta(\pi_{f},V(2))\in(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})^{\times}
be the determinant of the isomorphism
I_{\infty}\colon M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}
computed using the basis defined over
E(\pi_{f})
on both sides. Then the Deligne
E(\pi_{f})
-structure of

\mathrm{det}_{E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}}\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})

is

\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))=(2\pi i)^{\dim_{E(\pi_{f})}M_{B}(\pi_{f},W)^{-}}\delta(\pi_{f},W)^{-1}\mathcal{B}(\pi_{f},V(2)).

Remark 7.5
.

This definition does not depend on the choice of the bases.

Lemma 7.6
.

It can be seen that
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})
is a rank one
E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
-module.

Proof.

It can be seen that

\mathrm{rank}_{E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}}F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}=2

from the Hodge decomposition of
M(\pi_{f},V(2))
in Corollary
3.42
and the condition of coefficients in equation (
7.3
). By the exact sequence (
7.1
) and

\mathrm{rank}_{E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}}M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1)=3,

we conclude that
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})
is a rank one
E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
-module.
∎

Recall that by Theorem
5.15
, the map
\mathcal{E}is_{H}^{n}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n,\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))
factors through the inclusion

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbf{1},\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2)).

Let
\mathcal{K}(V(2))
be the sub
\mathbb{Q}[G(\mathbb{A}_{f})]
-module of

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})=\varinjlim_{L}\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S(L),V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})

generated by the image of
\mathcal{E}is_{H}^{n}
with domain
\mathcal{B}_{n}
and let
\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))
be defined by

(7.2)

\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2)):=\Hom_{\mathbb{Q}[G(\mathbb{A}_{f})]}(\Res_{E(\pi_{f})/\mathbb{Q}}\pi_{f},\mathcal{K}(V(2)).

This is an
E(\pi_{f})
-submodule of
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})
. Therefore, now we have two
E(\pi_{f})
-submodules of
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})
. The first one, which is called
\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))
, is defined using the comparison between Betti and De Rham cohomology, which is more elementary. The second one, called
\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))
, is defined using the Beilinson regulator
r_{H}
, which is more sophisticated. By definition, we know that

\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))
is non-zero, so we hope to know whether
\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))
is zero or not by a comparision between
\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))
and
\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))
.

Definition 7.7
.

If
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are two elements of
E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbb{C}}
, we write
\mu\sim\mu^{\prime}
if there exists
\lambda\in E(\pi_{f})^{\times}
such that
\mu=\lambda\mu^{\prime}
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
{v}_{D}
be a non-zero vector in
\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))
and
v_{K}
be a non-zero vector in
\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))
. Let
\tilde{v}_{D}
and
\tilde{v}_{K}
be any lifting of
v_{D}
and
v_{K}
in
M_{B}(\pi_{f},V)^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1)
using the last arrow in the exact sequence (
7.1
). Then for any
E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
-linear map
\psi:M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1)\rightarrow E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
which is trivial on
F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))=\frac{\psi(\tilde{v}_{K})}{\psi(\tilde{v}_{D})}\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2)).

Proof.

It follows from the exact sequence (
7.1
).
∎

Our goal is to compute
\psi(\tilde{v}_{K})
and
\psi(\tilde{v}_{D})
seperately for a well-chosen linear functional
\psi
. One natural way to choose
\psi
is to use Poincaré duality. Hence, let us first recall properties of
Poincaré duality for Picard modular surfaces. By previous computations, we assume that

(7.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &0\leq-r\leq a\\
&0\leq-s\leq b\\
&a>0\quad\text{and}\quad b>0\\
&r\neq 0\quad\text{or}\quad s\neq 0\end{aligned}\right.

Recall for a representation
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
, its contragredient representation is
D=V^{*}=V^{b,a}\{-a-b-r,-a-b-r\}
. In other words, we have a perfect pairing
16
16

16

Both
V
and
D
are in
\mathrm{Rep}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G)
.

V\otimes D\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(0).

The pairing induces a
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-equivariant pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}\colon\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))\otimes\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,D)\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{4}_{B,!}(S,\mathbb{Q}(2))\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(0),

where the last map is defined by the trace. The pairing becomes perfect after restriction to the vectors which are invariant by a compact open subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.

Fact 7.9
.

Here,
\mathbb{Q}(0)
is given by an action of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
by
|\mu|^{-2}
.

Proof.

We have

(7.4)

\mathrm{H}^{4}_{B,!}(S,\mathbb{Q}(2))\cong\mathrm{H}^{4}_{B,c}(S,\mathbb{Q}(2))\cong\mathrm{H}^{0}_{B}(S,\mathbb{Q}(-2))\cong\bigoplus(\chi|\mu|^{-2}),

where the sum is over finite Hecke characters
\chi\colon\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{f,\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. The first isomorphism in (
7.4
) comes from Corollary
4.27
(1) and the fact
\dim\partial S=0
; the second isomorphism in (
7.4
) holds by Poincaré duality, and the last isomorphism in (
7.4
) can be deduced from
(
Mil 17
, Theorem 5.17)
. The trace map is a projection onto the factor with
\chi
trivial.
∎

Remark 7.10
.

This fact is similar to
(
Tay 93
, p295)
in the Siegel 3-folds case.

Hence, this induces a
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
-equivariant morphism of Hodge structures
17
17

17

It is a
\mathbb{Q}
-morphism.

(7.5)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}\colon M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))\otimes M_{B}(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)\rightarrow E(\pi_{f})(0).

Recall by Proposition

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
\Omega\in M(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)_{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
. Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\Omega,\cdot\rangle_{B}}

be the morphism defined by the composition of the inclusion
M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1)\rightarrow M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}
and the pairing with
\Omega
. Assume

belongs to

. Then

(7.6)

\mathcal{K}(\pi_{f},V(2))=\frac{\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}}{\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{D}\rangle_{B}}\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))

Proof.

∎

Convention 7.12
.

From now on, we will use
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}
(resp.

) to denote
\mathrm{Sh}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{an}
(resp.,

).

Remark 7.13
.

Let
\mathbf{1}
be the multiplicative identities of
E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
.

(1)

By definition, we have the following isomorphisms

\displaystyle M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}\cong((\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\mathrm{Sh}_{G},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}})[\pi_{f}])\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}),

\displaystyle M_{B}(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)_{\mathbb{C}}\cong((\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\mathrm{Sh}_{G},D)_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}},D))_{\mathbb{C}})[\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2}])\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}),

where the notation
[\cdot]
means the corresponding isotypic subspaces.

(2)

If we could construct a differential form

\omega_{\Psi}=\omega\otimes\Psi_{f}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\mathrm{Sh}_{G},D)_{\mathbb{C}}[\pi_{f}]=\mathrm{H}^{2}(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G};D_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\tilde{\pi}_{\infty})\otimes\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2}

associated to a
\Psi=\Psi_{f}\otimes\Psi_{\infty}

that belongs to a cuspidal automorphic representation
\tilde{\pi}=\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2}\otimes\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
of
G(\mathbb{A})
, then it is natural to let
\Omega\in M(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)_{\mathbb{C}}^{+}
be

\frac{1}{2}(\omega_{\Psi}+\overline{\omega_{\Psi}})\otimes\mathbf{1},

where
\overline{\omega_{\Psi}}\in\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}},D)_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complex conjugate of
\omega_{\Psi}
.

(3)

Since the Eisenstein symbol is defined over
\mathbb{Q}
(see subsubsection
4.4.1
),

\tilde{v}_{K}
can be represented by

(\tilde{v}_{K}^{1},\tilde{v}_{K}^{1})\otimes\mathbf{1},

where the first
\tilde{v}_{K}^{1}
belongs to
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\mathrm{Sh}_{G},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}[\pi_{f}]
and the second
\tilde{v}_{K}^{1}
belongs to

\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}[\pi_{f}].

(4)

The Poincaré duality pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}\colon\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,D)_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(0),

can be decomposed into

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}+\overline{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}},

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}
is the Poincaré duality pairing on
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}
:

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}\colon\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\mathrm{Sh}_{G},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\mathrm{Sh}_{G},D)_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(0),

and its complex conjugate
\overline{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}}
is t Poincaré duality pairing on
\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}
:

\overline{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}}\colon\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(\overline{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}},D)_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(0).

(5)

We can see that

\displaystyle\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\frac{1}{2}(\omega_{\Psi}+\overline{\omega_{\Psi}}),(\tilde{v}_{K}^{1},\tilde{v}_{K}^{1})\rangle_{B}\otimes\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\omega_{\Psi},\tilde{v}_{K}^{1}\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\langle\overline{\omega_{\Psi}},\tilde{v}_{K}^{1}\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}}\otimes\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\omega_{\Psi},\tilde{v}_{K}^{1}\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}\otimes\mathbf{1}.

Therefore, in order to compute the pairing
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}
, it suffices to construct a differential form
\omega_{\Psi}
and compute
\langle\omega_{\Psi},\tilde{v}_{K}^{1}\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}
.

Definition 7.14
.

If
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are two elements of
E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbb{C}}
(resp.,
\mathbb{C}
), we denote
\mu\sim\mu^{\prime}
if there exists
\lambda\in(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}})^{\times}
(resp.,
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
) such that
\mu=\lambda\mu^{\prime}
.

Convention 7.15
.

In this paper, because we can only compute
\langle\omega_{\Psi},\tilde{v}_{K}^{1}\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}
up to
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
, we can freely choose
\tilde{v}_{K}
up to
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}
. In other words, we can use
\mathcal{B}_{n,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
as the domain of
\mathcal{E}is_{H}
.

7.2.
Differential forms

The next step is to explain how to construct a differential form
\omega_{\Psi}
on
S
. It is based on some elementary representation-theoretic considerations.

By equation (
3.11
), Theorem
3.30
and Proposition
3.33
, we have the decomposition

\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,D)_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathrm{H}^{2}_{dR,!}(S,D)_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{\tilde{\pi}=\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}\otimes\tilde{\pi}_{f}}\Hom_{K_{G}}(\wedge^{2}\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}},D_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\tilde{\pi}_{\infty})\otimes\tilde{\pi}_{f}.

Hence, in order to associate a differential form
\omega_{\Psi}=\omega\otimes\Psi_{f}
to a cusp form
\Psi=\Psi_{f}\otimes\Psi_{\infty}
in the cuspidal automorphic representation
\tilde{\pi}=\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2}\otimes\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
, it suffices to associate some

\omega\in\Hom_{K_{G}}(\wedge^{2}\liep_{G,\mathbb{C}},D_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{\infty})

to
\Psi_{\infty}\in\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
. Here
\Psi_{f}
is any vector in
\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2}
, and the choice of
\Psi_{\infty}\in\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
is explained below.

Now we explain the way to contruct
\omega
. Recall that the complexified Lie algebra of
G
can be decomposed as
\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{G,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{+}_{G,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{-}_{G,\mathbb{C}}
.
Let

\displaystyle X_{(1,-1,0)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\ X_{(-1,1,0)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle X_{(1,0,-1)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\ X_{(-1,0,1)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle X_{(0,1,-1)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\ X_{(0,-1,1)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}

be some fixed root vectors in
\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Let
\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
be the representation of
G(\mathbb{R})^{+}
in the discrete series
L
-packet
P(D_{\mathbb{C}})
with Blattner parameter
(1+a+r-s,-1-b+r-s,r-s)

18
18

18

This is the
\pi_{2}
in the talble (
3.12
) in Proposition
3.34
.
.

Proposition 7.16
.

Let
v\in D_{\mathbb{C}}
be a vector of weight
(-a+s-r,b+s-r,s-r;-b-2s+r)
that is the lowest weight vector in the
K_{G}
-type
\tau_{(b+s-r,-a+s-r,s-r)}
of the algebraic representation
D_{\mathbb{C}}
and let
\Psi_{\infty}\in\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
be a vector of weight
(-1-b+r-s,1+a+r-s,r-s;b+2s-r)
that is the lowest weight vector of the minimal
K_{G}
-type
\tau_{(1+a+r-s,-1-b+r+s,r-s)}
of
\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
. Let

X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}\in\liep^{+}_{G,\mathbb{C}}\otimes\liep^{-}_{G,\mathbb{C}}=\tau_{(1,-1,0)}\oplus\tau_{(0,0,0)}

be a highest weight vector of the
K_{G}
-type
\tau_{(1,-1,0)}
. Then there exists a unique non-zero map

\omega\in\Hom_{K_{G}}(\liep^{+}_{G,\mathbb{C}}\otimes\liep^{-}_{G,\mathbb{C}},D_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\tilde{\pi}_{\infty})

such that

(7.7)

\omega(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)})=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}.

Proof.

First, the
K_{G}
-weight of
X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}
is
(1,-1,0)
, which is the weight of
X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}
. Hence, the map
\omega
preserves
K_{G}
-weights.

Second, we have

\displaystyle X_{(1,-1,0)}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i+1}v\otimes X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}+\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{3+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle(-1)\sum\limits_{i=1}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{3+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{3+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle 0.

Therefore, the vector
\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}
is a highest weight vector of
K_{G}
-type
\tau(1,-1,0)
. So the map
\omega
is non-zero and by

\dim\Hom_{K_{G}}(\liep^{+}_{G,\mathbb{C}}\otimes\liep^{-}_{G,\mathbb{C}},D_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes\tilde{\pi}_{\infty})=1,

\omega
is uniquely determined.
∎

Remark 7.17
.

•

Since
\omega
is uniquely determined by the equaiton (
7.7
),
\omega
must satisfy

\omega(X_{(0,0,0)})=0,

for any
X_{(0,0,0)}\in\tau_{(0,0,0)}
.

•

Since
v\in D_{\mathbb{C}}
is a vector of weight
(-a+s-r,b+s-r,s-r;-b-2s+r)
and the highest weight of
D_{\mathbb{C}}
is
(b+s-r,s-r,-a+s-r,-a+s-r;-b-2s+r)
, we can see that
v
is lowest weight vector in
D_{\mathbb{C}}
with respect to the positive root system

\{(1,-1,0),(0,-1,1),(-1,0,1)\},

which guarantees the existence of
v
.
Hence, we have

(7.8)

X_{(1,0,-1)}^{a+b+1}v=0.

7.3.
Restriction of differential forms

Recall that we have the following inclusion of algebraic groups

\iota\colon H\hookrightarrow G,\quad(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix},z)\mapsto(\begin{pmatrix}a&&b\\
&z&\\
c&&d\end{pmatrix},z\bar{z}),

which induces the closed immersion of Shimura varieties

\iota\colon\mathrm{Sh}_{H}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sh}_{G}.

Now, we compute the pullback of the differential form

\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}=\iota^{*}(\omega\otimes\Psi_{f})=\iota^{*}(\omega)\otimes\Psi_{f}

along the map
\iota
. Hence, it suffices to compute
\iota^{*}(\omega)
.

Recall the complexified Lie algebra of
H
is
\lieh_{\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{H,\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}}^{+}\oplus\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}}^{-}
. Let

(7.9)

v^{+}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\in\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}}^{+},\ v^{-}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\liep_{H,\mathbb{C}}^{-}.

be two root vectors in
\liegl_{2,\mathbb{C}}
. So under the tangent map
\iota_{*}
of the map
\iota
of Shimura varieties, we have

\iota_{*}(v^{+})=X_{(1,0,-1)},\ \iota_{*}(v^{-})=X_{(-1,0,1)},

so

\iota_{*}(v^{+}\otimes v^{-})=\iota_{*}v^{+}\otimes\iota_{*}v^{-}=X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(-1,0,1)}.

Lemma 7.18
.

If we set

Y_{(1,-1,0)}:=\mathrm{ad}_{X_{(-1,1,0)}}(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}).

and
Y_{(0,0,0)}
is a vector in
\tau_{(0,0,0)}
, then we have

\iota_{*}(v^{+}\otimes v^{-})=-\frac{1}{2}Y_{(1,-1,0)}+\beta Y_{(0,0,0)}

for some
\beta\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

First, it follows from the fact that
X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}
is a highest weight vector of
\tau_{(1,-1,0)}
as a
K_{G}
-representation that

Y_{(1,-1,0)}
is vector in
\tau_{(1,-1,0)}
with weight
(0,0,0)
. Hence, we could let

\iota_{*}(v^{+}\otimes v^{-})=\alpha Y_{(1,-1,0)}+\beta Y_{(0,0,0)}

for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
, from which we have

(7.10)

\mathrm{ad_{X_{(1,-1,0)}}}(\iota_{*}(v^{+}\otimes v^{-}))=\alpha\mathrm{ad_{X_{(1,-1,0)}}}(\mathrm{ad}_{X_{(-1,1,0)}}(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)})).

Second, we can compute the left hand side of the formula (
7.10
) aså

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad_{X_{(1,-1,0)}}}(\iota_{*}(v^{+}\otimes v^{-}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ad_{X_{(1,-1,0)}}}(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(-1,0,1)})

\displaystyle=X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes\mathrm{ad_{X_{(1,-1,0)}}}(X_{(-1,0,1)})

\displaystyle=X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes[X_{(1,-1,0)},X_{(-1,0,1)}]

\displaystyle=-X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}.

Third, if we let

H:=[X_{(1,-1,0)},X_{(-1,1,0)}]=\left(\begin{matrix}1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&0\end{matrix}\right),

then we have

\displaystyle\alpha\mathrm{ad_{X_{(1,-1,0)}}}(\mathrm{ad}_{X_{(-1,1,0)}}(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}))

\displaystyle=\alpha\mathrm{ad_{X_{(-1,1,0)}}}(\mathrm{ad}_{X_{(1,-1,0)}}(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}))

\displaystyle+\alpha\mathrm{ad_{[X_{(1,-1,0)},X_{(-1,1,0)}]}}(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)})

\displaystyle=0+\alpha\mathrm{ad}_{H}(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)})

\displaystyle=\alpha X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}+\alpha X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}

\displaystyle=2\alpha X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}.

Finally, by equation (
7.10
), we have

-X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}=2\alpha X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}.

Hence, we conclude that
\alpha=-\frac{1}{2}
.

∎

Lemma 7.19
.

For
0\leq i\leq a+b
, we have the following identities:

(1)

X_{(-1,1,0)}X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}=(1+i)(2+a+b-i)X_{(1,-1,0)}^{1+a+b-i}\Psi_{\infty}
,

(2)

X_{(-1,1,0)}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v=\begin{cases}i(a+b+1-i)X^{i-1}_{(1,-1,0)}v&i>0\\
0&i=0\end{cases}.

Proof.

Since
\Psi_{\infty}
is the lowest weight vector with weight
(-1-b+r-s,1+a+r-s,r-s;b+2s-r)
in the minimal
K_{G}
-type
\tau_{(1+a+r-s,-1-b+r-s,r-s)}
of
\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
, which is the vector
\nu_{0}
in the representation
\tau_{(1+a+r-s,-1-b+r-s,r-s)}
in our setup of representations of
K_{G}
(see equation (
3.5
)),
we have

\displaystyle X_{(-1,1,0)}X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}

\displaystyle=X_{(-1,1,0)}X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\nu_{0}

\displaystyle=(2+a+b-i)!X_{(-1,1,0)}\nu_{2+a+b-i}.

\displaystyle=(1+i)(2+a+b-i)!\nu_{1+a+b-i}.

We also have

X^{1+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}=(1+a+b-i)!\nu_{1+a+b-i}.

Hence, we get

X_{(-1,1,0)}X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}=(1+i)(2+a+b-i)X^{1+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}.

The computation of
X_{(-1,1,0)}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v
is similar, which we finishes the proof of the lemma.

∎

Proposition 7.20
.

If we pullback the
\omega
defined in Proposition
7.16
along the map
\iota
, then we have the following formula:

\iota^{*}\omega(v_{+}\otimes v_{-})=-\sum_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}(i+1)X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{1+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}.

Proof.

The pullback functor
\iota^{*}
preserves the type of the differential form and the type of a differential form on a Shimura variety is determined by the archimedean part,
\iota^{*}\omega
is determined by its value on
v^{+}\otimes v^{-}
.
It follows from Lemma
7.18
that

\displaystyle\iota^{*}\omega(v^{+}\otimes v^{-})

\displaystyle=\omega(\iota_{*}(v^{+}\otimes v^{-}))

\displaystyle=\omega(-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{ad}_{X_{(-1,1,0)}}(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}X_{(-1,1,0)}(\omega(X_{(1,0,-1)}\otimes X_{(0,-1,1)}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}X_{(-1,1,0)}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(-1,1,0)}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X_{(-1,1,0)}X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}.

Plugging the formula in Lemma
7.19
into the formula for
\iota^{*}\omega(v^{+}\otimes v^{-})
above, we get

\displaystyle\iota^{*}\omega(v^{+}\otimes v^{-})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}i(a+b+1-i)X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i-1}v\otimes X^{2+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}(i+1)(2+a+b-i)X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{1+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}\otimes

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b-1}(-1)^{i}(i+1)(a+b-i)X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{1+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}(i+1)(2+a+b-i)X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{1+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}

\displaystyle=-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}(i+1)X_{(1,-1,0)}^{i}v\otimes X^{1+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}.

∎

7.4.
Deligne–Beilinson cohomology and tempered currents

In this subsection, we collect some facts about Deligne–Beilinson cohomology and tempered currents, mainly following
BGCLRJ 24
.

7.4.1.
Classical Definition of Deligne–Beilinson Cohomology

We first recall the classical definition of Deligne–Beilinson Cohomology (DB-cohomology for short).

Let
X
be a smooth, quasi-projective complex analytic variety of pure dimension
d
. Let
\overline{X}
be a smooth compactification of
X
such that
D=\overline{X}-X
is a simple normal crossings divisor. We let
j\colon X\rightarrow\overline{X}
be the open immersion. We will assume that
X
is defined as the analytification of the base change to
\mathbb{C}
of a smooth, quasi-projective
\mathbb{R}
-scheme. For
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathbb{R}(p)
denote the subgroup
(2\pi i)^{p}\mathbb{R}
of
\mathbb{C}
. We will also use the same notation to denote the constant sheaf with value
\mathbb{R}(p)
on
X
.

Let
\Omega^{*}_{X}
be the sheaf of holomorphic differential forms on
X
and let
\Omega_{\overline{X}}^{*}(\mathrm{log}\,D)
be the sheaf of differential forms on
X
with logarithmic singularities along
D
. It is defined to be the sheaf of sub-
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}
-module of
j_{*}\Omega_{X}
locally generated by sections
\xi_{i},\,1\leq i\leq d
(see
(
Del 71
, (3.1.2))
). Here, we let

\xi_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{dz{i}}{z_{i}}&1\leq i\leq m\\
dz_{i}&m<i\leq d.\end{cases}

The Hodge filtration on
\Omega_{\overline{X}}^{*}(\mathrm{log}\,D)
is defined as

F^{p}\Omega_{\overline{X}}^{*}(\mathrm{log}\,D)=\bigoplus_{p^{\prime}\geq p}\Omega_{\overline{X}}^{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{log}\,D).

Fact 7.21
.

There are natural quasi-isomorphisms:

(1)

Rj_{*}\mathbb{C}\rightarrow Rj_{*}\Omega_{X}^{*}
,

(2)

\Omega^{*}_{\overline{X}}(\mathrm{log}\,D)\rightarrow Rj_{*}\Omega_{X}^{*}

(
Del 71
, (3.1.8.2))
.

Definition 7.22
.

(1)

For each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
DB-cohomology group

\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X,\mathbb{R}(p))
of
X
with coefficients in
\mathbb{R}(p)
is defined as the
n
-th hypercohomology of the complex

(7.11)

\mathbb{R}(p)_{D}:=\mathrm{Cone}(Rj_{*}\mathbb{R}(p)\oplus F^{p}\Omega_{\overline{X}}^{*}(\mathrm{log}\,D)\rightarrow Rj_{*}\Omega_{X}^{*})[-1],

where the arrow is given by the difference of the natural maps.

(2)

Let
\overline{F^{*}_{\infty}}=F_{\infty}^{*}\otimes c
be the de Rham involution given by the complex conjugation
F_{\infty}
on
X
and
c
on the coefficients. The
real DB-cohomology groups
are defined as

(7.12)

\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X/\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}(p)):=\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X,\mathbb{R}(p))^{\overline{F_{\infty}^{*}}=1}.

7.4.2.
Tempered Currents

We recall the definition of tempered currents in
BGCLRJ 24
here.

Let
\Delta=\{z\in\mathbb{C}||z|<\frac{1}{2}\}
be the open disc of radius
\frac{1}{2}
and
\Delta^{*}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}|0<|z|<\frac{1}{2}\}
be the open punctured disc of radius
r
.

For each point
x\in\overline{X}
, there is an open neighborhood
U
of
x
that is isomorphic to
\Delta^{d}
with coordinates
(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{d})
for which
x=(0,0,\ldots,0)
, and such that there exist some integers
m,n
with
0\leq n,m\leq d
and
m+n=d
such that

X\cap U=(\Delta^{*})^{m}\times(\Delta)^{n}=\{(z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{d})\in\Delta^{d}|z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{m}\neq 0\}.

We call such a subset
U
an open coordinate neighborhood of
x
.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}_{\overline{X}}^{0}
the sheaf of smooth functions on
\overline{X}
and let
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{\overline{X}}
be the complex of sheaves of smooth differential forms. The complex
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{\overline{X}}(\mathrm{log}\,D)
of smooth differential forms on
X
with logarithmic growth at
D
is defined to be the
\mathcal{A}_{\overline{X}}
-algebra subsheaf of
j_{*}\mathcal{A}_{X}
locally generated by sections
\log|z_{i}|\,(1\leq i\leq m)
and
\xi_{i},\bar{\xi_{i}}\,(1\leq i\leq d)
.
(
Bur 94
, §2)

We first give the definition of sheaves of differential forms with growth conditions.

Definition 7.23
.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}_{si}^{0}
(resp.,
\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{0}
) the sheaf on
\overline{X}
whose sections on an open
U\subset\overline{X}
is given by complex-valued functions on
U\cap X
, which are locally slowly increasing (resp. rapidly decreasing)
(
BGCLRJ 24
, Definition 2.3)
at each point of
U
. The graded sheaf
\mathcal{A}_{si}^{*}
of slowly increasing differential forms is defined to be the
\mathcal{A}_{si}^{0}
-subalgebra of
j_{*}\mathcal{A}_{X}^{*}
locally generated by
\xi_{i},\bar{\xi_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq d
. Similarly, the graded sheaf
\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{*}
of rapidly decreasing differential forms is defined to be the
\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{0}
-subalgebra of
j_{*}\mathcal{A}_{X}^{*}
locally generated by
\xi_{i},\bar{\xi_{i}}
for
1\leq i\leq d
. We denote by
\mathcal{A}_{si}(\overline{X})
and
\mathcal{A}_{rd}(\overline{X})
the corresponding complex of global sections.

Remark 7.24
.

(1)

There are natural inclusions

\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\overline{X}}^{*}(\log D)\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{si}^{*}\subseteq j_{*}\mathcal{A}^{*}_{X}.

Moreover, all these sheaves are fine sheaves.

(2)

The complex structure on
\overline{X}
induces compatible bigradings

\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{p,q},\,\mathcal{A}_{si}^{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\mathcal{A}_{si}^{p,q},\,\mathcal{A}_{\overline{X}}^{n}(\log\,D)=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\mathcal{A}_{\overline{X}}^{p,q}(\log D),

with correspoding Hodge filtrations

F^{p}\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{n}=\bigoplus_{p^{\prime}\geq p}\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{p^{\prime},q},F^{p}\mathcal{A}_{si}^{n}=\bigoplus_{p^{\prime}\geq p}\mathcal{A}_{si}^{p^{\prime},q},F^{p}\mathcal{A}_{\overline{X}}^{n}(\log\,D)=\bigoplus_{p^{\prime}\geq p}\mathcal{A}_{\overline{X}}^{p^{\prime},q}(\log\,D).

(3)

We denote by

\mathcal{A}_{si,\mathbb{R}}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{si}^{*},\mathcal{A}_{rd,\mathbb{R}}^{*}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{*},\mathcal{A}_{\overline{X},\mathbb{R}}^{*}(\log\,D)\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\overline{X}}^{*}(\log\,D)

the subcomplexes of sheaves of
\mathbb{R}
-valued differential forms.

We now give the definition of sheaves of tempered currents.

Definition 7.25
.

(1)

For any
0\leq p,q\leq d
, the sheaf
\mathcal{D}_{si}^{p,q}
of tempered currents is defined to be the sheaf on
\overline{X}
assigning to any open coordinate neighborhood
U\subseteq\overline{X}
the complex vector space
\mathcal{D}^{p,q}_{si}(U)
of continuous linear forms on the compactly supported sections
\Gamma_{c}(U,\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{d-p,d-q})
. Similarly, We denote by
\mathcal{D}_{si,\mathbb{R}}^{p,q}
the sheaf on
\overline{X}
assigning to any open coordinate neighborhood
U\subseteq\overline{X}
the real vector space
\mathcal{D}_{si,\mathbb{R}}^{p,q}(U)
of continuous linear forms on the compactly supported sections
\Gamma_{c}(U,\mathcal{A}_{rd,\mathbb{R}}^{d-p,d-q})
.

(2)

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let

\mathcal{D}_{si}^{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\mathcal{D}_{si}^{p,q},\mathcal{D}_{si,\mathbb{R}}^{n}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\mathcal{D}_{si,\mathbb{R}}^{p,q}.

The exterior differentials
\partial
and
\overline{\partial}
on
\mathcal{D}_{si}^{*}
are defined as follows: for any open coordinate neighborhood
U\subseteq\overline{X}
, any
T\in\mathcal{D}^{p,q}_{si}(U)
and any
\omega\in\Gamma_{c}(U,\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{d-p,d-q})
, we have

\partial T(w):=(-1)^{p+q+1}T(\partial w),\overline{\partial}T(w):=(-1)^{p+q+1}T(\overline{\partial}w).

The exterior differential
d\colon\mathcal{D}_{si}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{si}^{n+1}
is defined as
d=\partial+\overline{\partial}
. This defines complexes of sheaves of tempered currents
\mathcal{D}^{*}_{si},\mathcal{D}_{si}^{*,*}
(with differential
d,\partial,\overline{\partial}
). We will denote by
\mathcal{D}_{si}^{*,*}(\overline{X})
the corresponding complex of global sections.

(3)

The complex
D^{*}_{si}
is equipped with a Hodge filtration given by

F^{p}D^{*}_{si}=\bigoplus_{p^{\prime}\geq p}\mathcal{D}_{si}^{p^{\prime},q}.

(4)

For any open
U\subseteq\overline{X}
, there is a natural way to associate any form
\omega\in\mathcal{A}_{si}^{p,q}(U)
a current
T_{\omega}\in\mathcal{D}_{si}^{p,q}(U)
given by

T_{\omega}(\eta)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{d}}\int_{U}\omega\wedge\eta,\,(\eta\in\Gamma_{c}(U,\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{d-p,d-q})).

Remark 7.26
.

Since currents are modules over
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{si}
, all these complexes are complexes of fine sheaves.

Proposition 7.27
.

(
BGCLRJ 24
, Theorem 1.1)

The natural inclusions

(\Omega^{*}_{\overline{X}}(\log\,D),F)\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}^{*}_{\overline{X}}(\log\,D),F)\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}_{si}^{*},F)\rightarrow(\mathcal{D}^{*}_{si},F)

are filtered quasi-isomorphisms. Futhermore, the last two quasi-isomorphisms are compatible for the corresponding real structures.

Remark 7.28
.

By Proposition
7.27
, Fact
7.21
and, the fact that
\mathcal{D}_{si}^{*}
is a fine sheaf, we can see that Betti cohomology classes of
X
can be represented by closed tempered currents.

7.4.3.
Deligne–Beilinson cohomology in terms of tempered currents

In
BGCLRJ 24
, the authors give a new definition of Deligne–Beilinson cohomology in terms of smooth slowly increasing differential forms and tempered currents, which will be recalled here. This definition is convenient for computing Beilinson regulators.

Proposition 7.29
.

There is a quasi-isomorphism

\mathbb{R}(p)_{D}\simeq\mathrm{Cone}(F^{p}\mathcal{A}^{*}_{si}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{si,\mathbb{R}(p-1)}^{*})[-1],

where the arrow is induced by the projection
\pi_{p-1}\colon\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}(p-1)
defined by
\pi_{p-1}(z)=\frac{z+(-1)^{p-1}\overline{z}}{2}
. In particular, we have the canonical isomorphism

(7.13)

\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X,\mathbb{R}(p))\simeq\frac{\{(\phi,\phi^{\prime})\in\mathcal{A}_{si,\mathbb{R}(p-1)}^{n-1}(\overline{X})\oplus F^{p}\mathcal{A}_{si}^{n}(\overline{X})|d\phi^{\prime}=0,d\phi=\pi_{p-1}(\phi^{\prime})\}}{\{d(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\phi^{\prime}})\}},

where
d(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\phi^{\prime}})=(d\tilde{\phi}-\pi_{p-1}(\tilde{\phi^{\prime}}),d\tilde{\phi^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

See
(
BGCLRJ 24
, Proposition 2.23)
.
∎

Proposition 7.30
.

There is a quasi-isomorphism

\mathbb{R}(p)_{D}\simeq\mathrm{Cone}(F^{p}\mathcal{D}^{*}_{si}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{si,\mathbb{R}(p-1)}^{*})[-1].

In particular, we have the canonical isomorphism

(7.14)

\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X,\mathbb{R}(p))\simeq\frac{\{(S,T)\in\mathcal{D}_{si,\mathbb{R}(p-1)}^{n-1}(\overline{X})\oplus F^{p}\mathcal{D}_{si}^{n}(\overline{X})|dT=0,dS=\pi_{p-1}(T)\}}{\{d(\tilde{S},\tilde{T})\}},

where
d(\tilde{S},\tilde{T})=(d\tilde{S}-\pi_{p-1}(\tilde{T}),d\tilde{T})
.

Proof.

See
(
BGCLRJ 24
, Theorem 2.25)
.
∎

In what follows, we will use
[(S,T)]\in\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X,\mathbb{R}(p))
to denote the cohomology class of the pair
(S,T)
.

Proposition 7.31
.

Let
x\in\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X,\mathbb{R}(p))
be a DB-cohomology class which is represented by a pair
(\phi,\phi^{\prime})
of smooth slowly increasing differential forms via Proposition
7.29
. Then via the isomorphism of Proposition
7.30
, the class
x
is represented by the pairs of tempered currents
(T_{\phi},T_{\phi^{\prime}})
.

Proof.

See
(
BGCLRJ 24
, Proposition 2.26)
.
∎

The reinterpretation of DB-cohomology classes in terms of tempered currents allows us to describe explicitly the Gysin morphism as follows.
Let
\iota\colon X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion of pure codimension
c
. Let
\overline{X^{\prime}}
be a smooth compactification of
X^{\prime}
such that
D^{\prime}=\overline{X^{\prime}}-X^{\prime}
is a simple normal crossings divisor. We further assume that
\iota
extends to a morphism
\iota\colon\overline{X^{\prime}}\rightarrow\overline{X}
such that
\iota^{-1}(D)=D^{\prime}
. Then we define the Gysin morphism

(7.15)

\iota_{*}\colon\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X^{\prime},\mathbb{R}(p))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n+2c}(X,\mathbb{R}(p+c))

by
\iota_{*}[(S,T)]=[(\iota_{*}S,\iota_{*}T)]
.
Here for a tempered current
T
, we denote by
\iota_{*}T
the tempered current defined as: for any smooth rapidly decreasing differential form
\omega
,

(7.16)

\iota_{*}T(\omega)=T(\iota^{*}\omega),

which makes sense because
\iota^{*}\omega
is also rapidly decreasing.

Proposition 7.32
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
p\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
\omega\in\mathcal{A}_{rd}^{2d-n}(\overline{X})
be a smooth closed rapidly decreasing differential form with Hodge type components inside
\{(a,b):a,b>d-p\}
. Then the assignment
(S,T)\mapsto S(\omega)
induces a linear map

\langle-,\omega\rangle\colon\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X^{\prime},\mathbb{R}(p))\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.

Proof.

See
(
BGCLRJ 24
, Proposition 2.27)

∎

7.5.
DB-cohomology with coefficients

In this subsection, we give the definition of DB-cohomology with coefficients using the “Liebermann’s trick”:

Let
p\colon A\rightarrow S
be the universal abelian
3
-fold over
S
.

Proposition 7.33
.

We have a inclusion.

(7.17)

\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))\subset\mathrm{H}_{H}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+3}(A^{a+5b-3(r+s)},\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s+2)).

Proof.

Since
V=V^{a,b}\{a,b\}=\lambda(a+r-s,r-s,r-s-b;2s-r+b)
is in
\mathrm{Rep}(G)
, we have

V\subset(\mathrm{std})^{\otimes(a+5b-3(r+s))}(2s+2r-2b),

where
\mathrm{std}
is the standard representation of
G
. We then get the inclusion

(7.18)

V\subset\mathcal{H}^{a+5b-3(r+s)}p_{*}\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s)_{A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}}

in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R}))
by applying the functor
\mu_{H}
to the inclusion (
7.18
).
Recall that for the morphism
p\colon A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}\rightarrow S
, we have the following isomorphism in
\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R}))

(
Sai90b
, (4.5.4))
:

p_{*}\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s)\cong\bigoplus\limits_{i}\mathcal{H}^{i}p_{*}\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s)_{A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}}[-i].

Thus, we can see that

\mathcal{H}^{a+5b-3(r+s)}p_{*}\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s)_{A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}}\subset p_{*}\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s)[a+5b-3(r+s)].

Finally, if we let
\mathcal{A}=A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}
, then it can be seen from the definition of absolute Hodge cohomology that

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathrm{D}^{b}(\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathbb{R}(0)_{S},V(2)[3])

\displaystyle\subset\Hom_{\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(S/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathbb{R}(0)_{S},p_{*}\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s+2)[a+5b-3(r+s)+3])

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathrm{MHM}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{A}/\mathbb{R}))}(\mathbb{R}(0)_{\mathcal{A}},\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s+2)[a+5b-3(r+s)+3])

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}_{H}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+3}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s+2)).

∎

Definition 7.34
.

Let
i=a+5b-3(r+s)
and
j=a+3b-r-s+2
.

(1)

We define
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{D}(S,V(2))
as

\mathrm{H}^{3}_{D}(S,V(2)):=r_{H\rightarrow D}(\mathrm{H}^{3}_{H}(S,V(2))),

where
r_{H\rightarrow D}
is the natural map

r_{H\rightarrow D}\colon\mathrm{H}_{H}^{i+3}(A^{i},\mathbb{R}(j))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{D}^{i+3}(A^{i},\mathbb{R}(j))

and
A
is the universal abelian
3
-fold over
S
;

(2)

We define
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{D}(M,\iota^{*}V(1))
as

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{D}(M,\iota^{*}V(1)):=r_{H\rightarrow D}(\mathrm{H}^{1}_{H}(M,\iota^{*}V(1))),

where
r_{H\rightarrow D}
is the natural map

r_{H\rightarrow D}\colon\mathrm{H}_{H}^{i+1}(A^{i},\mathbb{R}(j-1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}_{D}^{i+1}(A^{i},\mathbb{R}(j-1))

and
A
is the pullback of the universal abelian
3
-fold over
S
to
M
through
\iota
;

(3)

We define
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{D}(M,W(1))
as

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{D}(M,W(1)):=r_{H\rightarrow D}(\mathrm{H}_{H}(M,W(1))),

where
r_{H\rightarrow D}
is the map

r_{H\rightarrow D}\colon\mathrm{H}^{n+1}_{H}(E^{n},\mathbb{R}(n+1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{n+1}_{D}(E^{n},\mathbb{R}(n+1))

and
E
is the universal elliptic curve over
M
;

(4)

We define
\mathrm{H}^{1}_{D}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}},W(1))
as

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{D}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}},W(1)):=r_{H\rightarrow D}(\mathrm{H}_{H}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}},W(1))),

where
r_{H\rightarrow D}
is the map

r_{H\rightarrow D}\colon\mathrm{H}^{n+1}_{H}(E^{n},\mathbb{R}(n+1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{n+1}_{D}(E^{n},\mathbb{R}(n+1))

and
E
is the universal elliptic curve over
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}
.

Remark 7.35
.

•

This definition is enough for our application to Beilinson’s conjectures and can be easily generalized to all Shimura varieties of PEL type. However, we do not give a definition of “DB-cohomology with coefficients” for general coefficients over general analytic varieties.

•

It follows from
(
Bei86b
, Page 60)
that the maps
r_{H\rightarrow D}
in (3) and (4) are isomorphic, but the maps
r_{H\rightarrow D}
in (1) and (2) may not be isomorphic.

Convention 7.36
.

Since we only work with real DB-cohomology in this paper, from now on, we will write
\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X,\mathbb{R}(p))
for

\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n}(X/\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}(p))
.

7.6.
Deligne-Beilinson classes

In this subsection, we compute the realization of the motivic classes constructed in Subsection
4.4
in Deligne–Beilinson cohomology.

7.6.1.
Eisenstein symbol in DB-cohomology

We first compute the realization of Eisenstein symbols (see Subsubsection
4.4.1
) in Deligne-Beilinson cohomology. We follow
(
Kin 98
, 6.3)
closely but we change the setting to use the Hermitian form
J_{2}^{\prime}
.

Convention 7.37
.

Recall that when we write
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we always mean
\GU(J_{2}^{\prime})^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
.

Notation 7.38
.

•

We let
\lambda^{\prime}(n,c)
be the character of the torus
T_{c}=Z_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\U(1)(\mathbb{R})
of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})

\begin{pmatrix}t_{1}&\\
&\bar{t}_{1}\\
\end{pmatrix}\in T_{c}\longmapsto(x+iy)^{n}(x^{2}+y^{2})^{\frac{c-n}{2}},

where
Z_{2}
is the center of
\GL_{2}
,
t_{1}=x+iy
,
n
is the weight and
c
is the central character.

•

Let
T_{spl}=Z_{2}(\mathbb{R})\{\begin{pmatrix}\cosh t&\sinh t\\
\sinh t&\cosh t\end{pmatrix}\}
, where
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\cosh t=\frac{e^{t}+e^{-t}}{2}
is the hyperbolic cosine function and
\sinh t=\frac{e^{t}-e^{-t}}{2}
is the hyperbolic sine function.
It can viewed as a split torus of the group
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
for the following reason.
If we let
C=\begin{pmatrix}i&i\\
-1&1\\
\end{pmatrix}
,
for
\begin{pmatrix}\cosh t&\sinh t\\
\sinh t&\cosh t\\
\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, then we have

C\begin{pmatrix}\cosh t&\sinh t\\
\sinh t&\cosh t\\
\end{pmatrix}C^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{t}&\\
&e^{-t}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

•

We denote by
\lambda(n,c)
the character of
T_{spl}

\lambda(n,c)\colon z\begin{pmatrix}\cosh t&\sinh t\\
\sinh t&\cosh t\\
\end{pmatrix}\in T_{spl}\longmapsto(e^{t})^{n}z^{c},

where
z\in Z_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
n
is the weight and
c
is the central character.

Notation 7.39
.

•

Let
(X,Y)
be a basis of the standard representation
V_{2}
of
\GL_{2}
such that each
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
acts by

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}X=aX+bY,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}Y=cX+dY.

•

Let us view
\Sym^{n}V_{2,\mathbb{C}}
as the
n
-dimensional vector space of homogeneous polynomials of degree
n
in the variables
X
and
Y
with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}
. For any integer
0\leq j\leq n
, let
b_{j}^{n}
be the vector
b_{j}^{n}=(-1)^{j}X^{j}Y^{n-j}
, so it has weight
\lambda^{\prime}(2j-n,n)
. The family
(b_{j}^{n})_{0\leq j\leq n}
forms a basis of
\Sym^{n}V_{2,\mathbb{C}}
. We denote by
(a_{j}^{n})_{0\leq j\leq n}
the dual basis of
(b_{j}^{n})_{0\leq j\leq n}
. Hence, the vector
a_{j}^{n}
has weight
\lambda^{\prime}(n-2j,-n)
.

Definition 7.40
.

(1)

We define the function
z_{2}
,
z_{2}^{\prime}
,
w_{2}
and
w_{2}^{\prime}
on
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
as:

\displaystyle z_{2}(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}):=\frac{b+d}{-b+d}i,

\displaystyle z_{2}^{\prime}(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}):=\frac{a+c}{-a+c}i;

\displaystyle w_{2}(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}):=-b+d,

\displaystyle w_{2}^{\prime}(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}):=a-c.

(2)

For any integer
r
such that
r\equiv n(\text{mod}\ 2)
, we define the function
\phi_{r}^{n}
on
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
as

\phi_{r}^{n}=(z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime})w_{2}^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}{w_{2}^{\prime}}^{-\frac{n+r}{2}}.

Remark 7.41
.

Since we identify
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
\GU(J_{2}^{\prime})^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
,
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
if and only if
a=\bar{d}
and
c=\bar{b}
. Hence, it is impossible that
a=c
or
b=d
, and the functions
z_{2}
and
z_{2}^{\prime}
are well-defined on
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Notation 7.42
.

Let
B_{2}
be the standard Borel of
\GL_{2}
. Since we identify
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
\GU(J_{2}^{\prime})^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

B_{2}(\mathbb{R})=T_{spl}N
where
N=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1-\frac{1}{2i}u&\frac{1}{2i}u\\
-\frac{1}{2i}u&1+\frac{1}{2i}u\end{pmatrix}|u\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
.

Proposition 7.43
.

We have

\phi_{r}^{n}\in\Ind_{B_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}^{\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}\lambda(n+2,-n).

Furthermore, the compact torus
T_{c}
acts on
\phi_{r}^{n}
by right translation and
\phi_{r}^{n}
has weight
\lambda^{\prime}(-r,-n)
.

Proof.

(1)

We have the following identity:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\cosh t&\sinh t\\
\sinh t&\cosh t\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a\cosh t+c\sinh t&b\cosh t+d\sinh t\\
a\sinh t+c\cosh t&b\sinh t+d\cosh t\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1-\frac{1}{2i}u&\frac{1}{2i}u\\
-\frac{1}{2i}u&1+\frac{1}{2i}u\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a+(c-a)\frac{1}{2i}u&b+(d-b)\frac{1}{2i}u\\
c+(c-a)\frac{1}{2i}u&d+(d-b)\frac{1}{2i}u\end{pmatrix}.

Then by the identity

\displaystyle\cosh t\pm\sinh t=e^{\pm t}

and
\phi_{r}^{n}=(z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime})w_{2}^{-\frac{n-r}{2}}{w_{2}^{\prime}}^{-\frac{n+r}{2}}
, we can see that

\phi_{r}^{n}\in\Ind_{B_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}^{\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}\lambda(n+2,-n).

(2)

The weight of
\phi_{r}^{n}
under
T_{c}
can be seen from the identity

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}(x+iy)&0\\
0&(x-iy)\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}(x+iy)a&(x-iy)b\\
(x+iy)c&(x-iy)d\end{pmatrix}

and the definition of
\phi_{r}^{n}
.

∎

Recall that we have the decomposition

\liegl_{2,\mathbb{C}}=\liek_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{+}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{-}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}},

and we let
v^{+}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\in\liep^{+}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}
and

v^{-}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\liep^{-}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}
.

Let us define

w_{n}^{+},w_{n}^{-}\in\Hom_{K_{\GL_{2}}}(\liep^{+}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}\oplus\liep^{-}_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}},\Sym^{n}V_{2,\mathbb{C}}\otimes\Ind_{B_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}^{\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}\lambda(n+2,-n))

by

\displaystyle\omega_{n}^{+}(v^{+})=(2\pi i)^{n+1}b^{n}_{0}\otimes\phi^{n}_{-(n+2)},

\displaystyle\omega_{n}^{+}(v^{-})=0,

\displaystyle\omega_{n}^{-}(v^{+})=0,

\displaystyle\omega_{n}^{-}(v^{-})=(2\pi i)^{n+1}b^{n}_{n}\otimes\phi^{n}_{n+2}.

Remark 7.44
.

•

The weight of
b^{n}_{0}
is
\lambda^{\prime}(-n,n)
, the weight of
\phi^{n}_{-(n+2)}
is
\lambda^{\prime}(n+2,-n)
and the weight
v^{+}
is
\lambda^{\prime}(2,0)
; the weight of
b^{n}_{n}
is
\lambda^{\prime}(n,n)
, the weight of
\phi^{n}_{n+2}
is
\lambda^{\prime}(-(n+2),-n)
and the weight
v^{-}
is
\lambda^{\prime}(-2,0)
. So
\omega_{n}^{\pm}
preserves
K_{\GL_{2}}
-weight, so it is well-defined.

•

The vector
\phi^{n}_{-(n+2)}
is a minimal
K_{\GL_{2}}
-type vector in the discrete series
D_{n}^{+}
.

Let us state some basic properties.

Lemma 7.45
.

We have the following identities of the action of
v^{\pm}
:

(1)

\displaystyle v^{+}(w_{2})=-w_{2}^{\prime},\ v^{+}(w_{2}^{\prime})=0,

\displaystyle v^{+}(z_{2})=\frac{2(ad-bc)}{(-b+d)^{2}}i,\ v^{+}(z_{2}^{\prime})=0,

\displaystyle v^{-}(w_{2})=0,\ v^{-}(w_{2}^{\prime})=-w_{2},

\displaystyle v^{-}(z_{2})=0,\ v^{-}(z_{2}^{\prime})=\frac{2(bc-ad)}{(-a+c)^{2}}i;

(2)

\displaystyle v^{+}(\phi_{r}^{n})=\frac{n-r+2}{2}\phi^{n}_{r-2},\ v^{-}(\phi_{r}^{n})=\frac{n+2+r}{2}\phi^{n}_{r+2},

(3)

v^{+}b^{n}_{r}=-(n-r)b^{n}_{r+1},\ v^{-}b^{n}_{r}=-rb^{n}_{r-1}.

Proof.

If
f
is a linear function on
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, then

\displaystyle(v^{+}f)(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix})

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&t\\
0&1\end{pmatrix})-f(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix})}{t}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&t\\
0&0\end{pmatrix})}{t}=f(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}).

Similarly, we have

(v^{+}f)(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix})=f(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}).

(1)

It follows from the fact that
w_{2}
and
w_{2}^{\prime}
are linear functions that

\displaystyle(v^{+}w_{2})(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix})=-a+c=w_{2}^{\prime}(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}),

\displaystyle(v^{+}w_{2}^{\prime})(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix})=0.

The proof for
v^{-}(w_{2})
and
v^{-}(w_{2}^{\prime})
is similar.

It can be seen from the quotient rule that

\displaystyle(v^{+}z_{2})=\frac{(v^{+}(b+d))(-b+d)-(v^{+}(-b+d))(b+d)}{(-b+d)^{2}}i=\frac{2(ad-bc)}{(-b+d)^{2}}i,

\displaystyle(v^{+}z_{2}^{\prime})=\frac{(v^{+}(a+c))(-a+c)-(v^{+}(-a+c))(a+c)}{(-a+c)^{2}}i=0.

The proof for
v^{-}(z_{2})
and
v^{-}(z_{2}^{\prime})
is similar.

(2)

It follows from explicit computation that

\displaystyle z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(bc-ad)}{(-b+d)(-a+c)}i

\displaystyle=(v^{+}(z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime}))\frac{w_{2}}{w_{2}^{\prime}}.

From this, we get

v^{+}(z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime})=(z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime})\frac{w_{2}^{\prime}}{w_{2}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle v^{+}\phi^{n}_{r}

\displaystyle=(z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime})w_{2}^{-(\frac{n-r}{2}+1)}{w_{2}^{\prime}}^{-(\frac{n+r}{2}-1)}

\displaystyle+(\frac{n-r}{2})(z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime})w_{2}^{-(\frac{n-r}{2}+1)}{w_{2}^{\prime}}^{-(\frac{n+r}{2}-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-r+2}{2}(z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime})w_{2}^{-(\frac{n-(r-2)}{2})}{w_{2}^{\prime}}^{-(\frac{n+(r-2)}{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{n-r+2}{2}\phi^{n}_{r-2}.

The computation of
v^{-}\phi^{n}_{r}
is similar.

(3)

Since
X
and
Y
are linear functions, we have
v^{+}(X)=0
and
v^{+}(Y)=X
.
Hence, by the product rule,

v^{+}(b^{n}_{r})=v^{+}((-1)^{r}X^{r}Y^{n-r})=(-1)^{j}(n-r)X^{r+1}Y^{n-r-1}=-(n-r)b^{n}_{r+1}.

Similar computations hold for
v^{-}(b^{n}_{j})
.

∎

Lemma 7.46
.

We have the following identities with complex conjuagtion:

\displaystyle\overline{\phi^{n}_{r}}=(-1)\phi^{n}_{-r},\ \overline{b^{n}_{r}}=b^{b}_{n-r},\overline{v^{+}}=v^{-},

\displaystyle\overline{\omega^{\pm}}=(-1)^{n}\omega_{n}^{\mp},

where
\overline{(\cdot)}
denotes the complex conjugation.

Proof.

Recall that the Shimura data of
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}
is defined on
\mathbb{R}
-points as

\hbox to140.01pt{\vbox to43.08pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 70.00601pt\lower-21.54167pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

and the reflex field of the Shimura variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
and the complex conjugation on it is given by conjugation by

N=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
. From this we can see that

\overline{v^{+}}=\mathrm{Ad}_{N}(v^{+})=v^{-}.

It follows from

\mathrm{Ad}_{N}(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix})=\begin{pmatrix}d&c\\
b&a\end{pmatrix},

that
\overline{a}=d
and
\overline{b}=c
. So we have

\displaystyle\overline{w_{2}}=-\overline{b}+\overline{d}=a-c=w_{2}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\overline{z_{2}}=\frac{\overline{b}+\overline{d}}{-\overline{b}+\overline{d}}(-i)=\frac{a+c}{c-a}i=z_{2}^{\prime}.

We can see from this that

\overline{\phi^{n}_{r}}=(-1)(z_{2}-z_{2}^{\prime})w_{2}^{-(\frac{n-(-r)}{2})}{w_{2}^{\prime}}^{-(\frac{n+(-r)}{2})}=(-1)\phi^{n}_{-r}.

The complex conjugate on
X,Y
is given by
\overline{(X,Y)}=(X,Y)N=(-Y,-X)
. Hence, we have

\overline{b^{n}_{r}}=\overline{(-1)^{r}X^{r}Y^{n-r}}=(-1)^{n+r}Y^{r}X^{n-r}=b^{n}_{n-r}.

Finally, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\overline{\omega_{n}^{-}}(v^{-})

\displaystyle=\overline{(2\pi i)^{n+1}}\overline{b^{n}_{n}}\otimes\overline{\phi^{n}_{n+2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+1}(2\pi{i})^{n+1}b^{n}_{0}\otimes(-1)\phi^{n}_{-(n+2)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}(2\pi{i})^{n+1}b^{n}_{0}\otimes\phi^{n}_{-(n+2)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\omega^{+}_{n}(v^{+}).

Therefore,
\overline{\omega^{\pm}_{n}}=(-1)^{n}\omega_{n}^{\mp}
.
∎

Given
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, we let

Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})=\sum_{\gamma\in B_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q})}\gamma^{*}(\omega_{n}^{+}\otimes\phi_{f}),

which is absolutely convergent
(
Kin 98
, (6.3.1))
and defines an element of

\Hom_{K_{\GL_{2}}}(\liep_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}^{+}\oplus\liep_{\GL_{2},\mathbb{C}}^{-},\Sym^{n}V_{2,\mathbb{C}}\otimes C^{\infty}(\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A})).

Consider

\theta_{n}=\frac{(2\pi i)^{n+1}}{2(n+1)}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}b_{n-j}^{n}\otimes\phi_{n-2j}^{n}\in\Sym^{n}V_{2,\mathbb{C}}\otimes\Ind_{B_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}^{\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}\lambda(n+2,-n),

Similar to before, for
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
we define

(7.19)

Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})=\sum_{\gamma\in B_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q})}\gamma^{*}(\theta_{n}\otimes\phi_{f}).

This infinite series is absolutely convergent for
n\geq 1
. In this paper, we only treat the case
n\geq 1
.

Lemma 7.47
.

(
Kin 98
, (6.3.5))

We have the following identity:

\mathrm{d}(Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f}))=\pi_{n}(Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})).

In other words, for any
n\geq 1
and
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, the class
Eis_{D}^{n}(\phi_{f})
is represented by

(Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f}),Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f}))\in\mathrm{H}_{D}^{1}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}},\Sym^{n}V_{2}(1)),

which is a direct factor of
\mathrm{H}_{D}^{n+1}(E^{n},\mathbb{R}(n+1))
. Here
E\rightarrow\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}
is the universal elliptic curve over the Shimura variety
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle d\theta_{n}(v^{+})=v^{+}\theta_{n}=\frac{(2\pi{i})^{n+1}}{2(n+1)}(

\displaystyle b^{n}_{n}\otimes\phi^{n}_{n-2}

\displaystyle+2b^{n}_{n-1}\otimes\phi^{n}_{n-4}-b^{n}_{n}\otimes\phi^{n}_{n-2}

\displaystyle+3b^{n}_{n-2}\otimes\phi^{n}_{n-6}-2b^{n}_{n-1}\otimes\phi^{n}_{n-4}

\displaystyle+\cdots

\displaystyle+(n+1)b^{n}_{0}\otimes\phi^{n}_{-(n+2)}-nb^{n}_{1}\otimes\phi^{n}_{-n})

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi{i})^{n+1}}{2}b^{n}_{0}\otimes\phi^{n}_{-(n+2)}.

By a similar computation, we have
d\theta_{n}(v^{-})=\frac{(2\pi{i})^{n+1}}{2}b^{n}_{n}\otimes\phi^{n}_{n+2}
.
It follows from the definitions that

\pi_{n}(\omega^{+}_{n})=\frac{\omega_{n}^{+}+(-1)^{n}\overline{\omega_{n}^{+}}}{2}=\frac{\omega_{n}^{+}+{\omega_{n}^{-}}}{2}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(\omega^{+}_{n})(v^{+})=\frac{\omega_{n}^{+}+{\omega_{n}^{-}}}{2}(v^{+})=\frac{(2\pi{i})^{n+1}}{2}b^{n}_{0}\otimes\phi^{n}_{-(n+2)}=d\theta_{n}(v^{+}),

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(\omega^{+}_{n})(v^{-})=\frac{\omega_{n}^{+}+{\omega_{n}^{-}}}{2}(v^{-})=\frac{(2\pi{i})^{n+1}}{2}b^{n}_{n}\otimes\phi^{n}_{n+2}=d\theta_{n}(v^{-}).

In summary, we have shown
\pi_{n}(\omega_{n}^{+})=d\theta_{n}
so
\mathrm{d}(Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f}))=\pi_{n}(Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f}))
.
∎

Remark 7.48
.

This proof is essentially the same as in
(
Kin 98
, §6.3)
, but we chose to identify
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
\GU(J_{2}^{\prime})^{\prime}
, so we reproduce the proof here.

7.6.2.
Motivic classes in DB-cohomology

Now we give an expression of the realization of the motivic classes constructed in Construction
4.44
in Deligne–Beilinson cohomology using functoriality of motivic cohomology, DB-cohomology and the Beilinson regulator.

Notation 7.49
.

We use the following notations:

•

*:=a+5b-3(r+s)+1
,
**:=a+5b-3(r+s)+3
,
\Box:=a+5b-3(r+s)
,
\circ:=a+3b-r-s+1
and
\triangle:=a+3b-r-s+2
;

•

for
\diamond\in\{M,H,D\}
,
i_{\diamond}
stands for the natural inclusion induced by
W\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}V
.

Proposition 7.50
.

We have the following commutative diagram:

\hbox to470.54pt{\vbox to130.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 235.26787pt\lower-64.40909pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
