\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where in the last row,
E^{n}
in the first
\mathrm{H}^{n+1}_{D}(E^{n},\mathbb{R}(n+1))
is the
n
-th fiber product of the universal elliptic curve over
\mathrm{Sh}_{\GL_{2}}
,
E^{n}
in the second
\mathrm{H}^{n+1}_{D}(E^{n},\mathbb{R}(n+1))
is the
n
-th fiber product of the universal elliptic curve over
M
,
A^{\Box}
in
\mathrm{H}_{D}^{*}(A^{\Box},\mathbb{R}(\circ))
is the
\Box
-th fiber product of the pullback of the universal abelian
3
-fold from
S
to
M
through
\iota
, and
A^{\Box}
in
\mathrm{H}_{D}^{**}(A^{\Box},\mathbb{R}(\triangle))
is the
\Box
-th fiber product of the universal abelian
3
-fold over
S
.

Proof.

First, the commutativity of the diagram comes from the functoriality of
r_{H}
and
r_{H\rightarrow D}
.

Second, the decomposition of the Gysin morphism

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{\mathrm{\diamond}}(M,W(1))\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{3}_{\mathrm{\diamond}}(S,V(2))

into
\iota_{\diamond,*}\circ i_{\diamond}
is natural from the construction of Gysin morphism (Proposition
4.16
).

Finally, the embebding of the third row into the fourth row is from “Liebermann’s trick” as explained in Proposition
7.33
.
∎

Remark 7.51
.

From Construction
4.44
, the Eisenstein classes
c\in\mathrm{H}^{3}_{M}(S,V(2))
are

c=\iota_{M,*}\circ i_{M}\circ p_{M}^{*}\circ Eis_{M}^{n}(\phi_{f}),

for
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
.
By commutativity of the above diagram, their image under the Deligne regulator
r_{D}:=r_{H\rightarrow D}\circ r_{H}

is

\displaystyle r_{D}(c)

\displaystyle=r_{D}(\iota_{M,*}\circ i_{M}\circ p_{M}^{*}\circ Eis_{M}^{n}(\phi_{f}))

\displaystyle=\iota_{D,*}\circ i_{D}\circ p_{D}^{*}\circ Eis_{D}^{n}(r_{D}(\phi_{f}))

\displaystyle=\iota_{D,*}\circ i_{D}\circ p_{D}^{*}(Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f}),Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})).

By “Liebermann’s trick”, Proposition
7.30
, Proposition
7.31
and the fact that
Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})
and
Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})
are slowly increasing as explained in
(
Kin 98
, page 120)
, we can view

i_{D}\circ p_{D}^{*}\circ(Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f}),Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})).

as a pair of tempered currents

(T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})},T_{p^{*}Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})})\in\mathrm{H}^{1}_{D}(M,\iota^{*}V(1))\subset\mathrm{H}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+1}_{D}(A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}/M,\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s+1)),

where
A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}/M
is the
a+5b-3(r+s)
-th fiber product of the pullback of the universal abelian
3
-fold from
S
to
M
through
\iota
.
Hence, by the explicit description of the Gysin morphism for DB-cohomology (cf. equation (
7.15
)), we have

\displaystyle r_{D}(c)=(\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})},\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})})

\displaystyle\in\mathrm{H}^{3}_{D}(S,V(2))

\displaystyle\subset\mathrm{H}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+3}_{D}(A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}/S,\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s+2)),

where
A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}/S
is the
a+5b-3(r+s)
-th fiber product of the universal abelian
3
-fold over
S
.

7.7.
The pairing

In this subsection, we give an explicit formula for the Poincaré duality pairing with the tool of tempered currents.

7.7.1.
From Poincaré duality pairing to an integral of differential forms

We first express the Poincaré duality pairing in terms of an integration of differential forms on Shimura varieties.

Lemma 7.52
.

Let
\widetilde{A}^{a+5b-3(r+s)}
be a smooth toroidal compactification of
A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}
such that the complement

\widetilde{A}^{a+5b-3(r+s)}\backslash A^{a+5b-3(r+s)}
is a simple normal crossings divisor. Then there exists a closed tempered current

\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{si,\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s+1)}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+2}(\widetilde{A}^{a+5b-3(r+s)})\

such that:

(1)

the cohomology class
[\rho]
of
\rho
belongs to
\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))^{-}(-1)
;

(2)

the class
[\rho]
maps to
\mathcal{E}is_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})=r_{H}(\mathcal{E}is_{M}^{n}(\phi_{f}))

through the third map in the exact sequence (
7.1
):

\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}^{-}(-1)\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS}_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}(\mathbb{R}(0),\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))_{\mathbb{R}});

(3)

the pair of tempered currents
(\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})},\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})})
and
(\rho,0)
represent the same cohomology class in
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{D}(S,V(2))
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
(
Bei86b
, §5.7.)
.
∎

Corollary 7.53
.

We have the following identity:

\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{K}\rangle_{B}=\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}\otimes\mathbf{1},

where
\mathbf{1}
be unit of
E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

It is direct from Lemma
7.52
(2) and Remark
7.13
(5).
∎

We first take the dual of the map of representations

\Sym^{n}V_{2}\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}V,

and get the map
\iota^{*}D\rightarrow\Sym^{n}V_{2}^{\vee}
.
Composing the above map with the natural pairing
\Sym^{n}V_{2}^{\vee}\otimes\Sym^{n}V_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(0)
defined by

a^{n}_{j}\otimes b^{n}_{k}\mapsto\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\ j\neq k,\\
\binom{n}{j}&\text{if}\ j=k,\\
\end{cases}

we have a natural pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\colon\iota^{*}D\otimes\Sym^{n}V_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(0).

Convention 7.54
.

We normalize the embedding

\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,V(2))\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+2}_{B,!}(A^{a+5b-3(r+s)},\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s+2))

and

\mathrm{H}^{2}_{B,!}(S,D)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+2}_{B,!}(A^{a+b-3(r+s)},\mathbb{R}(2b-2r-2s))

such that we have the following commutative diagram:

\hbox to322.87pt{\vbox to60.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 161.43317pt\lower-27.66922pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{99.91142pt}{-13.79564pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
*=a+5b-3(r+s)+2
and
\Box=a+5b-3(r+s)
.

Let
\Psi=\Psi_{f}\otimes\Psi_{\infty}
be a factorizable cusp form in the irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation
\tilde{\pi}=\tilde{\pi}_{f}\otimes\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
of
G(\mathbb{A})
with central character
19
19

19

Here
\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
is a
(\lieg_{\mathbb{C}},K_{G})
-module, but
A_{G}\subset K_{G}
, so the word “central character of
\tilde{\pi}
makes sense.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\pi}\colon Z_{G}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash Z_{G}(\mathbb{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|z|^{n}\nu(z)^{-1}},}

where
\nu
is a finite order Hecke character of sign
(-1)^{n}
. Let

\omega_{\Psi}:=\omega\otimes\Psi_{f}

be a differential form associated to
\Psi
, where
\omega
is the Lie algebra cohomology class constructed in Proposition
7.16
.

Recall that we let
n=a+b+r+s
and we use
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)
(resp.,
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)
) to denote
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}(L)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}
(resp.,
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}
).

Proposition 7.55
.

Let
\phi_{f}\in I_{n}(\nu)\subset\mathcal{B}_{n,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
defined in Definition
4.42
and let
\rho
be the closed tempered current associated to the class
\mathcal{E}is_{D}^{n}(\phi_{f})=r_{D}(\mathcal{E}is_{M}^{n}(\phi_{f}))
by Lemma
7.52
. We have

\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{n+1}}\int_{\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)}p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi},

where
K
is the level of
\phi_{f}
.

Proof.

For the pair of tempered currents
(\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})},\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{B}^{n}(\phi_{f})})
and
(\rho,0)
that represent the same cohomology class in
\mathrm{H}^{3}_{D}(\mathrm{Sh}_{G},V(2))
, there exists

(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}_{si,\mathbb{R}(a+3b-r-s+1)}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+1}(\widetilde{A}^{a+5b-3(r+s)})\oplus F^{a+3b-r-s+2}\mathcal{D}_{si}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+2}(\widetilde{A}^{a+5b-3(r+s)})

such that

\rho=\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})}+dS^{\prime}+\pi_{a+3b-r-s+1}(T^{\prime}).

It follows from the fact that
\omega_{\Psi}
is a diffferential form on
\mathrm{Sh}_{G}
that

\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}=\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{G}}.

Then we can see that

\displaystyle\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}

\displaystyle=\rho(\omega_{\Psi})

\displaystyle=\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})}(\omega_{\Psi})+dS^{\prime}(\omega_{\Psi})+\pi_{a+2r-s+1}(T^{\prime})(\omega_{\Psi})

\displaystyle=\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})}(\omega_{\Psi})+\pi_{a+2r-s+1}(T^{\prime})(\omega_{\Psi})

\displaystyle=\iota_{*}T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})}(\omega_{\Psi})

\displaystyle=T_{p^{*}Eis_{H}^{n}(\phi_{f})}(\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{n+1}}\int_{\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)}p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}.

The first equality holds by the compatibility between Poincaré duality pairings and integrations (see
(
Har 90
, Prop.1.4.4.(c))
).
The third equality follows from the fact that
\omega_{\Psi}
is closed.
The fourth equality follows from the fact that the Hodge type of
\omega_{\Psi}
is
(a+r+1,b+s+1)
and the fact that
T^{\prime}\in F^{a+3b-r-s+2}\mathcal{D}_{si}^{a+5b-3(r+s)+2}(\widetilde{A}^{a+5b-3(r+s)})
.
The fifth equality follows from the definition of the pushforward of tempered currents (see equation (
7.16
)). The last equality comes from the definition of tempered currents. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

7.7.2.
From the integral of differential forms to an adelic integral

We now translate the above integral into an adelic integral. In order to do so, we need to make the following choices of measures.

Convention 7.56
.

(1)

We normalize the Haar measure for a reductive or unipotent group
G^{\prime}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
such that
\mathrm{Vol}(G^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))=1
.

(2)

At the archimedean place, we use the Iwasawa decomposition to determine the measure. More precisely, we define the measure on the unipotent radical of the Borels
20
20

20

The algebraic group
G
is quasi-split over
\mathbb{Q}
.
of
G(\mathbb{R})
and
H(\mathbb{R})
so that the integral points have covolume
1
. We choose the Haar measure on
K_{G}
(resp.,
K_{H}
) such that the volume of
K_{G}/Z_{G}(\mathbb{R})
(resp.,
K_{H}/Z_{H}(\mathbb{R})
) is
1
. On the maximal split torus
\mathbb{R}^{\times,n}
, we choose the multiplicative Haar measure
\frac{dx_{1}dx_{2}\cdots dx_{n}}{|x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}|}
.

(3)

The measures on
G(\mathbb{A})
and
H(\mathbb{A})
are the product of the above nonarchimedean measures and archimedean measures.

(4)

For quotients of groups, we use the unique measure compatible with these choices.

(5)

Once we fix
\mathbf{1}=v^{+}\otimes v^{-}
, we have fixed an equivalence of top diffferential forms on
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)
and invariant measures on
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)
(see
(
Har 97
, Page 83)
).

Proposition 7.57
.

For
\phi_{f}
and
\rho
be as in Proposition
7.55
. We have

\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}=\frac{[H(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):K]^{-1}}{(2\pi{i})^{n+1}}\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}(p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}(\mathbf{1}))(g)dg,

where
\mathbf{1}
is the vector
v^{+}\otimes v^{-}
and the vectors
v^{+}
and
v^{-}
are defined in equation (
7.9
).

Proof.

By Proposition
7.55
, we have

\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{n+1}}\int_{\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)}p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}.

Thanks to the equivalence of top differential forms on
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)
and invariant measures on
\mathrm{Sh}_{H}(K)
explained above, we can write the integral as an adelic integral. More precisely, we have

\displaystyle\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{n+1}}\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})/K_{H}K}(p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}(\mathbf{1}))(g)dg

\displaystyle=\frac{[H(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):K]^{-1}}{(2\pi i)^{n+1}}\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})Z_{H}(\mathbb{R})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}(p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}(\mathbf{1}))(g)dg

\displaystyle=\frac{[H(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):K]^{-1}}{(2\pi i)^{n+1}}\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})Z(\mathbb{R})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}(p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}(\mathbf{1}))(g)dg

\displaystyle=\frac{[H(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):K]^{-1}}{(2\pi i)^{n+1}}\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}(p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}(\mathbf{1}))(g)dg.

The second equality follows from
\mathrm{Vol}(K)=[H(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):K]^{-1}
and
\mathrm{Vol}(K_{H}/Z_{H}(\mathbb{R}))=1
. The third equality is from the fact that
\mathrm{Vol}(\mathbb{S}^{1})=1
and the fourth comes from
\mathrm{Vol}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times})=1
.
∎

7.7.3.
An explicit form of the adelic integral

Recall that we let
\Psi_{\infty}\in\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
be a vector of weight
(-1-b+r-s,1+a+r-s,r-s;b+2s-r)
and
v\in D
be a vector of weight
(-a+s-r,b+s-r,s-r;-b-2s+r)
. We also have the differential form
\omega_{\Psi}
associated to
\Psi
by Proposition
7.16
. The following proposition gives a precise formula for
\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}
.

Notation 7.58
.

•

We let

C_{j}:=[H(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):K]^{-1}\langle X^{2j-b-r-s}_{(1,-1,0)}v,b^{a+b+r+s}_{n-j}\rangle.

•

We denote by
\Xi_{m,t}(\phi_{f})
the functions on
H(\mathbb{A})
defined by

\Xi_{m,t}(\phi_{f}):=X^{m}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi\sum\limits_{\gamma\in B_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\GU(J_{2}^{{}^{\prime}})^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Q})}\gamma^{*}(\phi_{t}^{a+b+r+s}\otimes\phi_{f}).

•

We let

\displaystyle A=\max\{0,\frac{b+r+s}{2}\},

\displaystyle B=\min\{\frac{a+2b+r+s}{2},a+b+r+s\}.

Proposition 7.59
.

The pairing
\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}
is equal to

(7.20)

-\frac{1}{2(a+b+r+s+1)}\sum\limits_{j=A}^{B}(-1)^{b+r+s}(2j-b-r-s+1)C_{j}\int\Xi_{1+a+2b-2j+r+s,a+b+r+s-2j}(\phi_{f}),

where the integrals are over
H(\mathbb{Q})Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.57
, we have

\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}=\frac{[H(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):K]^{-1}}{(2\pi{i})^{n+1}}\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}(p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}(\mathbf{1}))(g)dg,

where
\mathbf{1}
is the vector
v^{+}\otimes v^{-}
.

It follows from the formula for
Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})
in equation (
7.19
) and the formula for
\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi}
in Proposition
7.20
that for
g\in H(\mathbb{A})
,

\displaystyle(p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi})(\mathbf{1})(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(2\pi{i})^{n+1}}{2(n+1)}(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{a+b}(-1)^{i}(i+1)X^{1+a+b-i}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}\otimes\Psi_{f})(g)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle(\sum\limits_{\gamma\in B_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q})}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n}\gamma^{*}(\phi_{n-2j}^{n}\otimes\phi_{f}))(g)\langle X^{i}_{(1,-1,0)}v,b^{n}_{n-j}\rangle.

The weight of
X^{i}_{(1,-1,0)}v
is
\lambda^{\prime}(i-a,-a-b-r-s)
when restricting to
\GL_{2}
since its weight is
(i-a+s-r,-i+b+s-r,s-r;-b-2s+r)
. It can be seen from the definition of the pairing and the fact that the weight of
b^{n}_{n-j}
is
\lambda^{\prime}(a+b+r+s-2j,a+b+r+s)

that if
a+b+r+s-2j\neq-(i-a)
, then

\langle X^{i}_{(1,-1,0)}v,b^{n}_{n-j}\rangle=0.

Thus only the term satisfying
i=2j-b-r-s
remains.
By the inequality
0\leq i\leq a+b
, we have

\frac{b+r+s}{2}\leq j\leq\frac{a+2b+r+s}{2}.

By
0\leq j\leq a+b+r+s
and the definition of
A
and
B
, we have
A\leq j\leq B
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle(p^{*}Eis_{H}(\phi_{f})\iota^{*}\omega_{\Psi})(\mathbf{1})(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(2\pi{i})^{n+1}}{2(n+1)}\sum\limits_{j=A}^{B}(-1)^{b+r+s}(2j-b-r-s+1)[H(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):K]C_{j}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle(X^{1+a+2b-2j+r+s}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}\otimes\Psi_{f})\times(\sum\limits_{\gamma\in B_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q})}\gamma^{*}(\phi^{a+b+r+s}_{a+b+r+s-2j}\otimes\phi_{f})).

Plugging this into the integral, we get

\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}=-\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\sum\limits_{j=A}^{B}(-1)^{b+r+s}(2j-b-r-s+1)C_{j}\int\Xi_{1+a+2b-2j+r+s,a+b+r+s-2j}(\phi_{f}).

∎

Remark 7.60
.

(1)

By the inequality
A\leq j\leq B
, we can see that
2j-b-r-s+1>0
, which is important for later applications.

(2)

The
K_{\GL_{2}}
-weight of
\Xi_{1+a+2b-2j+r+s,a+b+r+s-2j}(\phi_{f})
is
0
, so the integral

\int\Xi_{1+a+2b-2j+r+s,a+b+r+s-2j}(\phi_{f})

could be non-zero.

Lemma 7.61
.

The constant
C_{b+s}
is non-zero.

Proof.

First, recall that

C_{b+s}=[H(\mathbb{Z}_{p}):K]^{-1}\langle X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v,b^{a+b+r+s}_{a+r}\rangle.

The weight of
X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v
is
(b-a+2(s-r),0,s-r;-b-2s+r)
, so the weight of it is
\lambda^{\prime}(b+s-(a+r),-(a+b+r+s))
when restricting to
\GL_{2}
. By the fact that the weight of the representation
\Sym^{n}V_{2}^{\vee}
of
\GL_{2}
is multiplicity -free, we have

X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v|_{\GL_{2}}=\lambda^{b+s}(v)a^{a+b+r+s}_{a+r},

where
a^{a+b+r+s}_{a+r}
is
(b^{a+b+r+s}_{a+r})^{\vee}
and
\lambda^{b+s}(v)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
By the definition of the pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, in order to show that
C_{b+s}\neq 0
, it suffices to show that

\rho(X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v)=X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v|_{\GL_{2}}\neq 0.

Second, if we untwist by the character
\mu^{-b-2s+r}
, the weight of
X^{a+r}_{(1,0,-1)}X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v
is
(b+2s-r,0,-a+s-2r)
. After the untwist, the highest weight of

\Sym^{n}V_{2}^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}D

is also
(b+2s-r,0,-a+s-2r)
, which is the same as the weight of
X^{a+r}_{(1,0,-1)}X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v
.

Thirdly, we have the following two formulas: for a representation
V
of the Lie algebra
\lieg_{\mathbb{C}}
, for any vector
v\in V
,
we have

\displaystyle X_{(1,0,-1)}X_{(1,-1,0)}v

\displaystyle=[X_{(1,0,-1)},X_{(1,-1,0)}]v+X_{(1,-1,0)}X_{(1,0,-1)}v

\displaystyle=X_{(1,-1,0)}X_{(1,0,-1)}v.

Finally, by the above formula, we have

X_{(1,0,-1)}(X^{a+r}_{(1,0,-1)}X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v)=X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}(X_{(1,0,-1)}^{a+r+1}v)=0,

where the last equality follows from equation (
7.8
).
Hence,
X^{a+r}_{(1,0,-1)}X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v
is a highest weight vector of

\Sym^{n}V_{2}^{\vee}\hookrightarrow\iota^{*}D.

By the branching law of
\GL_{2}\subset\GL_{3}
, the highest weight vector is unique up to a non-zero constant, so
X^{a+r}_{(1,0,-1)}X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v\neq 0
. Thus

\rho(X^{a+r}_{(1,0,-1)}X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)})v=X^{a+r}_{(1,0,-1)}\rho(X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v)\neq 0.

Hence, we have

\rho(X^{b+s-r}_{(1,-1,0)}v)\neq 0.

∎

Remark 7.62
.

(1)

From the computation of an archimedean zeta integral in the next section, we can see that the only non-zero summand in the summation of Lemma
7.59
is associated to
j=b+s
, so we only need to verify that
C_{b+s}
is non-zero.

(2)

We can replace
v
by
C_{b+s}^{-1}v
so that we replace
\omega_{\Psi}
by
C_{b+s}^{-1}\omega_{\Psi}
. This will not affect the result because our goal is compute the quotient

\frac{\langle\omega_{\Psi},\tilde{\nu}_{K}\rangle_{B}}{\langle\omega_{\Psi},\tilde{\nu}_{D}\rangle_{B}}.

8.
Computation of the integral

Convention 8.1
.

•

In this section, we do not identify
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
automatically.

•

In this section, we choose measures as in Convention
7.56
.

•

Let
n=a+b+r+s
.

8.1.
The zeta integral of Gelbart and Piatetski-Shapiro

In this subsection, we recall the zeta integral constructed in
GPS 84
and modified in
PS 18
.

Convention 8.2
.

•

Let
dt_{\infty}
be the Lebesgue measure on the additive group
\mathbb{R}
. If
v
is a nonarchimedean place of
\mathbb{Q}
, let
dt_{v}
be the Haar measure on
\mathbb{Q}_{v}
such that
\mathbb{Z}_{v}
has volume one.

•

Let
d^{\times}t_{v}
be the measure on
\mathbb{Q}_{v}^{\times}
by

d^{\times}t_{v}=\begin{cases}&\frac{dt_{v}}{|t_{v}|}\quad\text{if}\ v\ \text{is archimedean},\\
&\frac{p}{p-1}\frac{dt_{v}}{|t_{v}|}\quad\text{if}\ v\ \text{is nonarchimedean}.\end{cases}

•

Let
dt
(resp.,
d^{\times}t
) denote the restricted product measure
\prod_{v}dt_{v}
on
\mathbb{A}
(resp., the restricted product measure
\prod_{v}d^{\times}t_{v}
on
\mathbb{A}^{\times}
).

Definition 8.3
.

Let
V_{2}
be the standard representation of
\GL_{2}
on row vectors and let
B_{2}=T_{2}U_{2}
be the standard Borel of
\GL_{2}
. Let
\Phi
be a Schwartz-Bruhat function on
V_{2}(\mathbb{A})
and let
\nu=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\colon T(\mathbb{A})/T(\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a character of the diagonal torus of
\GL_{2}
. We now define

f(g,\Phi,\nu,s)=\nu_{1}(\det(g))|\det(g)|^{s}\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{A})}\Phi((0,t)g)\nu_{1}\nu_{2}^{-1}(t)|t|^{2s}dt

and set the Eisenstein series on
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A})
to be

E(g,\Phi,\nu,s)=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in B(\mathbb{Q})\backslash GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q})}f(\gamma g,\Phi,\nu,s).

We write
f(g_{1},\Phi,\nu,s)
and
E(g_{1},\Phi,\nu,s)
as functions on
H(\mathbb{A})
via the projection
H(\mathbb{A})\twoheadrightarrow\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A})
.

Remark 8.4
.

•

The general theorem
(
Lan 76
, Lemma 4.1)
implies that the Eisenstein series

E(g,\Phi,\nu,s)=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in B_{2}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q})}f(\gamma g,\Phi,\nu,s)

is absolutely convergent for
\mathrm{Re}(s)
big enough and satisfies a functional equation.

•

By the explicit formula for
f(g,\Phi,\nu,s)
, we can see that the central character is
\nu_{1}\nu_{2}
.

Definition 8.5

(Global zeta integral)
.

For an irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
of
G(\mathbb{A})
and a cusp form
\varphi
in the space of
\pi
, we define the global zeta integral as follows:

I(\varphi,\Phi,\nu,s)=\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}\varphi(g)E(g_{1},\Phi,\nu,s)dg.

8.2.
Comparison of the integral and the zeta integral

In this subsection, we express the integral

\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}\Xi_{m,t}(\phi_{f})(g)dg

in Proposition
7.59
in terms of the zeta integral in Definition
8.5
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\colon\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be continuous characters and let
s\in\mathbb{C}
. Let
\chi_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},s}
be the character of
B_{2}(\mathbb{A})
defined by

\chi_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},s}(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
&d\end{pmatrix})=\nu_{1}(a)\nu_{2}(d)\left|\frac{a}{d}\right|^{s}.

Then for any Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi
on
\mathbb{A}^{2}
, the function
f(g,\Phi,\nu,s)
on
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A})
defined by

(8.1)

f(g,\Phi,\nu,s)=\nu_{1}(\det(g))|\det(g)|^{s}\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{A})}\Phi((0,t)g)\nu_{1}\nu_{2}^{-1}(t)|t|^{2s}dt

belongs to
\Ind_{B_{2}(\mathbb{A})}^{\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A})}\chi_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},s}
, where
\Ind
means unnormalized induction.

Proof.

The proposition follows from the following computations:

f(\begin{pmatrix}1&b\\
&1\\
\end{pmatrix}g,\Phi,\nu,s)=f(g,\Phi,\nu,s),

\displaystyle f(\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&\\
&d_{1}\\
\end{pmatrix}g,\Phi,\nu,s)

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}(a_{1}d_{1})|a_{1}d_{1}|^{s}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}^{-1}(d_{1}^{-1})|d_{1}|^{-2s}f(g,\Phi,\nu,s)

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}(a_{1})\nu_{2}(d_{1})\left|\frac{a_{1}}{d_{1}}\right|^{s}f(g,\Phi,\nu,s).

∎

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
\nu_{1}
be the Hecke character
||^{-n}\nu
and
\nu_{2}
be the trivial Hecke character, where
\nu
is a finite-order Hecke character of sign
(-1)^{n}
. Then the following statements hold.

•

The nonarchimedean part
\chi_{f}
of
\chi_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},1+n}
satisfies

\chi_{f}(\begin{pmatrix}a\alpha&b\\
&d\delta\\
\end{pmatrix})=a^{-1}d^{n+1}\nu(\alpha),

for any
a,d\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}^{\times}
and
\alpha,\delta\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{\times}
.

•

The restriction of the archimedean part
\chi_{\infty}
of
\chi_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},1+n}
to the identity component of the diagonal maximal torus of
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
is identified with
\lambda(n+2,-n)
(see notation
7.38
) through the isomorphism
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\cong\GU({J_{2}})^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
.

Proof.

For the nonarchimedean part, we have

\chi_{f}(\begin{pmatrix}a\alpha&b\\
&d\delta\\
\end{pmatrix})=|a|_{f}^{-n}\nu(\alpha)|\frac{a}{d}|_{f}^{1+n}=a^{-1}d^{n+1}\nu(\alpha).

For the archimedean part, for
\begin{pmatrix}a&\\
&a^{-1}v\\
\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
,
we have

\chi_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}a&\\
&a^{-1}v\\
\end{pmatrix})=|a|_{\infty}^{-n}|a^{2}v^{-1}|_{\infty}^{1+n}=a^{n+2}v^{\frac{-n-(n+2)}{2}}.

Hence, the action of
\chi_{\infty}
is
\lambda(n+2,-n)
via the isomorphism
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}\cong\GU({J_{2}})^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})^{+}
.
∎

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
\nu_{1}
be the Hecke character
||^{-n}\nu
and
\nu_{2}
be the trivial Hecke character, where
\nu
is a finite-order Hecke character of sign
(-1)^{n}
.
Let
\Phi
be a factorizable Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}\Phi_{v}
on
V_{2}(\mathbb{A})
such that

(1)

The function
\Phi_{f}=\otimes_{v<\infty}^{\prime}\Phi_{v}
is
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued,

(2)

The function
\Phi_{\infty}(x,y)
is defined by

\Phi_{\infty}(x,y):=(ix-y)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}(ix+y)^{\frac{n+k}{2}}e^{-\pi(x^{2}+y^{2})}.

Then there exists a
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
such that for any
g\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{A})
, we have

(\phi_{k}^{n}\otimes\phi_{f})(g)=(-1)^{\frac{a+b+r+s-k}{2}}2i\pi^{1+a+b+r+s}\Gamma(1+a+b+r+s)^{-1}f(g,\Phi,\nu,1+a+b+r+s),

where the
\phi_{k}^{n}
on the left-hand side the pullback of the function
\phi_{k}^{n}
defined in Definition
7.40
through the isomorphism
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})\cong\GU(J_{2})^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
and
f
is defined in equation (
8.1
).

Proof.

First, we can factor
f(g,\Phi,\nu,s)
into an Euler product of local Tate integrals

f(g,\Phi,\nu,s)=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}f(g_{v},\Phi_{v},\nu_{v},s),

where

f(g_{v},\Phi_{v},\nu_{v},s)=\nu_{1,v}(\det(g_{v}))|\det(g_{v})|^{s}\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})}\Phi((0,t_{v})g_{v})(\nu_{1,v}\nu_{2,v})^{-1}(t_{v})|t_{v}|_{v}^{2s}dt_{v}.

Second, it follows from Proposition
8.6
, Proposition
8.7
and Defintion
4.42
that

f^{\infty}(g,\Phi,\nu,1+n)=\otimes_{v<\infty}^{\prime}f(g_{v},\Phi_{v},\nu_{v},1+n)

belongs to
I_{n}(\nu)\subset\mathcal{B}_{n,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
for
g=(g_{v})_{v<\infty}\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. Hence, there exists a
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
such that

\phi_{f}=f^{\infty}(g,\Phi,\nu,1+n)

for
g\in G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.

Third, it can seen from the fact that
\phi^{n}_{k}
(see Proposition
7.43
) and
\Phi_{\infty}
(see Lemma
8.25
) has
\U(1)(\mathbb{R})
-weight
-k
and
\Ind_{B_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}^{\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{+}}\lambda(2+n,-n)
is
\U(1)(\mathbb{R})
-multiplicity-free that there is a
c\in\mathbb{C}
such that for any
g\in G(\mathbb{R})
,

\phi^{n}_{k}(g)=c\cdot f(g,\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},1+n),

where
\nu_{\infty}=(\nu_{1,\infty}=||^{-1}\mathrm{sgn}^{n},\nu_{2,\infty}=1)
is the archimedean component of
\nu=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
.
Since
\phi_{k}^{n}(1)=2i
(see Proposition
7.40
) and

f(1,\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},1+n)=(-1)^{\frac{a+b+r+s-k}{2}}\pi^{-1-a-b-r-s}\Gamma(1+a+b+s)

(see Lemma
8.26
),
we get

c=(-1)^{\frac{a+b+r+s-k}{2}}2i\pi^{1+a+b+r+s}\Gamma(1+a+b+r+s)^{-1}.

∎

Corollary 8.9
.

There exists a
\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{B}_{n,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
such that the integral

\int_{H(\mathbb{Q})Z(\mathbb{A})\backslash H(\mathbb{A})}\Xi_{m,k}(\phi_{f})(g)dg

defined in notation
7.58
is equal to

(-1)^{\frac{a+b+r+s-k}{2}}2i\pi^{1+a+b+r+s}\Gamma(1+a+b+r+s)^{-1}I(\varphi,\Phi,\nu,1+a+b+r+s),

where
\varphi=X^{m}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi
and the choice of
\Phi
and
\nu
is explained in Proposition
8.8
.

Proof.

This is direct from Proposition
8.8
.
∎

8.3.
The unfolding

In this subsection, we unfold the zeta integral defined in Subsection
8.1
and factor it into the product of the standard automorphic
L
-function and a local integral of Whittaker functions.

Definition 8.10
.

(1)

(Global Whittaker functionals) We define a non-zero global Whittaker functional
\Lambda\colon\pi\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
on an irreducible cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi
by the following integral: for
\varphi\in\pi
,

\Lambda(\varphi):=\int_{U_{B}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash U_{B}(\mathbb{A})}\chi^{-1}(u)\varphi(u)du.

Here,
U_{B}
is the unipotent radical of the upper-triangular Borel subgroup of
G
and define the character
\chi\colon U_{B}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash U_{B}(\mathbb{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
by
\chi(u)=\psi(\Tr_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta^{-1}u_{23}))
, where
\psi
is the standard additive character
\psi\colon\mathbb{Q}\backslash\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and
u_{23}
is the entry of
u
in the position of the second row and the third column.

(2)

(Global Whittaker functions) For a cusp form
\varphi
in the space of
\pi
, denote by

W_{\varphi}(g)=\Lambda(\pi(g)\varphi)=\int_{U_{B}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash U_{B}(\mathbb{A})}\chi^{-1}(u)\varphi(ug)du

the global Whittaker function associated to
\varphi
.

(3)

(Local Whittaker functions)
We assume that
\varphi=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}\varphi_{v}
is a pure tensor in the decomposition
\pi=\otimes_{v}^{\prime}\pi_{v}
and such that
\Lambda(\varphi)=W_{\varphi}(1)\neq 0
. Then for each place
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
, we define a local Whittaker functional
W_{\varphi,v}\colon G(\mathbb{Q}_{v})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by setting
W_{\varphi,v}(g_{v})=\Lambda(\pi(g_{v})\varphi)/\Lambda(\varphi)
for
g_{v}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
and
\varphi\in\pi
. We then have

W_{\varphi}(g)=W_{\varphi}(1)\prod_{v}W_{\varphi,v}(g_{v}),

where the product runs over all the places
{v}
of
\mathbb{Q}
.

Convention 8.11
.

•

Let
\omega_{\pi}\colon Z_{G}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash Z_{G}(\mathbb{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the central character of
\pi
. For later sections, we assume that
\omega_{\pi}|_{Z(\mathbb{A})}=(\nu_{1}\nu_{2})^{-1}
.

•

When
\varphi
is clear, we use
W_{v}
to denote
W_{\varphi,v}
.

Definition 8.12

(Local zeta integral)
.

Let
U_{2}
be the upper-triangular unipotent subgroup
\{\begin{pmatrix}1&*\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\}
of
\GL_{2}
. For a Whittaker function
W_{v}
on
G(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
and a Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi_{v}
on
V_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
,
we define the local zeta integral as

(8.2)

\displaystyle I_{v}(W_{v},\Phi_{v},\nu_{v},s)

\displaystyle:=\int_{Z(\mathbb{Q}_{v})U_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})\backslash H(\mathbb{Q}_{v})}f(g_{1,v},\Phi_{v},\nu_{v},s)W_{v}(g_{v})dg_{v},

(8.3)

\displaystyle=\int_{U_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})\backslash H(\mathbb{Q}_{v})}\nu_{1}(\det(g_{1,v}))\Phi_{v}((0,1)g_{1,v})W_{v}(g_{v})|\det(g_{1,v})|_{v}^{s}dg_{v},

where
g_{1,v}
is projection of
g_{v}
onto
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
and

f(g_{1,v},\Phi_{v},\nu_{v},s)=\nu_{1}(\det(g_{1,v}))|\det(g_{1,v})|^{s}\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})}\Phi((0,t)g_{1,v})(\nu_{1}\nu_{2})^{-1}(t)|t|_{v}^{2s}dt.

We now recall the definition of the standard automorphic
L
-function attached to
\pi
.

Definition 8.13

(Standard automorphic
L
-function)
.

•

The Langlands dual group of
G
is
\widehat{G}=\Gm(\mathbb{C})\times\GL_{3}(\mathbb{C})
, with an action of the non-trivial element
\sigma\in\Gal(E/\mathbb{Q})
given by

\sigma\colon(x,g)\in\Gm(\mathbb{C})\times\GL_{3}(\mathbb{C})\mapsto(x\det g,\Phi_{3}^{-1}{{}^{t}g^{-1}}\Phi_{3})\in\Gm(\mathbb{C})\times\GL_{3}(\mathbb{C}),

where

\Phi_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

The
L
-group of
G
is
{{}^{L}G=\widehat{G}\rtimes\Gal(E/\mathbb{Q})}
.

•

Define
G^{\prime}=\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\Gm\times\GL_{3})
. The Langlands dual group of
G^{\prime}
is
\widehat{G^{\prime}}=\widehat{G}\times\widehat{G}
with an action of the non-trivial element
\sigma\in\Gal(E/\mathbb{Q})
given by

\sigma\colon(g,h)\in\widehat{G^{\prime}}\mapsto(\sigma(h),\sigma(g))\in\widehat{G^{\prime}},

where
\sigma(g)
and
\sigma(h)
is the action of
\sigma
on
\widehat{G}
defined before. Define the
L
-group of
G^{\prime}
as
{{}^{L}G^{\prime}}=\widehat{G^{\prime}}\rtimes\Gal(E/\mathbb{Q})
.

•

It can be seen from the definition that we have the diagonal embedding of Langlands dual groups
\widehat{G}\rightarrow\widehat{G^{\prime}}
, and we can extend the map to the
L
-groups
{{}^{L}G}\rightarrow{{}^{L}G^{\prime}}
. We define the standard representation
\mathrm{std}\colon{{}^{L}G^{\prime}}\rightarrow\GL_{6}(\mathbb{C})
as

\displaystyle((x_{1},g_{1}),(x_{2},g_{2}))\rtimes 1\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}g_{1}&\\
&x_{2}\det(g_{2})\Phi_{3}{{}^{t}g_{2}^{-1}\Phi_{3}^{-1}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle 1\rtimes\sigma\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}&1_{3}\\
1_{3}&\\
\end{pmatrix}.

•

For unramified
\pi_{p}
, using the Satake transform of Cartier
Car 79
or Haines–Rostami
HR 10
, the well-defined local standard
L
-function
L_{p}(s,\pi_{p},\mathrm{std})
is a meromorphic function of
s\in\mathbb{C}
.

•

For a Hecke character
\nu\colon\mathbb{Q}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, we define the twisted standard
L
-function
L_{p}(s,\pi_{p}\times\nu_{p},\mathrm{std})
of
\pi\times\nu
as the standard
L
-function of
(\nu\circ\mu)\pi
, where
(\nu\circ\mu)\pi
is the product of the representation
\pi
by the automorphic character
\nu\circ\mu
of
G
, where
\mu
is the similitude character of
G
.

We now state a result regarding twisted base change that can be used to define
L
-factors at all places.

Proposition 8.14

(
Rog 90
, §13)
.

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal automorphic representation of
G
over
\mathbb{Q}
such that
\pi_{\infty}
is a discrete series. Then there exists a unique automorphic representation
\tau
on
\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\Gm\times\GL_{3})
that is compatible with
\pi
at every place
v
of
\mathbb{Q}
such that

•

v
splits in
E
, or

•

v
is inert in
E
and
\pi_{v}
is unramified, or

•

v
is ramified in
E
and
\pi_{v}
has a vector fixed by
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

Remark 8.15
.

•

The results in
Rog 90
are only for the ordinary unitary group. Results for the similitude setting are also constructed in
Mor 10
and
Shi 14
.

•

Since
\tau
is uniquely determined by
\pi
, one can use
\tau
to make a well-defined
L
-factor at
all
places by using the
L
-group representation in Definition
8.13
and the known local
L
-parameters of representations of
\Res_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\Gm\times\GL_{3})
.

Proposition 8.16
.

GPS 84
;
Bar 97

(
PS 18
, Proposition 3.11.)

With the factorizable data and notation as above, we have

I(\varphi,\Phi,\nu,s)=W_{\varphi}(1)\prod_{v}I_{v}(W_{v},\Phi_{v},\nu_{v},s).

For all finite places
p
of
\mathbb{Q}
, the local integral is absolutely convergent for
\mathrm{Re}(s)\gg 0
and has meromorphic continuation to a rational function of
p^{-s}
.
If the finite place
p
is not
2
,
p
is unramified in
E
,
\pi_{p}
and
\nu_{p}
are unramified,
\varphi
is fixed by the maximal compact subgroup
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
\Phi_{p}
is the characteristic function of
V_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, then

I_{p}(W_{p},\Phi_{p},\nu_{p},s)=L_{p}(s,\pi_{p}\times\nu_{1,p},\mathrm{std}).

If we let
S
be the completement set of the above finite places, then we have

\displaystyle I(\varphi,\Phi,\nu,s)

\displaystyle=W_{\varphi}(1)\prod_{p\in S}I_{p}(W_{p},\Phi_{p},\nu_{p},s)\times I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},s)\times\prod_{p\notin S,p<\infty}L_{p}(s,\pi_{p}\times\nu_{1,p},\mathrm{std})

\displaystyle=W_{\varphi}(1)\prod_{p\in S}\frac{I_{p}(W_{p},\Phi_{p},\nu_{p},s)}{L_{p}(s,\pi_{p}\times\nu_{1,p},\mathrm{std})}\times I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},s)\times L(s,\pi\times\nu_{1},\mathrm{std}).

8.4.
The nonarchimedean local zeta integral

In this subsection, we recall results about algebraicity of the nonarchimedean local zeta-integral
I_{p}(W_{p},\Phi_{p},\nu_{p},s)
and the nonarchimedean local
L
-factor
L_{p}(s,\pi_{p}\times\nu_{1,p},\mathrm{std})
.

Proposition 8.17

(
PS 18
, Proposition 7.1)
.

Suppose that the Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi_{p}
on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{2}
and the Whittaker function
W_{p}
are both
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued. Then the local integral
I_{p}(W_{p},\Phi_{p},\nu_{p},s)
has a meromorphic continuation to a rational function
\frac{P(X)}{Q(X)}
of
X=p^{-s}
, where
P
and
Q
have algebraic coefficients.

In particular, the meromorphic continuation of
I_{p}(W_{p},\Phi_{p},\nu_{p},s)
to any
s\in\mathbb{Q}
is
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued if it is finite.

We now address the hypothesis that
W_{p}
is
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued in the previous proposition. In order to use it in the global setting later, we work in the general setting.

Definition 8.18
.

Assume that
\pi
is a representation of a group
G
on a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
V
. We say that
\pi
is defined over
L\subset\mathbb{C}
if there exists a representation
\pi_{0}
on a
L
-vector space
V_{0}
such that
V_{0}\otimes_{L}\mathbb{C}
is isomorphic to
\pi
. We call
V_{0}
a model of
V
over
L
.

Proposition 8.19

(
GS 18
, Proposition 3.3.2)
.

Suppose that a representation
\pi
of a group
G
is defined over
L
and let
V
and
V_{0}
be as in Definition
8.18
. Assume that
\Lambda\colon V\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a non-zero functional on
V
that has a left transformation property with respect to a subgroup
H
and a character
\psi\colon H\rightarrow L^{\times}
that characterizes it uniquely up an element of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. (For instance,
\Lambda
can be a Whittaker functional.) Then there exists a functional
\Lambda_{0}\colon V\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with
\mathrm{Im}(\Lambda_{0}|_{V_{0}})\subseteq L
.

Notation 8.20
.

Let
\Lambda:\pi_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a fixed Whittaker functional of
\pi_{p}
. If
\pi_{p}
is defined over a number field, we can assume that
\Lambda
is
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued by Proposition
8.19
. For any
\varphi\in\pi_{p}
, we define the Whittaker function
W_{\varphi}
by

W_{\varphi}(g)=\Lambda(g\varphi),

for any
g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.
(Note that this is slightly different from Definition
8.10
, which we use since we will vary our choice of
\varphi
.)

Proposition 8.21
.

Let
\pi_{p}
be a representation of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
defined over a number field
L
. For any
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued Schwartz-Bruhat function
\Phi_{p}
on
V_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
\varphi_{p}
in a model of
\pi_{p}
over
L
such that
\Lambda(\varphi_{p})\neq 0
, there exists a function
\eta
in the Hecke algebra
C_{c}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}),\mathbb{Q})
such that

I_{p}(W_{\pi_{p}(\eta)\varphi_{p}},\Phi_{p},\nu_{p},s)\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times},

for any
s\in\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

The algebraicity follows from Proposition
8.17
and the nonvanishing is from
(
PS 18
, Lemma 7.4)
.
∎

Definition 8.22
.

For any finite place
p
, we could also define the local
L
-factor
L_{p}(s,\pi_{p}\times\nu_{1,p},\mathrm{std})
to be the greatest common divisor of the zeta integrals
I_{p}(W_{p},\Phi_{p},\nu_{p},s)
as
\Phi_{p}
and
W_{p}
vary, whose existence has been proved in
Bar 97
.

The following corollary is a direct consequence of Proposition
8.21
.

Corollary 8.23
.

The local
L
-factor
L_{p}(s,\pi_{p}\times\nu_{1,p},\mathrm{std})
is equal to

L_{p}(s,\pi_{p}\times\nu_{1,p},\mathrm{std})=\frac{1}{Q(p^{-s})},

where
Q(X)
is a polynomial with
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-coefficients and constant
1
.
Hence, for any
s\in\mathbb{Q}
, we have

L_{p}(s,\pi_{p}\times\nu_{1,p},\mathrm{std})\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}.

8.5.
The archimedean local zeta integral

In this subsection, we compute the archimedean zeta integral for some “nice” test vectors.

Convention 8.24
.

•

Let
\nu_{1,\infty},\nu_{2,\infty}\colon\mathbb{R}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be the characters defined by

\displaystyle\nu_{1,\infty}(x)=|x|^{-n}\sgn(x)^{n},

\displaystyle\nu_{2,\infty}(x)=1,

and let
\nu_{\infty}=(\nu_{1,\infty},\nu_{2,\infty})
.

•

For
(x,y)\in V_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we let

(8.4)

\Phi_{\infty}(x,y):=(ix-y)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}(ix+y)^{\frac{n+k}{2}}e^{-\pi(x^{2}+y^{2})}.

•

Let
\pi_{\infty}
be a discrete series representation of
G(\mathbb{R})^{+}
with Blattner parameter

(1+s-r+b,-1-a+s-r,s-r).

•

Let
\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
be the contragredient of
\pi_{\infty}
, which is a discrete series representation with Blattner parameter
\mu=(1+a+r-s,-1-b+r-s,r-s)
.

•

Let
\Psi_{\infty}\in\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
be a vector of weight
(-1-b+r-s,1+a+r-s,r-s;b+2s-r-2)
, which is the lowest weight vector of the minimal
K_{G}
-type
\tau_{(1+a+r-s,-1-b+r+s,r-s)}
of
\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
.

Lemma 8.25
.

The function
\Phi_{\infty}(x,y)
has
\U(1)(\mathbb{R})
-weight
-k
.

Proof.

For any
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix}\in\U(1)(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
we have

(x,y)g=(ax-by,bx+ay).

We can see from this that:

\Phi_{\infty}((x,y)\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
-b&a\end{pmatrix})=(a+ib)^{-k}\Phi_{\infty}(x,y).

Hence, the function
\Phi_{\infty}(x,y)
has
\U(1)(\mathbb{R})
-weight
-k
.
∎

Lemma 8.26
.

For any
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, the section
f(g,\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)
is equal to

\sgn(\det(g))^{n}|\det(g)|^{z-n}(ic-d)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}(ic+d)^{\frac{n+k}{2}}\pi^{-z}(c^{2}+d^{2})^{-z}\Gamma(z)

for
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

It follows from the identity
(0,t)\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=(ct,dt)
that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\infty}(ct,dt)

\displaystyle=(ict-dt)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}(ict+dt)^{\frac{n+k}{2}}e^{-\pi(c^{2}+d^{2})t^{2}}

\displaystyle=(ic-d)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}(ic+d)^{\frac{n+k}{2}}t^{n}e^{-\pi(c^{2}+d^{2})t^{2}},

so we have

\displaystyle f(g,\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)

\displaystyle=\sgn(\det(g))^{n}|\det(g)|^{z-n}(ic-d)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}(ic+d)^{\frac{n+k}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}}e^{-\pi(c^{2}+d^{2})t^{2}}t^{n}|t|^{2z-n}\sgn(t)^{n}\frac{dt}{t}

\displaystyle=2\sgn(\det(g))^{n}|\det(g)|^{z-n}(ic-d)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}(ic+d)^{\frac{n+k}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\pi(c^{2}+d^{2})t^{2}}t^{2z}\frac{dt}{t}.

It follows from a change of variables that

2\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\pi(c^{2}+d^{2})t^{2}}t^{2z}\frac{dt}{t}=\left(\frac{1}{\pi(c^{2}+d^{2})}\right)^{z}\Gamma(z),

where
\Gamma(z)
is the Gamma function.
So we get

f(g,\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)=\sgn(\det(g))^{n}|\det(g)|^{z-n}(ic-d)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}(ic+d)^{\frac{n+k}{2}}\pi^{-z}(c^{2}+d^{2})^{-z}\Gamma(z).

∎

Notation 8.27
.

•

If we use the convention in
(
KO 95
, page 965)
, we could write the Blattner parameter of
\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
as

\mu=(1+a,-1-b).

•

Let
d=2+a+b
.

•

It follows from the Iwasawa decomposition
G(\mathbb{R})=U_{B}(\mathbb{R})T_{B}(\mathbb{R})K_{G}
that the Whittaker function with a fixed
K_{G}
-type is determined by its value on

T_{B}(\mathbb{R})=\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
.

•

Recall that we have uniqueness of Whittaker models for
G(\mathbb{R})
(see
(
KO 95
, §3.2)
). Let
C_{m}^{(1+a,-1-b)}
be the Whittaker function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
in
(
KO 95
, P974)
for
0\leq m\leq d
, which is associated to the vector
\nu_{d-m}
in the
K_{G}
-type
\tau_{\mu}
(see subsubsection
3.2.2
).

Definition 8.28
.

For an integer
l
, we define the classical Whittaker function
W_{0,l}
on
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
by

(8.5)

W_{0,l}(x)=\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}x}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+|l|)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-\frac{1}{2}+|l|}(1+\frac{t}{x})^{-\frac{1}{2}+|l|}e^{-t}dt.

Remark 8.29
.

We have the following identity:

W_{0,l}(x)=\pi^{-\frac{1}{2}}x^{1/2}K_{l}(\frac{x}{2}),

where
K_{l}(\cdot)
is the modified
K
-Bessel function.

Lemma 8.30
.

The function
C_{m}^{(1+a,-1-b)}
is related to a classical Whittaker function by

C_{m}^{(1+a,-1-b)}(t)=(-i)^{2+a+b-m}t^{a+b+\frac{7}{2}}W_{0,m-(1+a)}(8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}}t),t\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}

Proof.

In this section, we use the Hermitian form
J
to define the unitary group, but in
KO 95
the Hermitian form
J^{\prime}
is used. The corresponding transform is

g\in\GU(J^{\prime})(\mathbb{R})\mapsto CgC^{-1}\in\GU(J)(\mathbb{R}),

where
C=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{D^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\sqrt{2}}&0&\frac{D^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\sqrt{2}}\\
0&1&0\\
-\frac{D^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\sqrt{-2}}&0&\frac{D^{\frac{1}{4}}}{\sqrt{-2}}\end{pmatrix}
.

Recall that in the setup of
KO 95
,
E_{2,+}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&-1\\
0&-1&0\end{pmatrix}
and
E_{2,-}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i&0\\
-i&0&i\\
0&-i&0\end{pmatrix}
.
So in our setting, we have

\displaystyle E_{2,+}=C\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&-1\\
0&-1&0\end{pmatrix}C^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\sqrt{2}D^{\frac{1}{4}}&0\\
0&0&-\sqrt{-2}D^{-\frac{1}{4}}\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle E_{2,-}=C\begin{pmatrix}0&-i&0\\
-i&0&i\\
0&-i&0\end{pmatrix}C^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\sqrt{2}D^{\frac{1}{4}}&0\\
0&0&-\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{1}{4}}\\
0&-\sqrt{2}D^{\frac{1}{4}}&0\end{pmatrix}.

It follows from Definition
8.10
that

\displaystyle\chi(E_{2,+})=\psi(\Tr_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta^{-1}(-\sqrt{-2}D^{-\frac{1}{4}})))=-4\pi{i}\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{3}{4}},

\displaystyle\chi(E_{2,+})=\psi(\Tr_{E/\mathbb{Q}}(\delta^{-1}(-\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{1}{4}})))=0.

Plugging these into the formulas in
(
KO 95
, Theorem (4.5), Page 977)
, we have

\displaystyle\eta_{\pm}=\chi(E_{2,+})\pm\sqrt{-1}\chi(E_{2,-})=-4\sqrt{2}\pi{i}D^{-\frac{3}{4}},

\displaystyle b=-\eta_{+}\eta_{-}=32\pi^{2}D^{-\frac{3}{2}},\gamma_{m}=(-i)^{2+a+b-m},

and

C_{m}^{(1+a,-1-b)}(t)=(-i)^{2+a+b-m}t^{a+b+\frac{7}{2}}W_{0,m-(1+a)}(8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}}t).

∎

Lemma 8.31
.

The Mellin transform of the Whittaker function
C_{m}^{(1+a,-1-b)}
at
l\in\mathbb{C}
is

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}C_{m}^{(1+a,-1-b)}(t)t^{l}d^{\times}t

\displaystyle=(-i)^{2+a+b-m}\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{3}{4}}})^{l+a+b+\frac{7}{2}}\pi^{-(l+a+b+4)}\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}(l+m+b+3))

\displaystyle\times\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}(l+b-m+5)+a).

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
8.30
that

\int_{0}^{\infty}C_{m}^{(1+a,-1-b)}(t)t^{l}d^{\times}t=(-i)^{2+a+b-m}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{l+a+b+\frac{7}{2}}W_{0,m-(1+a)}(8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}}t)d^{\times}t.

If we change variables by
t\mapsto\frac{t}{8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}}}
, then this integral is

(-i)^{2+a+b-m}(\frac{1}{8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}}})^{l+a+b+\frac{7}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{l+a+b+\frac{7}{2}}W_{0,m-(1+a)}(t)d^{\times}t.

Using the formula in
(
KO 95
, §5.2, P979)
, we find it is equal to

(-i)^{2+a+b-m}\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{3}{4}}})^{l+a+b+\frac{7}{2}}\pi^{-(l+a+b+4)}\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}(l+m+b+3))\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}(l+b-m+5)+a).

∎

Now we compute the archimedean zeta integral with specific chosen test vectors.

Convention 8.32
.

•

Let
\Psi_{\infty}\in\tilde{\pi}_{\infty}
be a vector of weight
(-1-b+r-s,1+a+r-s,r-s;b+2s-r)
, which is the lowest weight vector of the minimal
K_{G}
-type
\tau_{\mu}
.

•

We write the basis
\nu_{0},\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{d}
in the
K_{G}
-type
\mu
defined in subsubsection
3.2.2
as
\nu_{0}^{\mu},\nu_{1}^{\mu},\ldots,\nu_{d}^{\mu}
.

•

We identify
\Psi_{\infty}
with the vector
\nu_{0}^{\mu}
, so
X^{m}_{(1,-1,0)}\Psi_{\infty}
is
m!\nu_{m}^{\mu}
.

•

By the uniqueness of Whittaker models (see
(
KO 95
, §3.2)
), we choose
W_{\infty}
to be the Whittaker function corresponding to
X_{(1,-1,0)}^{m}\Psi_{\infty}
such that
W_{\infty}(1)=1
.

•

Because
W_{\infty}(1)=1
and
C_{d-m}^{(1+a,-1-b)}(1)=(-i)^{m}W_{0,1+b-m}(8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}})
(see Lemma
8.30
), it can be seen from uniqueness of Whittaker models that if we let

C=(-i)^{m}W_{0,1+b-m}(8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}}),

then we have

(8.6)

W_{\infty}(t)=C^{-1}C_{d-m}^{(1+a,-1-b)}(t),

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
21
21

21

Here we view
W_{\infty}
as a function on
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
.
.

•

Let
K_{\infty}=\U(1)(\mathbb{R})
.

Lemma 8.33
.

If
m=1+a+r-s
, then the weight of
W_{\infty}
is

(a-b+2(r-s),0,r-s).

In this case, the integral

I_{1}=\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{R})}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dt

is equal to

\displaystyle C^{-1}(-i)^{1+a-r-s}(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{3}{4}}})^{(2z-r-s+\frac{3}{2})}\pi^{-(2z-r-s+2)}\Gamma(z-r+1+\frac{1}{2}(b-a))\Gamma(z-s+1-\frac{1}{2}(a+b)).

Proof.

First, we have

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{R})}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{<0}}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dt.

By a change of variables and the fact that
W_{\infty}
has weight
(a-b+2(r-s),0,r-s)
, we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dt=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{<0}}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dt.

Hence, we get

I_{1}=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\times}_{>0}}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dt,

which is a Mellin transform of a Whittaker function.

Second, it follows from equation (
8.6
) that

I_{1}=2C^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{2z-n-2}C_{1+b-r+s}^{(1+a,-1-b)}(t)d^{\times}t.

Finally, using the formula in Lemma
8.31
, we have

\displaystyle I_{1}=C^{-1}(-i)^{1+a-r-s}(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{3}{4}}})^{(2z-r-s+\frac{3}{2})}\pi^{-(2z-r-s+2)}\Gamma(z-r+1+\frac{1}{2}(b-a))\Gamma(z-s+1-\frac{1}{2}(a+b)).

∎

Proposition 8.34
.

(1)

If
m\neq 1+a+r-s
, then the archimedean local zeta integral

I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)=\int_{Z(\mathbb{R})U_{2}(\mathbb{R})\backslash H(\mathbb{R})}f(g_{1,\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)W_{\infty}(g_{\infty})dg_{\infty}

is equal to
0
.

(2)

If
m=1+a+r-s
, then the zeta integral
I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},1+a+b+r+s)
is only non-zero when the
K_{\infty}
-type of
\Phi_{\infty}
is
-(a+r-b-s)
. In this case,
I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},1+a+b+r+s)
is equal to

\displaystyle(-1)^{a}\pi^{-(3a+3b+2r+2s+5)}W_{0,b+s-a-r}(8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}})^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{3}{4}}}\right)^{2a+2b+r+s+\frac{7}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\Gamma(a+b+r+s+1)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}a+\frac{3}{2}b+s+2\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}a+\frac{1}{2}b+r+2\right),

where
W_{0,b+s-a-r}
is the classical Whittaker function on
\mathbb{R}
defined in Definition
8.28
and
\Gamma
is the Gamma function.

Proof.

Recall that
H=\GL_{2}\boxtimes E^{\times}
and
Z(\mathbb{R})=\left\{(\begin{pmatrix}z&0\\
0&z\\
\end{pmatrix},z)|z\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
, so it follows from the Iwasawa decomposition that

Z(\mathbb{R})U_{2}(\mathbb{R})\backslash H(\mathbb{R})/K_{\infty}\cong\GL_{1}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}=\left\{(\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix},c)|t\in\GL_{1}(\mathbb{R}),c\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\right\},

where
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is the unit circle
\{z\in\mathbb{C}|z\bar{z}=1\}
. There is an embedding

\iota\colon\GL_{1}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\hookrightarrow G(\mathbb{R})
that maps
(\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix},c)
to
\left(\begin{matrix}t&0&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{matrix}\right)
.

Since the
K_{\infty}
-weight of
\Phi_{\infty}
is
-k
and the
K_{\infty}
-weight of
W_{\infty}
is
m-1-b
, we have

I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)=0

unless
k=m-1-b
. In this case, the action of
K_{\infty}
on
f(g_{1,\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)W_{\infty}(g_{\infty})
is trivial, so

(8.7)

\displaystyle I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)

(8.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{Z(\mathbb{R})U_{2}(\mathbb{R})\backslash H(\mathbb{R})}f(g_{1,\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)W_{\infty}(g_{\infty})dg_{\infty}

(8.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{-z}\Gamma(z)\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}(-t^{-1})^{\frac{n-k}{2}}(t^{-1})^{\frac{n+k}{2}}t^{2z}W_{\infty}(\iota(\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix},c))|t|^{-2}dtdc

(8.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{-z}\Gamma(z)(-1)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dtdc.

(8.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi^{-z}\Gamma(z)(-1)^{\frac{n-k}{2}}\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{R})}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dt,\times\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}c^{1+a-m+r-s}dc

where the last identity holds since

W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})=W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&c&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix})=c^{1+a-m+r-s}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}).

Now we set
I_{1}=\int_{\GL_{1}(\mathbb{R})}t^{2z-n-2}W_{\infty}(\begin{pmatrix}t&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix})dt
and
I_{2}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}c^{1+a-m+r-s}dc
,
and will compute the integral
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
, respectively.

The integral
I_{2}
is computed as follows.
We have

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}c^{1+a-m+r-s}dc

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}e^{2\pi{i}(1+a-m+r-s)x}dx

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{If}\ m=1+a+r-s,\\
0&\text{If}\ m\neq 1+a+r-s.\end{cases}

When
m=1+a+r-s
, the integral
I_{1}
is computed in Lemma
8.33
.

Finally, plugging the formulas for
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
into equation (
8.11
), we have:

•

if
m\neq 1+a+r-s
, then

I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)=0;

•

if
m=1+a+r-s
, then

\displaystyle I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{a+r}C^{-1}(-i)^{1+a-r-s}(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{3}{4}}})^{(2z-r-s+\frac{3}{2})}\pi^{-(3z-r-s+2)}

\displaystyle\times\Gamma(z-r+1+\frac{1}{2}(b-a))\Gamma(z-s+1-\frac{1}{2}(a+b))\Gamma(z).

Plugging
z=1+a+b+r+s
and
C=(-i)^{1+a+r-s}W_{0,b+s-a-r}(8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}})
into the formula, we have

\displaystyle I_{\infty}(W_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty},\nu_{\infty},1+a+b+r+s)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{a}

\displaystyle\pi^{-(3a+3b+2r+2s+5)}W_{0,b+s-a-r}(8\sqrt{2}\pi D^{-\frac{3}{4}})^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}D^{-\frac{3}{4}}}\right)^{2a+2b+r+s+\frac{7}{2}}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Gamma(a+b+r+s+1)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}a+\frac{3}{2}b+s+2)\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}a+\frac{1}{2}b+r+2).

∎

Remark 8.35
.

(1)

If we let
m=1+a+r-s=1+a+2b-2j+r+s
as in Proposition
7.59
, then
j=b+s
. So we can see from Corollary
8.9
only one term in the summation of equation (
7.20
) is left. So the pairing
\langle\omega_{\Psi},[\rho]\rangle_{B}
is Eulerian by our previous discussion of zeta integral.

(2)

The integer
j=b+s
satisfies the condition
A\leq j\leq B
in Proposition
7.59
.

9.
The periods

9.1.
The motivic periods

In this subsection, we compute the pairing
\langle\Omega,\tilde{v}_{D}\rangle_{B}
defined in Lemma
7.11
.

Recall that
V=V^{a,b}\{r,s\}
and we have the following exact sequence:

(9.1)

0\rightarrow F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1)\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow 0.

The Beilinson
E(\pi_{f})
-structure of
\mathrm{det}_{E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}}\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})
is defined as

\mathcal{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))=\mathrm{det}_{E(\pi_{f})}F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{*}\otimes_{E(\pi_{f})}\mathrm{det}_{E(\pi_{f})}M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1).

Let
\delta(\pi_{f},V(2))\in(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})^{\times}
be the determinant of the isomorphism

I_{\infty}\colon M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}

computed using the basis defined over
E(\pi_{f})
on both sides. The Deligne
E(\pi_{f})
-structure of
\mathrm{det}_{E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}}\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{MHS_{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}}(\mathbb{R}(0),M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}})
is

\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2)):=(2\pi i)^{3}\delta(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-1}\mathcal{B}(\pi_{f},V(2)).

Let
v_{D}
be a generator of
\mathcal{D}(\pi_{f},V(2))
,
\tilde{v}_{D}
be a lifting of
v_{D}
via the last arrow in the exact sequence (
9.1
)
and
\Omega=\frac{1}{2}(\omega_{\Psi}+\overline{\omega_{\Psi}})\cdot\mathbf{1}
, where
\omega_{\Psi}
is constructed in Subsection
7.2
and
\mathbf{1}
the multiplicative identities of
E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
.

It can be seen from Remark
3.9
and Proposition
3.41
that we have the following Hodge decompositions:

\displaystyle M_{B}({\pi}_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle M_{B}^{-r,-2-a-b-s}\oplus M_{B}^{-1-a-r,-1-b-s}\oplus M_{B}^{-2-a-b-r,-s}

\displaystyle\oplus

\displaystyle M_{B}^{-2-a-b-s,-r}\oplus M_{B}^{-1-b-s,-1-a-r}\oplus M_{B}^{-s,-2-a-b-r},

and

\displaystyle M_{B}(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)_{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle M_{B}^{a+b+r+2,s}\oplus M_{B}^{a+r+1,b+s+1}\oplus M_{B}^{r,a+b+s+2}

\displaystyle\oplus

\displaystyle M_{B}^{s,a+b+r+2}\oplus M_{B}^{b+s+1,a+r+1}\oplus M_{B}^{a+b+s+2,r},

from which we can see that

M_{B}(\pi_{f},V(2))^{-}_{\mathbb{R}}(-1)
is
3
-dimensional.
By the condition of the parameters stated in (
7.3
), we get that
F^{0}M_{dR}(\pi_{f},V(2))_{\mathbb{R}}
is
2
-dimensional.

Notation 9.1
.

We use
M_{dR}^{s,t}
to denote
I_{\infty}^{-1}(M_{B}^{s,t})
for
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We now recall the definition of Deligne periods as in
(
Del 79
, §1.7)
.

Definition 9.2

(Deligne period)
.

Assume that
a+r\neq b+s
22
22

22

This assumption implies that condition that the infinite Frobenius
F_{\infty}
acts on the component with Hodge type
(n,n)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by a scalar
1
or
-1
(see
(
Del 79
, §1.7)
) is satisfied.
and let

\displaystyle F^{+}(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F^{-}(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M_{dR}^{a+b+r+2,s}\oplus M_{dR}^{a+b+s+2,r}\oplus M_{dR}^{A,B},

where
A=\max\{a+r+1,b+s+1\}
,
B=\min\{a+r+1,b+s+1\}
.
It is shown in
(
Del 79
, §1.7)
that the map

I_{\infty}^{\pm}\colon M_{B}(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)_{\mathbb{C}}^{\pm}\longrightarrow M_{dR}(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)_{\mathbb{C}}/F^{\pm}(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)

is an isomorphism.

The Deligne period
c^{\pm}(\tilde{\pi}_{f}|\mu|^{-2},D)
is defined as
\det(I_{\infty}^{\pm})\in(E(\pi_{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})^{\times}
.

Remark 9.3
.

We can define the Deligne period
c^{\pm}(\pi_{f},V(2))
similarly. This is left to the reader.
